--- layout: default title: Treatise on the Stages of Yogic Practice, Fascicle 98 permalink: /sutras/stages-of-yogic-practice/fascicle-098/ excerpt: Ru-Yi's bilingual translation of fascicle 98 of the Yogācārabhūmi-śāstra — completing the foundations of mindfulness' fourth verse, closing right effort and the bases of supernormal power, the faculties, the powers, the factors of enlightenment, and the eightfold path, then mindfulness of breathing, the trainings, and confirmed purity, and bringing the sūtra matters of the Compendium of Bases to a close. ---
Mahāyāna Scriptures › Treatise on the Stages of Yogic Practice › Fascicle 98
Fascicle 98 · 瑜伽師地論卷第九十八 · Complete

Five Great Topics Close; the Eightfold Path Begins

Fascicle 98 continues directly from fascicle 97, which closed the first three particular verses of the foundations of mindfulness (念住攝) in their entirety. This fascicle opens and closes the fourth and final particular verse, completing the foundations of mindfulness — the first of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics — in its entirety. It then closes, one after another, four more great topics entire: right effort combined with the bases of supernormal power, the faculties, the powers, and the seven factors of enlightenment, this last closing with the four immeasurables' own upper-limit attainments and a wide sweep of companion-sūtra background. Fascicle 98 then opens the sixth great topic, the eightfold path, through its four sections: the inward and outward powers that alone give rise to the noble path — a good spiritual friend, outwardly, and right reflection, inwardly — then purity's own distinctions, the alternate approach, and the true renunciant and brāhmaṇa. The fascicle closes with the remaining three great topics, mindfulness of breathing, the trainings, and the four confirmed purities, and so brings sūtra matters to their end.

Translator: Ru-Yi Meditation Center Status: complete Source: Taishō T30, no. 1579, fascicle 98 Commentary guide: Master Cháng Bǎi's lecture notes

1. Section: The Fascicle's Own Title — Continuing the Embrace of the Factors of Enlightenment

Within the compendium of bases, sūtra matters, the embrace of the factors of enlightenment, the fourth, part two.

Chinese Source

攝事分中契經事菩提分法擇攝第四之二

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the compendium of bases, sūtra matters, the embrace of the factors of enlightenment, the fourth, part two.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This is the fascicle's own chapter title, marking its place as the second part ("第四之二") of the fourth and final great division of sūtra matters (契經事) within the compendium of bases (攝事分, spanning fascicles 85 through 100). Master Cháng Bǎi recaps the whole division's structure: the aggregates' embrace (行擇攝), the sense-bases' embrace (處擇攝), the dependent-origination-food-truths-elements embrace (緣起食諦界擇攝), and the factors of enlightenment's embrace (菩提分法擇攝) — this last spanning fascicles 97 and 98, with fascicle 97 having already closed the general summary verse and the foundations of mindfulness's first three particular verses in their entirety, and this fascicle now continuing with the foundations of mindfulness's fourth and final particular verse.

2. Section: The Fourth Particular Summary Verse Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing

Further, a summary verse says: the false teacher's dwelling, the snow mountain; exhortation, and being bound to purity; the gradual sequence, the precepts' fullness; the ear of grain, and accomplishment, coming last.

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

邪師住雪山　勸勉繫屬淨　漸次戒圓滿　穗成就為後

Ru-Yi Translation

The false teacher's dwelling, the snow mountain; exhortation, and being bound to purity; the gradual sequence, the precepts' fullness; the ear of grain, and accomplishment, coming last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens **the fourth and final particular summary verse of the foundations of mindfulness's embrace (癸四第四嗢柁南2)**, beginning with the verse marking and listing (子一頌標列) — eight matters to unpack in the long-form explanation that follows: the false teacher's dwelling, the snow mountain, exhortation, being bound to purity, the gradual sequence, the precepts' fullness, the ear of grain, and accomplishment.

3. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — The False Teacher's Dwelling Begins — Manifesting Exclusive Self-Interest Begins — Marking

There are various outside paths who, among their assembly of disciples, establish themselves as teacher, yet exclusively seek gain, exclusively seek veneration, and exclusively seek their own benefit.

Chinese Source

有諸外道，於弟子眾自立為師，專求利養、專求恭敬、專求自利。

Ru-Yi Translation

There are various outside paths who, among their assembly of disciples, establish themselves as teacher, yet exclusively seek gain, exclusively seek veneration, and exclusively seek their own benefit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the long-form explanation (子二長行釋8), an eightfold structure matching the verse's eight matters, beginning with the false teacher's dwelling (丑一邪師住2), named by the verse's first phrase "the false teacher's dwelling, the snow mountain" (邪師住雪山), beginning with manifesting exclusive self-interest (寅一明專自利2), beginning with marking (卯一標) — an outside-path teacher who sets himself up as a guide to disciples while exclusively chasing gain, veneration, and self-benefit is, at root, a false teacher regardless of outward appearance.

4. Section: Explaining Begins — Raising the Two Speeches Begins — The Outside-Path Teacher

Meeting a conjunction of conditions, there is a son of good family who goes forth under him. He then says to him: "You and I have not previously had any resources or requisites to share and use in common. You should, for my sake, go to another place, praise my virtues, and conceal my faults; I, too, will do the same for you. The two of us, taking turns relying on and protecting one another, will thereby obtain much gain and veneration from kings, from those equal to kings, and even from every great merchant lord." If a teacher speaks in this way, the various outside teachers are called exclusively seeking their own benefit.

Chinese Source

遇緣和合，有族姓子投其出家。因而謂曰：汝之與我先無一切資身眾具可共受用，汝應為我往詣他處，褒讚我德，掩藏我失，我亦為汝行如是事。我等二人迭相依護，當於諸王若與王等乃至一切大商主邊，多獲利養及以恭敬。若作是言，諸外道師名專自利。

Ru-Yi Translation

Meeting a conjunction of conditions, there is a son of good family who goes forth under him. He then says to him: "You and I have not previously had any resources or requisites to share and use in common. You should, for my sake, go to another place, praise my virtues, and conceal my faults; I, too, will do the same for you. The two of us, taking turns relying on and protecting one another, will thereby obtain much gain and veneration from kings, from those equal to kings, and even from every great merchant lord." If a teacher speaks in this way, the various outside teachers are called exclusively seeking their own benefit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining (卯二釋2), the second of manifesting exclusive self-interest's two matters, beginning with raising the two speeches (辰一舉二言2), beginning with the outside-path teacher (巳一外道師) — a newly-ordained disciple is instructed to trade mutual flattery for worldly gain: each praising the other's virtues and concealing the other's faults before patrons, so that both profit from the arrangement.

5. Section: The Disciple, Closing "Raising the Two Speeches" Entirely

Yet his disciple then speaks in protest: "Do not hold this view! Such protection as this is not called self-protection from the fault of going to an unfortunate destiny; only guarding against this fault is called self-protection. Therefore you should protect yourself as before, and I too shall separately protect myself in another way. Since I am unable to protect you, you likewise need not protect me."

Chinese Source

然其弟子便發抗言：勿為此見！如是護者，未名自護往惡趣失；若防此失，乃名自護。是故汝應如前自護，我亦當自別為餘護。我既不能護汝，汝亦不須護我。

Ru-Yi Translation

Yet his disciple then speaks in protest: "Do not hold this view! Such protection as this is not called self-protection from the fault of going to an unfortunate destiny; only guarding against this fault is called self-protection. Therefore you should protect yourself as before, and I too shall separately protect myself in another way. Since I am unable to protect you, you likewise need not protect me."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

With this, the disciple (巳二彼弟子) closes entirely, closing raising the two speeches (辰一舉二言2: 外道師/彼弟子) entirely — the disciple sees through the arrangement: mutual flattery protects neither party from an unfortunate rebirth; only guarding one's own conduct against that fault deserves the name "self-protection," so each should attend to their own practice rather than trading favors. The 披尋記 confirms "self-protection as before" refers back to guarding against the very fault of an unfortunate destiny.

6. Section: Distinguishing Merit and Fault, Closing "Manifesting Exclusive Self-Interest" Entirely

Within this meaning, one should know that the disciple is one who speaks in accordance with principle, one who is wise, since he gives weight to what is to come; one should know that his teacher is one who speaks contrary to principle, one who is foolish, since he gives weight only to the present.

Chinese Source

於此義中，當知弟子是如理語者，是聰慧者，重當來故。應知其師是非理語者，是愚癡者，重現在故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within this meaning, one should know that the disciple is one who speaks in accordance with principle, one who is wise, since he gives weight to what is to come; one should know that his teacher is one who speaks contrary to principle, one who is foolish, since he gives weight only to the present.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, distinguishing merit and fault (辰二辨德失) closes entirely, closing **manifesting exclusive self-interest (寅一明專自利2: 標/釋), the first of the false teacher's dwelling's two matters, entirely** — weighing future consequence over present convenience marks the wise; weighing present convenience over future consequence, however dressed up as mutual aid, marks the fool — and, in this case, the false teacher.

7. Section: Manifesting the Fault of Failing to Protect Begins — Marking the Fault

Further, there is defilement that touches and disturbs another; because of this defilement, one is unable to protect oneself; and because of thereby disturbing another, this is not called protecting another.

Chinese Source

復有雜染觸惱於他，由雜染故，不能自護；因此惱他，不名護他。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, there is defilement that touches and disturbs another; because of this defilement, one is unable to protect oneself; and because of thereby disturbing another, this is not called protecting another.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens manifesting the fault of failing to protect (寅二明不護失2), the second of the false teacher's dwelling's two matters, beginning with marking the fault (卯一標失) — a mind still defiled by affliction, when it disturbs another, has failed to protect itself, and disturbing another can never be mistaken for genuinely protecting them.

8. Section: Explaining the Meaning Begins — One Should Protect Oneself

Here, as said before, severing every affliction through drawing near a good teacher and the rest is called protecting oneself.

Chinese Source

此中如前由親近等斷諸煩惱，名當自護。

Ru-Yi Translation

Here, as said before, severing every affliction through drawing near a good teacher and the rest is called protecting oneself.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens explaining the meaning (卯二釋義2), the second of manifesting the fault of failing to protect's two matters, beginning with one should protect oneself (辰一當自護) — genuine self-protection, as established throughout this treatise, comes through drawing near a good spiritual friend, hearing the true dharma, giving principled attention, and practicing in accordance with the dharma, severing affliction at its root; the 披尋記 confirms this same fourfold sequence as the very cause of guarding against an unfortunate destiny's fault.

9. Section: One Should Protect Others Begins — Marking the Name

From this point on, taking this severance as cause, not disturbing others and the rest is called protecting others.

Chinese Source

從此已後，由斷為因，不惱他等，名當護他。

Ru-Yi Translation

From this point on, taking this severance as cause, not disturbing others and the rest is called protecting others.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens one should protect others (辰二當護他2), the second of explaining the meaning's two matters, beginning with marking the name (巳一標名) — protecting oneself through severing affliction naturally flows outward into protecting others as well, since a mind no longer disturbed by its own defilement no longer disturbs those around it.

10. Section: Explaining the Meaning Begins — Through Being Without Aversion and Without Harmfulness

One should know that, here, being without aversion and without harmfulness is the meaning of being without disturbance.

Chinese Source

應知此中，無瞋無害，是無惱義。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, being without aversion and without harmfulness is the meaning of being without disturbance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining the meaning (巳二釋義2), the second of one should protect others' two matters, beginning with through being without aversion and without harmfulness (午一由無瞋無害) — a mind free of aversion and free of the wish to harm is, by that very freedom, incapable of disturbing another.

11. Section: Through Sympathetic Concern for All Begins — Directly Manifesting This Meaning

Giving rise, without any personal condition, to the two minds of benefit and ease; giving rise, without any personal condition, to the two minds of loving-kindness and compassion — one should know that this is the meaning of sympathetic concern. Because of this sympathetic concern, one does not disturb others.

Chinese Source

無緣而起利樂二心，無緣而起慈悲二心，當知如此是哀愍義。由哀愍故，不惱於他。

Ru-Yi Translation

Giving rise, without any personal condition, to the two minds of benefit and ease; giving rise, without any personal condition, to the two minds of loving-kindness and compassion — one should know that this is the meaning of sympathetic concern. Because of this sympathetic concern, one does not disturb others.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens through sympathetic concern for all (午二由一切哀愍2), the second of explaining the meaning's two matters, beginning with directly manifesting this meaning (未一正顯此義) — toward hostile, neutral, and dear beings alike, without regard to any personal cause, one gives rise to the wish for their benefit and ease, and to loving-kindness and compassion — the very meaning of sympathetic concern, which by its nature cannot disturb another. Master Cháng Bǎi distinguishes three levels of loving-kindness: sentient-being-conditioned kindness (an ordinary being's initial practice, regarding every being as a former parent across beginningless rebirths); dharma-conditioned kindness (a śrāvaka's realization that beings are merely the five aggregates, with no real striker or struck, beater or beaten); and unconditioned great kindness (a bodhisattva's embrace of every being as not separate from one's own mind — the same-essence great compassion).

12. Section: Setting Out Its Reversal, Closing "Through Sympathetic Concern for All," "Explaining the Meaning," "One Should Protect Others," "Explaining the Meaning," "Manifesting the Fault of Failing to Protect," and "The False Teacher's Dwelling" Entirely

Therefore one should know that sympathetic concern for all beings is the very reverse of that defiled disturbance.

Chinese Source

是故當知，一切哀愍與彼相違。

Ru-Yi Translation

Therefore one should know that sympathetic concern for all beings is the very reverse of that defiled disturbance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a companion sūtra of the acrobat and his apprentice

With this, setting out its reversal (未二出彼相違) closes entirely, closing through sympathetic concern for all (午二由一切哀愍2: 正顯此義/出彼相違) entirely, closing explaining the meaning (巳二釋義2: 由無瞋無害/由一切哀愍) entirely, closing **one should protect others (辰二當護他2: 標名/釋義) entirely**, closing explaining the meaning (卯二釋義2: 當自護/當護他) entirely, closing **manifesting the fault of failing to protect (寅二明不護失2: 標失/釋義) entirely**, closing **the false teacher's dwelling (丑一邪師住2: 明專自利/明不護失), the first of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely** — sympathetic concern for every being stands in direct contrast to the defiled disturbance that opened this section. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this teaching to a companion sūtra: an acrobat tells his apprentice that each should watch out for the other while performing atop a pole, so both profit from their shared act; the apprentice objects that each should instead watch out for himself alone, so each descends safely — and the Buddha confirms the apprentice's wisdom, teaching that protecting oneself from affliction is itself the truest way of protecting others, since one free of affliction cannot disturb anyone, and calling every monk to cultivate the four foundations of mindfulness as both self-protection and protection of others at once.

13. Section: The Snow Mountain Begins — Comparing to Skillful Liberation Begins — Marking and Listing the Three Grounds

Further, one should know that the snow mountain is a metaphor for the Buddha's well-taught dharma and discipline; here, briefly, three parts can be found: first, the ground of the one beyond training; second, the ground of the trainee; third, the ground of the ordinary being.

Chinese Source

復次，應知雪山喻佛善說法毗奈耶，此中略有三分可得。一、無學地，二、有學地，三、異生地。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, one should know that the snow mountain is a metaphor for the Buddha's well-taught dharma and discipline; here, briefly, three parts can be found: first, the ground of the one beyond training; second, the ground of the trainee; third, the ground of the ordinary being.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the snow mountain (丑二雪山2), the second of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's first phrase's second half "the snow mountain" (邪師住雪山), beginning with comparing to skillful liberation (寅一喻善解脫2), beginning with marking and listing the three grounds (卯一標列三地) — the Buddha's own well-taught dharma is likened to a great snow mountain, its three elevations corresponding to the arhat's ground beyond training (the summit, where neither monkey nor hunter can reach), the trainee's ground (the middle slopes, where the monkey but not the hunter can still be found), and the ordinary being's ground (the foothills, where both freely roam).

14. Section: Correlating and Explaining Their Marks Begins — Within the Ground of the One Beyond Training

The monkey is a metaphor for the various minds conjoined with unprincipled attention; the hunter is a metaphor for Māra. Within the ground of the one beyond training, neither of these is able to operate.

Chinese Source

猿猴喻彼非理作意諸相應心，獵人喻魔，於無學地俱不能行。

Ru-Yi Translation

The monkey is a metaphor for the various minds conjoined with unprincipled attention; the hunter is a metaphor for Māra. Within the ground of the one beyond training, neither of these is able to operate.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens correlating and explaining their marks (卯二配釋其相3), the second of comparing to skillful liberation's two matters, a threefold structure, beginning with within the ground of the one beyond training (辰一於無學地) — the monkey's restless leaping stands for unprincipled attention's every conjoined mind; the hunter stalking it stands for Māra, disturbing body and mind, obstructing wholesome dharmas, and ruining flourishing states. The arhat, having attained the wisdom of exhaustion and the wisdom of no-further-arising, is reached by neither.

15. Section: Within the Ground of the Trainee

Within the ground of the trainee, up through the non-returner, only the monkey-like mind conjoined with unprincipled attention is alone able to go there; it is not a place where the hunter-like Māra is able to operate.

Chinese Source

於有學地乃至不還，唯有非理作意相應猿猴喻心獨一能往，非獵人喻魔所能行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the ground of the trainee, up through the non-returner, only the monkey-like mind conjoined with unprincipled attention is alone able to go there; it is not a place where the hunter-like Māra is able to operate.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens within the ground of the trainee (辰二於有學地), the second — the trainee, from stream-entry through non-return, has already directly realized the noble truths and attained non-discriminating wisdom, severing every discursively-arisen affliction, but the innate afflictions still remain, like a lone monkey able to roam the mountain's deep middle slopes where no hunter ever ventures.

16. Section: Within the Ground of the Ordinary Being

Within the ground of the ordinary being, both of these are able to operate.

Chinese Source

於異生地二俱能行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the ground of the ordinary being, both of these are able to operate.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens within the ground of the ordinary being (辰三於異生地) — an ordinary being, unable to give principled attention when facing objects and still subject to Māra's manifest activity, dwells at the mountain's very foothills, where both monkey and hunter freely appear, mind not yet purified. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows — comparing to skillful liberation's own further matter (寅二, presumably setting out the absence of liberation) — before further closure is claimed.

17. Section: Setting Out the Absence of Liberation, Closing "The Snow Mountain" Entirely

Further, the various foolish ones must observe a further object in order to leave a given object; pursuing one further object after another, they remain bound by that further object — therefore, toward these objects, they do not attain liberation.

Chinese Source

又諸愚夫，要觀餘境能出餘境，追求餘境餘境所縛，是故於境不得解脫。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the various foolish ones must observe a further object in order to leave a given object; pursuing one further object after another, they remain bound by that further object — therefore, toward these objects, they do not attain liberation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a companion sūtra on the monkey caught by pitch

With this, setting out the absence of liberation (寅二出不解脫) closes entirely, closing **the snow mountain (丑二雪山2: 喻善解脫/出不解脫), the second of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely** — an ordinary being pursuing the mundane path of meditation must always observe a still-higher, purer object (the first dhyāna's own faults driving one toward the second, and so on) merely to leave the ground below, yet remains bound within the three realms regardless of how high the ascent goes, since the underlying view of a real body is never actually severed. Master Cháng Bǎi elaborates the mountain's three elevations once more (the arhat's summit reached by neither monkey nor hunter; the trainee's middle slopes reached only by the monkey; the ordinary being's foothills reached by both), then turns to the companion sūtra's own vivid image: a foolish monkey, its hand caught in sticky pitch, tries to free itself by stamping with its feet, only to become caught by all four limbs at once — just as an ordinary being, failing to give principled attention, guards neither the sense-doors within nor the body without, becomes bound by the five desires, follows wherever Māra leads, and abandons the very contemplation of the four foundations of mindfulness that alone could free it. The Buddha calls every being to progress, through the four foundations rightly practiced, from the trainee's own attainment onward to the arhat's deep, mountain-summit liberation.

18. Section: Exhortation Begins — Setting Out the Four Meanings

Further, through hearing the true dharma, upholding it, observing its meaning, and practicing in accordance with the dharma, in their proper sequence, one should know that exhortation and transformation establish four meanings.

Chinese Source

復次，由於正法聽聞、受持、觀察義理、法隨法行，如其次第，應知勸化安立四義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, through hearing the true dharma, upholding it, observing its meaning, and practicing in accordance with the dharma, in their proper sequence, one should know that exhortation and transformation establish four meanings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Foundation Section, fascicle 43

This opens exhortation (丑三勸勉2), the third of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's second phrase "exhortation, and being bound to purity" (勸勉繫屬淨), beginning with setting out the four meanings (寅一出四義) — four terms correlate exactly to the fourfold sequence of hearing, upholding, observing meaning, and practicing accordingly: exhortation (urging a being to hear the true dharma), transformation (taming affliction as the dharma is upheld in mind), settling (placing the mind within the object of observed meaning), and establishing (founding the pure cause and effect of practicing in accordance with the dharma, accomplishing nirvāṇa's own merit) — the same fourfold structure the Foundation Section, fascicle 43, identifies as a bodhisattva's own natural conduct of benefiting others.

19. Section: Elaborating the Three Powers Begins — Marking and Listing

There are, further, three dharmas which are able to sever every supreme, sublime bond of sexual craving, let alone the various baser bonds of sense-desire craving. What are the three? First, the power of diligence; second, the power of freedom from heedlessness; third, the power of the remedy.

Chinese Source

復有三法，尚能斷除一切勝妙婬欲貪纏，況乎鄙劣諸欲貪纏。何等為三？一、精進力，二、不放逸力，三、對治力。

Ru-Yi Translation

There are, further, three dharmas which are able to sever every supreme, sublime bond of sexual craving, let alone the various baser bonds of sense-desire craving. What are the three? First, the power of diligence; second, the power of freedom from heedlessness; third, the power of the remedy.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens elaborating the three powers (寅二廣三力3), the second of exhortation's two matters, beginning with marking and listing (卯一標列) — three powers, if genuinely accomplished, are strong enough to sever even the most refined bond of sexual craving, let alone lesser sense-desires: the power of diligence, the power of freedom from heedlessness, and the power of the remedy.

20. Section: Correlating and Explaining

Through the power of diligence, what has already arisen is made not to abide firmly. Through the remaining two powers, what has not yet arisen is made not to arise.

Chinese Source

由精進力，其已生者令不堅住。由餘二力，其未生者令不得生。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through the power of diligence, what has already arisen is made not to abide firmly. Through the remaining two powers, what has not yet arisen is made not to arise.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens correlating and explaining (卯二配釋), the second of elaborating the three powers' three matters — diligence uproots evil already present before it can take firm hold, while freedom from heedlessness and the remedy together forestall evil not yet arisen and sustain wholesome dharmas already present or newly arising — the same fourfold task the four right efforts name.

21. Section: The Concluding Statement Manifesting the Meaning, Closing "Exhortation" Entirely

Thus the practitioner diligently cultivates right conduct, in order to sever what evil has already arisen, and in order to make what has not yet arisen not arise.

Chinese Source

如是行者勤修正行，為欲斷除已生惡故，及未生者令不生故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Thus the practitioner diligently cultivates right conduct, in order to sever what evil has already arisen, and in order to make what has not yet arisen not arise.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing three companion sūtras (including one on the young monk Udayī, and one of the Buddha's teaching prompted by the sight of the courtesan Āmrapālī)

With this, the concluding statement manifesting the meaning (卯三結顯) closes entirely, closing elaborating the three powers (寅二廣三力3: 標列/配釋/結顯) entirely, closing **exhortation (丑三勸勉2: 出四義/廣三力), the third of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely** — diligent cultivation of the four foundations, sustained by these three powers, severs both present and future evil. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this teaching to three companion sūtras: one in which the Buddha instructs Ānanda that young monks, trainees, and even arhats alike should all be guided toward the four foundations of mindfulness; one occasioned by the Buddha's own sighting of the courtesan Āmrapālī from a distance, in which he warns the assembly, through the simile of a man forced to carry a bowl brimful of oil past a beautiful dancer under threat of instant beheading should a single drop spill, that a monk must guard the mind just as single-mindedly, never letting the five sense-objects distract from the body's own contemplation; and a closing verse urging the assembly to hold the four foundations as one holds that same oil bowl, since only sustained, undistracted attention — never a heedless one — can reach the sublime, subtle state beyond craving, aversion, and delusion.

22. Section: Being Bound to Purity Begins — Pointing to the Mark of Cultivation

Further, cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness with earnest gravity should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

復次，於四念住殷重修習，如聲聞地應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness with earnest gravity should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens being bound to purity (丑四繫屬2), the fourth of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's second phrase's second half "being bound to purity" (勸勉繫屬淨), beginning with pointing to the mark of cultivation (寅一指修相) — cultivating the four foundations with earnest gravity is, once again, pointed to the Śrāvaka Ground's own fuller treatment.

23. Section: Manifesting Being Bound Begins — Distinguishing by the Three Realms Begins — Being Bound to Māra

Being bound to Māra means being within the desire realm; this, the non-returner's fruit is able to transcend.

Chinese Source

繫屬魔者，謂在欲界，此不還果即能超度。

Ru-Yi Translation

Being bound to Māra means being within the desire realm; this, the non-returner's fruit is able to transcend.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens manifesting being bound (寅二明繫屬2), the second of being bound to purity's two matters, beginning with distinguishing by the three realms (卯一約三界辨2), beginning with being bound to Māra (辰一繫屬魔) — craving, Māra's own domain, binds beings within the desire realm; the non-returner (the third fruit) has already transcended this particular bondage.

24. Section: Being Bound to Death, Closing "Distinguishing by the Three Realms" Entirely

Being bound to death means from the desire realm up through the peak of existence; this, only the arhat is able to transcend.

Chinese Source

繫屬死者，謂從欲界乃至有頂，此阿羅漢乃能超度。

Ru-Yi Translation

Being bound to death means from the desire realm up through the peak of existence; this, only the arhat is able to transcend.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, being bound to death (辰二繫屬死) closes entirely, closing distinguishing by the three realms (卯一約三界辨2: 繫屬魔/繫屬死) entirely — every being throughout the three realms, so long as birth continues, remains bound to death; only the arhat, who takes no further rebirth, transcends this bondage entirely. Naming it "bound to death" rather than "bound to birth" itself signals the endless continuation of rebirth after death, which only the arhat's own finality brings to a close.

25. Section: Distinguishing by Way of Persons Begins — Setting Out the Distinctions Begins — The Impure

Those spoken of as the various impure beings mean the various ordinary beings.

Chinese Source

言不清淨諸有情者，謂諸異生。

Ru-Yi Translation

Those spoken of as the various impure beings mean the various ordinary beings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens distinguishing by way of persons (卯二約有情辨2), the second of manifesting being bound's two matters, beginning with setting out the distinctions (辰一出差別3), a threefold structure, beginning with the impure (巳一不清淨) — ordinary beings, still carrying every affliction of view and craving throughout the three realms, are called impure.

26. Section: The Pure

Those spoken of as pure mean the various trainees.

Chinese Source

言清淨者，謂諸有學。

Ru-Yi Translation

Those spoken of as pure mean the various trainees.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the pure (巳二清淨), the second — trainees, from stream-entry onward, have already severed every discursively-arisen affliction; though innate afflictions of varying subtlety still remain depending on their stage, they are, relative to the ordinary being, called pure.

27. Section: Bright and Pure, Closing "Setting Out the Distinctions" Entirely

Those spoken of as bright and pure mean the various ones beyond training.

Chinese Source

言鮮白者，謂諸無學。

Ru-Yi Translation

Those spoken of as bright and pure mean the various ones beyond training.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, bright and pure (巳三鮮白) closes entirely, closing setting out the distinctions (辰一出差別3: 不清淨/清淨/鮮白) entirely — sustained, thoroughgoing purity is called bright and pure; unlike the trainee's own purity, which has only cleared away the coarser afflictions, the arhat alone is purified to the very end.

28. Section: Elaborating on Purity Begins — Setting Out the Four Confirmed Purities

There are, further, four confirmed purities, which are able to make the impure pure, and the already pure bright and pure.

Chinese Source

復有四種證淨，未清淨者能令清淨，已清淨者能令鮮白。

Ru-Yi Translation

There are, further, four confirmed purities, which are able to make the impure pure, and the already pure bright and pure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens elaborating on purity (辰二廣清淨2), the second of distinguishing by way of persons' two matters, beginning with setting out the four confirmed purities (巳一出四證淨) — the four confirmed purities toward the Buddha, dharma, saṅgha, and precepts, cultivated through the four foundations of mindfulness, bring the impure to purity and carry the already pure onward to the arhat's own bright, thoroughgoing purity.

29. Section: Distinguishing Its Stages, Closing "Elaborating on Purity," "Distinguishing by Way of Persons," "Manifesting Being Bound," and "Being Bound to Purity" Entirely

One should know that, here, the higher trainees are called pure, and the lower trainees are called impure, since they, through the path of cultivation, are not yet pure. The rest is as said before.

Chinese Source

當知此中，上諸有學說名清淨，下諸有學名不清淨，彼由修道未清淨故。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, the higher trainees are called pure, and the lower trainees are called impure, since they, through the path of cultivation, are not yet pure.

Chinese Source

餘如前說。

Ru-Yi Translation

The rest is as said before.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記 (twice) and a substantial cluster of companion sūtras (including the monk Utkaṭa and the monk Vāhika)

With this, distinguishing its stages (巳二辨其分位) closes entirely, closing elaborating on purity (辰二廣清淨2: 出四證淨/辨其分位) entirely, closing **distinguishing by way of persons (卯二約有情辨2: 出差別/廣清淨) entirely**, closing **manifesting being bound (寅二明繫屬2: 約三界辨/約有情辨) entirely**, closing **being bound to purity (丑四繫屬2: 指修相/明繫屬), the fourth of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely** — even among trainees, purity admits degrees: the higher trainee (the non-returner) is called pure, while the lower trainees (stream-enterer and once-returner), not yet free of desire-realm affliction through the path of cultivation, are called impure relative to the non-returner — though both remain pure relative to the ordinary being, and both remain impure relative to the arhat's own bright, final purity, per the 披尋記's own distinction of upper and lower trainee stages within the four confirmed purities. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this teaching to a substantial cluster of companion sūtras: the monk Utkaṭa's threefold petition to the Buddha, answered with the instruction to first purify one's own precepts and straighten one's own view before taking up the four foundations of mindfulness, after which he attained arhatship; the monk Vāhika's parallel teaching, adding that this same purification overcomes every class of Māra in turn (the aggregates, affliction, death, and the heavenly Māra); Aniruddha's question on how a trainee still short of security should proceed (through the very same four foundations); and Ānanda's own exchange with Udāyin on why the Buddha, despite having realized ultimate truth directly, nonetheless dwells at length on the merit of precepts — because purified precepts are themselves the necessary foundation for the four foundations of mindfulness's own cultivation. Several shorter companion sūtras follow in immediate succession, each simply reaffirming that sustained cultivation of the four foundations yields non-retrogression, purification, crossing to nirvāṇa's far shore, and, ultimately, arhatship itself — echoing the teaching that all the Buddha's teachings, however many, can be summarized as the four foundations of mindfulness alone.

30. Section: Purity's Gradual Sequence Begins — Marking and Listing the Five Kinds

Further, cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness, one should know there are, briefly, five gradual stages: first, the increasing power of faith, pure going forth; second, restraint through precepts; third, restraint through the sense-faculties; fourth, delight in seclusion; fifth, purity from the hindrances.

Chinese Source

復次，修四念住，應知略有五種漸次。一、信增上力，清淨出家；二、戒律儀；三、根律儀；四、樂遠離；五、蓋清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness, one should know there are, briefly, five gradual stages: first, the increasing power of faith, pure going forth; second, restraint through precepts; third, restraint through the sense-faculties; fourth, delight in seclusion; fifth, purity from the hindrances.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens purity's gradual sequence (丑五淨漸次2), the fifth of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's second phrase's third word and the third phrase's first word "purity...the gradual sequence" (勸勉繫屬淨...漸次戒圓滿), beginning with marking and listing the five kinds (寅一標列五種) — five stages unfold in order: faith's increasing power leading to a pure going-forth (never fleeing debts or taxes, but genuinely aspiring to seek and to save); precepts received, studied, and upheld (monks devoting their first five years, nuns their first six, chiefly to the monastic code, purifying body, speech, and mind); restraint of the six sense-faculties (guarding the mind, the source from which every dharma arises or ceases, through sustained right knowledge in every activity); delight in seclusion (bodily, mental, and physical seclusion — dwelling apart from crowds, free of scattered thought, cultivating only wholesome, beneficial attention); and purity from the five hindrances (craving, ill will, torpor-and-sleep, restlessness-and-worry, doubt — severed, then progressively purified, enabling calm-abiding and, within it, insight into ultimate truth).

31. Section: Distinguishing "Not for the Householder," Closing "Purity's Gradual Sequence" Entirely

Householders, though they may repeatedly cultivate the foundations of mindfulness, attain pure faith, and become pure of the hindrances, nevertheless lack the precincts of training; one should know that their cultivation does not reach completion.

Chinese Source

諸在家者，雖復數數修諸念住，獲得淨信，諸蓋清淨，然闕學處，當知所修不得圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Householders, though they may repeatedly cultivate the foundations of mindfulness, attain pure faith, and become pure of the hindrances, nevertheless lack the precincts of training; one should know that their cultivation does not reach completion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a companion sūtra

With this, distinguishing "not for the householder" (寅二簡非在家) closes entirely, closing **purity's gradual sequence (丑五淨漸次2: 標列五種/簡非在家), the fifth of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely** — a householder may indeed attain pure faith and freedom from the hindrances, but, lacking the precincts of monastic training that householders are not bound by, cannot bring this gradual sequence to full completion. Master Cháng Bǎi traces each of the five stages to its own companion sūtra passage: pure faith leading to going forth, free of desire's entangling cords; precepts as the traffic light that tells a practitioner when to go and when to stop; the six sense-faculties guarded so that whatever the eyes see becomes an occasion for mindfulness of the Three Jewels; delight in seclusion, sitting joyfully apart under a tree or in an empty room; and the five hindrances purified by stages — first severed, then progressively refined into full purity.

32. Section: The Precepts' Fullness Begins — Marking

Further, through three causes and conditions, one should know that a monk endowed with precepts brings the fullness of restrained conduct and pure livelihood to completion.

Chinese Source

復次，由三因緣，具戒苾芻當知禁戒淨命圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, through three causes and conditions, one should know that a monk endowed with precepts brings the fullness of restrained conduct and pure livelihood to completion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the precepts' fullness (丑六戒圓滿4), the sixth of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's third phrase's second half "the precepts' fullness" (漸次戒圓滿), beginning with marking (寅一標) — three causes and conditions bring a precept-endowed monk's restrained conduct and pure livelihood (all requisites obtained through blameless means) to full completion.

33. Section: Questioning

What are the three?

Chinese Source

云何為三？

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the three?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens questioning (寅二徵), the second of the precepts' fullness's four matters.

34. Section: Listing

First, fullness of conduct; second, fullness of what is taken up; third, fullness of what is used.

Chinese Source

一、所行圓滿，二、攝取圓滿，三、受用圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, fullness of conduct; second, fullness of what is taken up; third, fullness of what is used.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens listing (寅三列), the third of the precepts' fullness's four matters — a precept-endowed monk examines his own purity through three lenses: whether his own conduct is blameless, whether what he takes up is pure, and whether what he actually uses is pure.

35. Section: Explaining Begins — Fullness of Conduct

Fullness of conduct means being entirely free from buying and selling, up through harming, binding, severing limbs, striking, and massaging others, and every such matter.

Chinese Source

所行圓滿者，謂從買賣，乃至害、縛、斷截、撾打、揣摩等事，皆悉遠離。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fullness of conduct means being entirely free from buying and selling, up through harming, binding, severing limbs, striking, and massaging others, and every such matter.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 22

This opens explaining (寅四釋3), the fourth and final of the precepts' fullness's matters, beginning with fullness of conduct (卯一所行圓滿) — a monk's conduct is full when free of commerce and of every violent or improper act toward others' bodies. The Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 22, frames this instead as avoiding five improper places (a herald's house, a courtesan's house, a wine-seller's house, a king's palace, and an outcaste's or executioner's house) while otherwise moving about blamelessly at the right time — Master Cháng Bǎi notes this passage's own "fullness of conduct" corresponds instead to that other passage's "moving about blamelessly at the right time," while the Śrāvaka Ground's own "fullness of conduct" corresponds to avoiding the five improper places named there.

36. Section: Fullness of What Is Taken Up

Fullness of what is taken up means being entirely free from taking up elephants, horses, and the like, up through taking up unhusked grain and every such matter.

Chinese Source

攝取圓滿者，謂於攝取象馬等事，乃至攝取生穀等事，皆悉遠離。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fullness of what is taken up means being entirely free from taking up elephants, horses, and the like, up through taking up unhusked grain and every such matter.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Ground of Investigation and Analysis, fascicle 68

This opens fullness of what is taken up (卯二攝取圓滿), the second — keeping livestock (elephants, horses, cattle, sheep, chickens, pigs, dogs, cats) easily breeds attachment and is therefore forbidden to monastics; taking up unhusked grain, requiring hours of hulling before it can be eaten, likewise wastes practice time. Neither adorns the path of precepts, concentration, and wisdom, and both are therefore impure to take up — detailed further, per Master Cháng Bǎi, in the Ground of Investigation and Analysis's own treatment of the Śrāvaka and Solitary-Realizer Grounds, fascicle 68, under "unadorned."

37. Section: Fullness of What Is Used, Closing "Explaining" and "The Precepts' Fullness" Entirely

Fullness of what is used means that clothing merely covers the body, and food merely fills the stomach, and one is thereby satisfied; toward every surplus possession, eating at the wrong time, and the like, one is entirely free.

Chinese Source

受用圓滿者，謂衣僅蔽身，食纔充腹，便生喜足。於餘長物、非時食等，皆悉遠離。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fullness of what is used means that clothing merely covers the body, and food merely fills the stomach, and one is thereby satisfied; toward every surplus possession, eating at the wrong time, and the like, one is entirely free.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, fullness of what is used (卯三受用圓滿) closes entirely, closing explaining (寅四釋3: 所行圓滿/攝取圓滿/受用圓滿) entirely, closing **the precepts' fullness (丑六戒圓滿4: 標/徵/列/釋), the sixth of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely** — clothing enough only to cover the body and food enough only to sustain it, with contentment at even the barest provision and complete freedom from surplus possessions or eating outside the proper time, brings a monk's restrained conduct and pure livelihood to their full completion.

38. Section: The Ear of Grain Begins — Marking the Distinction

Further, body and the rest's four dharmas are like four great roads; the unprincipled attention that arises toward them is like wrongly praying for an ear of rice or wheat grain; the principled attention that arises toward them is like rightly praying for an ear of rice or wheat grain.

Chinese Source

復次，身等四法如四大路，於彼所生非理作意，如邪祈願稻穀麥穗；於彼所生如理作意，如正祈願稻穀麥穗。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, body and the rest's four dharmas are like four great roads; the unprincipled attention that arises toward them is like wrongly praying for an ear of rice or wheat grain; the principled attention that arises toward them is like rightly praying for an ear of rice or wheat grain.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing 攝異門分, fascicle 84

This opens the ear of grain (丑七穗2), the seventh of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's fourth phrase's first word "the ear of grain" (穗成就為後), beginning with marking the distinction (寅一標差別) — body, feeling, mind, and dharmas are the shared ground everyone stands on, like four great roads; misperceiving them (taking the impure body for pure, the selfless for possessing self) is like praying wrongly for a harvest — even with seed in the ground, without sun, air, water, and fertilizer, no ripened ear of grain will ever appear. 攝異門分, fascicle 84, is cited on affliction itself as a stump or stubble left in the field, causing ever-deepening, self-perpetuating growth and rendering body and mind incapable, like salt-poisoned soil unfit for any planting at all. Conversely, principled attention toward these same four is like praying rightly: the causes for purity are properly gathered, and a pure fruit is certain to follow — a practitioner who instead clings to unprincipled thought only continues circling further into saṃsāra.

39. Section: Manifesting the Outflows, Closing "The Ear of Grain" Entirely

One should know that the desire realm is the unfixed ground, just like the skin; the form and formless realms are both fixed grounds, just like the flesh; ignorance is like the blood. Within the three realms, through the three outflows, there is the meaning of trickling.

Chinese Source

當知欲界是不定地，猶如其皮；色無色界俱是定地，猶如其肉；無明如血。於三界中，由三種漏有淋漏義。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that the desire realm is the unfixed ground, just like the skin; the form and formless realms are both fixed grounds, just like the flesh; ignorance is like the blood. Within the three realms, through the three outflows, there is the meaning of trickling.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Ground with Both Investigation and Analysis (fascicle 8) and 攝異門分 (fascicle 84), and the 披尋記

With this, manifesting the outflows (寅二明有漏) closes entirely, closing **the ear of grain (丑七穗2: 標差別/明有漏) entirely** — the desire realm's affliction, coarse and readily severed through principled attention, is like grime on the skin; the form and formless realms' subtler, meditation-linked affliction (desire's outflow only temporarily suppressed, its seed unsevered) sinks deeper, like flesh; and ignorance, pervading every one of the three realms alike, is deepest of all, like blood filling the whole body — the three outflows (of desire, of existence, of ignorance) that keep every unliberated being trickling, waterlogged, unable to dwell at ease, so long as their seeds remain. The Ground with Both Investigation and Analysis, fascicle 8, defines an outflow simply as that which keeps the mind flowing outward; 攝異門分, fascicle 84, as that which is able to give rise to future birth — together naming affliction's power to detain beings within the three realms and obstruct their approach to liberation. The 披尋記 confirms the skin-flesh-blood simile maps precisely onto desire's outflow (欲漏), existence's outflow (有漏), and ignorance's outflow (無明漏) in turn, graded by their coarseness or subtlety, both within and across the three realms.

40. Section: Accomplishment Begins — Generally Marking and Distinguishing

Further, as said before, toward every immeasurable, evil, unwholesome dharma such as craving and the rest — if one possesses these through two causes and conditions, one is unable to cultivate the four foundations of mindfulness; this does not mean everyone who possesses them in a general sense.

Chinese Source

復次，如先所說所有貪等種種無量惡不善法，由二因緣若成就者，不能修習四種念住，非是一切泛成就者。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, as said before, toward every immeasurable, evil, unwholesome dharma such as craving and the rest — if one possesses these through two causes and conditions, one is unable to cultivate the four foundations of mindfulness; this does not mean everyone who possesses them in a general sense.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens accomplishment (丑八成就3), the eighth and final of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, named by the verse's fourth phrase's second half "accomplishment, coming last" (穗成就為後), beginning with generally marking and distinguishing (寅一總標簡) — merely possessing craving and the other unwholesome dharmas in some general sense does not by itself prevent cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness; only possessing them through two specific causes and conditions, unpacked in what follows, does. Per the 披尋記, this phrasing signals that possessing these unwholesome dharmas through any other cause and condition still leaves room for cultivating the four foundations. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows before further closure is claimed.

41. Section: Separately Listing the Marks

What are the two? First, because the bonds of craving and the rest are manifestly present; second, because one does not see the fault in this bond.

Chinese Source

云何為二？一、有貪等纏現前故；二、於此纏不見過故。

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the two? First, because the bonds of craving and the rest are manifestly present; second, because one does not see the fault in this bond.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately listing the marks (寅二別列相), the second of accomplishment's three matters — two causes prevent cultivating the four foundations: affliction actively, forcefully present in the mind, unable to be first subdued; and, more insidiously, never recognizing that this affliction is a fault at all, so it goes untreated and, through repetition, hardens into settled habit.

42. Section: Explaining the Reason Begins — Through the Defiled Mind

Because the bond is manifestly present, defiling the mind, one is unable to cultivate.

Chinese Source

纏現在前雜染心故，不能修習。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because the bond is manifestly present, defiling the mind, one is unable to cultivate.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing fascicle 8 and 攝異門分, fascicle 84

This opens explaining the reason (寅三釋所以2), the third of accomplishment's three matters, beginning with through the defiled mind (卯一由雜染心) — a mind actively agitated by present affliction cannot settle enough to cultivate. Per fascicle 8, a "bond" is so called because it repeatedly manifests; 攝異門分, fascicle 84, adds that its continuous, unbroken manifestation is precisely what earns the name, naming eight such bonds (shamelessness, lack of conscience, torpor, sleepiness, restlessness, remorse, envy, and stinginess) mapped onto four kinds of obstruction — shamelessness and lack of conscience obstructing the heightened precepts, torpor and sleepiness obstructing the heightened mind, restlessness and remorse obstructing the heightened wisdom's discernment, and envy and stinginess obstructing the mutual sharing of wealth and dharma among fellow practitioners.

43. Section: Through an Attached Nature Begins — Distinguishing the Mark

Though temporarily removed from it, because of an attached nature, not being free of longing, toward the various dharmas that accord with craving and the rest, the mind is scattered and agitated, constantly chasing after and sinking into them, forever disturbed by various kinds of discursive thought — therefore one is unable to cultivate the foundations of mindfulness.

Chinese Source

雖暫遠離，性染著故，非無戀故，於能隨順貪等諸法，其心散動，常逐漂淪，種種尋思恆隨擾亂，是故不能修習念住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Though temporarily removed from it, because of an attached nature, not being free of longing, toward the various dharmas that accord with craving and the rest, the mind is scattered and agitated, constantly chasing after and sinking into them, forever disturbed by various kinds of discursive thought — therefore one is unable to cultivate the foundations of mindfulness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens through an attached nature (卯二由性染著2), the second of explaining the reason's two matters, beginning with distinguishing the mark (辰一辨相) — even someone temporarily free of an affliction's manifest activity, if the underlying attachment remains unaddressed, still carries a longing that keeps the mind scattered and chasing after alluring objects, disturbed by discursive thought regardless of the temporary respite; left unremedied long enough, this hardens into settled habit.

44. Section: Refuting the Objection, Closing "Through an Attached Nature," "Explaining the Reason," "Accomplishment," the Fourth Particular Verse, and the Foundations of Mindfulness in Their Complete Entirety

If this were not so, then everyone whose nature is not deeply attached ought likewise to be unable to cultivate the foundations of mindfulness. If this were the case, there could be no one at all capable of cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness.

Chinese Source

若不爾者，諸有其性不深染著，皆應不能修習念住。若如是者，無容有能修四念住。

Ru-Yi Translation

If this were not so, then everyone whose nature is not deeply attached ought likewise to be unable to cultivate the foundations of mindfulness. If this were the case, there could be no one at all capable of cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing companion sūtras on Śāriputra's and Maudgalyāyana's parinirvāṇa (attributed by Venerable Master Yìnshùn to two lost sūtras corresponding to Saṃyutta Nikāya 47.13-14)

With this, refuting the objection (辰二遮非) closes entirely, by way of a double-negative argument: were it true that anyone lacking deep attachment could still not cultivate the four foundations, then literally no one — not even the mildly, superficially afflicted — could ever cultivate them at all, which is absurd; therefore, one whose affliction, though still present at times, is recognized as faulty and kept from deepening into settled attachment remains genuinely capable of cultivation, even without yet severing its seed. With this, **explaining the reason (寅三釋所以2: 由雜染心/由性染著) closes entirely**, closing **accomplishment (丑八成就3: 總標簡/別列相/釋所以), the eighth and final of the fourth particular verse's eight sections, entirely**, closing **the fourth and final particular summary verse of the foundations of mindfulness's embrace (癸四第四嗢柁南2: 頌標列/長行釋) entirely**. Master Cháng Bǎi, citing Venerable Master Yìnshùn's note that the corresponding Chinese Saṃyukta Āgama sūtra (juan 25) is lost, but its likely content preserved via its Pāli parallels (Saṃyutta Nikāya 47.13, the Cunda Sutta, and 47.14, the Ukkacela Sutta), recounts: grieving deeply at Śāriputra's and Maudgalyāyana's own parinirvāṇa, Ānanda is consoled by the Buddha, who teaches that their five aggregates of dharma (precepts, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and the knowledge and vision of liberation) have not vanished along with them, any more than the thirty-seven factors of enlightenment themselves have vanished — for every conditioned thing, however cherished, is by nature bound to perish, just as a great tree's largest boughs fall first, or a great mountain's largest boulders crumble first, and just as Śāriputra and Maudgalyāyana, the Buddha's own greatest śrāvaka disciples, entered nirvāṇa before the Buddha himself would. Every disciple, the Buddha teaches, should be their own island, their own refuge, and the dharma's own refuge, relying on nothing else — sustained diligent practice of the four foundations of mindfulness, cultivating right knowledge and right mindfulness while taming worldly craving and sorrow, is how the dharma is genuinely, personally realized.

With this section's closure, the foundations of mindfulness's embrace (念住攝) — the first of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, spanning fascicles 97 and 98 in their entirety, all four particular summary verses (八/五/六/八 sections respectively, 27 sections in total across both fascicles) — closes in its complete entirety. The text now turns to the second great topic, the embrace of right effort and the bases of supernormal power (正斷神足攝), taken together as a single combined topic.

45. Section: The Embrace of Right Effort and the Bases of Supernormal Power Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing

Further, a summary verse says: valor and strength, cultivation and equipoise; the alternate approach, and supernormal power, coming last.

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

勇力修等持　異門神通後

Ru-Yi Translation

Valor and strength, cultivation and equipoise; the alternate approach, and supernormal power, coming last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens **the embrace of right effort and the bases of supernormal power (壬二正斷神足攝2), the second of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics** — named by the general summary verse's own first line "the foundations of mindfulness and right effort; supernormal bases" (念住與正斷　神足及根力), combining the four right efforts (正斷) and the four bases of supernormal power (神足) into a single treatment, since the two topics share a single half-verse in the overall structure — beginning with the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列) — five matters unfold in the long-form explanation that follows: valor, strength, cultivation, equipoise, the alternate approach, and supernormal power (six terms across five sections, since the last two are treated together).

46. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — Valor Begins — Pointing to Right Effort

One should know that the four right efforts are established, as already broadly distinguished in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

應知建立四種正斷，如聲聞地已廣分別。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that the four right efforts are established, as already broadly distinguished in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋5), a fivefold structure, beginning with valor (子一勇2), named by the verse's first phrase "valor and strength" (勇力修等持), beginning with pointing to right effort (丑一指正斷) — the four right efforts (severing evil already arisen, preventing evil not yet arisen, cultivating good already arisen, and giving rise to good not yet arisen) are pointed to the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 29, for their fuller treatment.

47. Section: Explaining Diligence Begins — Setting Out the Fifth Phrase

Here, the fifth phrase of diligence, valor, is expounded.

Chinese Source

此中宣說勇第五句。

Ru-Yi Translation

Here, the fifth phrase of diligence, valor, is expounded.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing 攝事分, fascicle 85, and the 披尋記

This opens explaining diligence (丑二釋精進2), the second of valor's two matters, beginning with setting out the fifth phrase (寅一出第五句) — diligence, per 攝事分, fascicle 85, has five aspects: possessing vigor (resolving at the outset, like donning armor before battle, to cultivate the four foundations without fear); possessing exertion (sustaining the actual, uninterrupted practice of precepts, concentration, and wisdom); possessing valor (advancing courageously toward liberation, or even the Great Vehicle's own teaching, undeterred by failure); firmness and fierceness (undisturbed by cold, heat, insects, or any other bodily hardship); and not relinquishing the yoke toward every wholesome dharma (never settling for a small success, but continuing to press upward). This section treats specifically the fifth of these five, "valor" in its narrower sense.

48. Section: Explaining How the Name "Valor" Is Attained, Closing "Explaining Diligence" and "Valor" Entirely

What is called valor? It means, as said before, being able to bear every kind of suffering that arises from applying diligent effort, the suffering of exposure, the suffering of the elements' imbalance, and every kind of suffering arising from another's coarse, harsh, injurious speech and the like. Not regressing from or relinquishing the preparatory practice of cultivating right effort on account of these causes and conditions — this is therefore called valor.

Chinese Source

云何名勇？謂如前說，堪能忍受發勤精進所生眾苦、諸淋漏苦、界不平苦、他麤惡言損惱等事所生眾苦。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is called valor? It means, as said before, being able to bear every kind of suffering that arises from applying diligent effort, the suffering of exposure, the suffering of the elements' imbalance, and every kind of suffering arising from another's coarse, harsh, injurious speech and the like.

Chinese Source

非此因緣退捨修習正斷加行，故名為勇。

Ru-Yi Translation

Not regressing from or relinquishing the preparatory practice of cultivating right effort on account of these causes and conditions — this is therefore called valor.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing 攝事分, fascicle 95, and the 披尋記

With this, explaining how the name "valor" is attained (寅二釋得勇名) closes entirely, closing **explaining diligence (丑二釋精進2: 出第五句/釋得勇名) entirely**, closing **valor (子一勇2: 指正斷/釋精進), the first of the embrace of right effort and the bases of supernormal power's five sections, entirely** — valor means bearing the fatigue of sustained effort, the exposure to wind, rain, cold, and heat that comes from dwelling without proper shelter, the bodily discomfort of the elements' imbalance, and the sting of another's envious, harsh speech, all without ever regressing from the preparatory practice of the right efforts. The 披尋記 notes the phrasing here closely parallels, with minor wording differences, an earlier passage in 攝事分, fascicle 95, and cautions that "trickling" (淋漏) in this context names exposure's own physical suffering, not the trickling of affliction discussed in fascicle 97 — the two meanings must not be conflated, since bearing affliction's own drift would wrongly imply tolerating unrestrained affliction rather than merely enduring hardship.

49. Section: Strength Begins — Pointing to the Bases of Supernormal Power

Further, one should know that the four bases of supernormal power are established, as already broadly distinguished in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

復次，應知建立四種神足，如聲聞地已廣分別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, one should know that the four bases of supernormal power are established, as already broadly distinguished in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens strength (子二力2), the second of the embrace of right effort and the bases of supernormal power's five sections, named by the verse's first phrase's second half "strength" (勇力修等持), beginning with pointing to the bases of supernormal power (丑一指神足) — the four bases of supernormal power (accomplishment through aspiration's samādhi and the conduct of severance, through diligence's samādhi, through mind's samādhi, and through investigation's samādhi) are, once more, pointed to the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 29, for their fuller treatment.

50. Section: Briefly Explaining Strength Begins — Marking

Stated briefly, through four powers, the mind is sustained and brought to concentration; therefore the four bases of supernormal power are established.

Chinese Source

若略說者，由四種力持心令定，是故建立四種神足。

Ru-Yi Translation

Stated briefly, through four powers, the mind is sustained and brought to concentration; therefore the four bases of supernormal power are established.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens briefly explaining strength (丑二略說力5), the second of strength's two matters, beginning with marking (寅一標) — briefly summarized, four powers sustain and bring the mind to concentration, and it is on this basis that the four bases of supernormal power are established.

51. Section: Questioning

What are the four?

Chinese Source

云何為四？

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the four?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens questioning (寅二徵), the second of briefly explaining strength's five matters.

52. Section: Listing

First, the power of pure aspiration; second, the power of diligent application; third, the power of the mind's delight; fourth, the power of right wisdom.

Chinese Source

一、淨意樂力，二、勤務力，三、心喜樂力，四、正智力。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, the power of pure aspiration; second, the power of diligent application; third, the power of the mind's delight; fourth, the power of right wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens listing (寅三列), the third of briefly explaining strength's five matters — pure aspiration toward concentration, diligent application in pursuing it, the mind's own delight once settled within it, and the right wisdom that discerns what remains to be remedied together constitute the four powers that establish the bases of supernormal power.

53. Section: Explaining Begins — Through the First Power

One should know that, here, through the first power, one gives rise to a delighted wish toward samādhi, and, in order to realize it, cultivates diligent application.

Chinese Source

當知此中，由第一力，於三摩地發生樂欲，為證得故，修習勤務。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, through the first power, one gives rise to a delighted wish toward samādhi, and, in order to realize it, cultivates diligent application.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining (寅四釋4), the fourth of briefly explaining strength's five matters, beginning with through the first power (卯一由第一力) — hearing the true dharma among wise fellow practitioners, a delighted, confident wish toward samādhi first arises, and, carefully attending to every requisite condition for its cultivation, one settles the mind diligently upon its object.

54. Section: Through the Second Power

Through the second power, the mind first settled upon its object is made stable.

Chinese Source

由第二力，最初住心令其安定。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through the second power, the mind first settled upon its object is made stable.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens through the second power (卯二由第二力), the second — diligent, sustained application of samādhi stabilizes the mind that has just begun to settle upon its object.

55. Section: Through the Third Power

Through the third power, the mind already settled in concentration no longer scatters or is agitated, and is not made to drift outward once again.

Chinese Source

由第三力，已住定心無復散動，不令於外更復飄轉。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through the third power, the mind already settled in concentration no longer scatters or is agitated, and is not made to drift outward once again.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens through the third power (卯三由第三力), the third — a mind already settled, such as within the preparatory stage of concentration, delights so fully in that stillness that it no longer drifts back outward toward the objects of the senses.

56. Section: Through the Fourth Power

Through the fourth power, one observes the affliction to be remedied within equipoise, truly knowing whether it is severed or not yet severed. Further, toward the marks of entering, abiding in, and emerging from equipoise, one is able to skillfully discern them. Thus, further, toward every mark belonging to calm-abiding and the rest, whatever secondary afflictions belong to calm-abiding or insight, and whatever is able to remedy those secondary afflictions, one truly knows all of it.

Chinese Source

由第四力，觀察等持所治煩惱，於斷未斷如實了知。又於等持入、住、出相，能善了別。如是復於奢摩他等所有諸相，若奢摩他、毗鉢舍那諸隨煩惱，及隨煩惱能對治等，皆如實知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through the fourth power, one observes the affliction to be remedied within equipoise, truly knowing whether it is severed or not yet severed. Further, toward the marks of entering, abiding in, and emerging from equipoise, one is able to skillfully discern them. Thus, further, toward every mark belonging to calm-abiding and the rest, whatever secondary afflictions belong to calm-abiding or insight, and whatever is able to remedy those secondary afflictions, one truly knows all of it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Śrāvaka Ground

This opens through the fourth power (卯四由第四力), the fourth and final of explaining's matters — right wisdom discerns exactly which affliction within equipoise has, or has not, been severed, skillfully distinguishes the marks of entering, abiding in, and emerging from that equipoise, and truly knows every mark belonging to calm-abiding and insight alike, together with whatever secondary afflictions and remedies pertain to each. The Śrāvaka Ground identifies two such marks for each of calm-abiding and insight: an object-mark (the counterpart image of the known matter that each takes as its object) and a causal mark (calm-abiding serving as insight's own causal condition, and insight's preparatory practice serving as calm-abiding's own causal condition for future purity) — detailed further, per Master Cháng Bǎi, in the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 31. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows — briefly explaining strength's own concluding matter (寅五結) — before further closure is claimed.

57. Section: The Concluding Statement, Closing "Briefly Explaining Strength" and "Strength" Entirely

For one who delights in equipoise, within equipoise, there is only this much task belonging to equipoise; beyond this, there is nothing further, whether excess or increase.

Chinese Source

樂等持者，於等持中，但有爾所等持作事。除此更無若過若增。

Ru-Yi Translation

For one who delights in equipoise, within equipoise, there is only this much task belonging to equipoise; beyond this, there is nothing further, whether excess or increase.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the concluding statement (寅五結) closes entirely, closing briefly explaining strength (丑二略說力5: 標/徵/列/釋/結) entirely, closing **strength (子二力2: 指神足/略說力), the second of the embrace of right effort and the bases of supernormal power's five sections, entirely** — these four powers (pure aspiration, diligent application, the mind's delight, right wisdom) exhaust the entire task involved in establishing the four bases of supernormal power (aspiration's, diligence's, mind's, and investigation's own samādhi-with-the-conduct-of-severance), with nothing more to add.

58. Section: Cultivation Begins — Briefly Marking and Listing

Further, through five causes and conditions, one should know the bases of supernormal power's brief mark of cultivation: first, through becoming free of calm-abiding's own class of secondary affliction; second, through becoming free of insight's own class of secondary affliction; third, through binding the mind to insight's own class of object; fourth, through binding the mind to calm-abiding's own class of object; fifth, through binding the mind to the object shared by both classes together.

Chinese Source

復次，由五因緣，當知神足略修習相。一、由遠離奢摩他品隨煩惱故；二、由遠離毗鉢舍那品隨煩惱故；三、於毗鉢舍那品所緣境界繫縛心故；四、於奢摩他品所緣境界繫縛心故；五、俱於二品所緣境界繫縛心故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, through five causes and conditions, one should know the bases of supernormal power's brief mark of cultivation: first, through becoming free of calm-abiding's own class of secondary affliction; second, through becoming free of insight's own class of secondary affliction; third, through binding the mind to insight's own class of object; fourth, through binding the mind to calm-abiding's own class of object; fifth, through binding the mind to the object shared by both classes together.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens cultivation (子三修4), the third of the embrace of right effort and the bases of supernormal power's five sections, named by the verse's first phrase's third word "cultivation" (勇力修等持), beginning with briefly marking and listing (丑一略標列) — the first two of these five causes remedy the secondary afflictions specific to calm-abiding and to insight; the latter three establish the meditative perceptions proper to each and to both together.

59. Section: Separately Explaining the Marks Begins — The First Two Marks Begin — Setting Out the Nature Begins — Calm-Abiding's Own Secondary Affliction

One should know that, here, calm-abiding's own class of secondary affliction means the aspiration and the rest conjoined with laziness, and the aspiration and the rest conjoined with torpor and sleepiness. One should know that the aspiration and the rest conjoined with laziness is the very support for the aspiration and the rest conjoined with torpor and sleepiness.

Chinese Source

應知此中，奢摩他品隨煩惱者，謂懈怠俱行欲等，及惛沈睡眠俱行欲等。當知懈怠俱行欲等，是惛沈睡眠俱行欲等所依止性。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, calm-abiding's own class of secondary affliction means the aspiration and the rest conjoined with laziness, and the aspiration and the rest conjoined with torpor and sleepiness. One should know that the aspiration and the rest conjoined with laziness is the very support for the aspiration and the rest conjoined with torpor and sleepiness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Compendium of Consciousness-Only and the 披尋記

This opens separately explaining the marks (丑二別釋相2), the second of cultivation's four matters, beginning with the first two marks (寅一初二相2), beginning with setting out the nature (卯一出體2), beginning with calm-abiding's own secondary affliction (辰一奢摩他品隨煩惱) — laziness (defined by the Compendium of Consciousness-Only as idleness in the cultivation and severance proper to wholesome and unwholesome dharmas, obstructing diligence and increasing defilement) is itself the underlying support for the still deeper affliction of torpor and sleepiness: a practitioner too lazy to diligently cultivate the four aspiration-diligence-mind-investigation samādhis will, even while formally seated, lose the object to torpor, and, if severe enough, to outright sleep. The 披尋記 confirms "aspiration and the rest" here abbreviates the same four samādhis named throughout, each of which can fall into this slack, unhurried laziness.

60. Section: Insight's Own Secondary Affliction, Closing "Setting Out the Nature" Entirely

Insight's own class of secondary affliction means the aspiration and the rest conjoined with restlessness, and the aspiration and the rest conjoined with the agitation of sublime desires. One should know that the aspiration and the rest conjoined with restlessness is the very support for the aspiration and the rest conjoined with the agitation of sublime desires.

Chinese Source

毗鉢舍那品隨煩惱者，謂掉舉俱行欲等，及妙欲散動俱行欲等。當知掉舉俱行欲等，是妙欲散動俱行欲等所依止性。

Ru-Yi Translation

Insight's own class of secondary affliction means the aspiration and the rest conjoined with restlessness, and the aspiration and the rest conjoined with the agitation of sublime desires. One should know that the aspiration and the rest conjoined with restlessness is the very support for the aspiration and the rest conjoined with the agitation of sublime desires.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, insight's own secondary affliction (辰二毗鉢舍那品隨煩惱) closes entirely, closing setting out the nature (卯一出體2: 奢摩他品隨煩惱/毗鉢舍那品隨煩惱) entirely — restlessness, an excited, elevated mind reminiscing over past pleasant sights, sounds, places, and companions rather than seeing every dharma as illusion-like, is itself the underlying support for the still deeper affliction of agitation toward "sublime" desires — the deluded conviction that the five sense-pleasures are genuinely pure and excellent, drawing the practitioner to actively long to see, hear, or touch them once again, obstructing concentration.

61. Section: Distinguishing the Differences Begins — Toward Calm-Abiding

Further, here, through the aspiration and the rest conjoined with laziness, calm-abiding's own class is caused to dwell in defilement, yet this is not able to make every calm-abiding entirely vanish. Through the aspiration and the rest conjoined with torpor and sleepiness, calm-abiding's own class is caused to dwell in defilement, and this is, further, able to make every calm-abiding entirely vanish.

Chinese Source

又於此中，由懈怠俱行欲等，於奢摩他品令住雜染，然不能令諸奢摩他皆悉滅沒。由惛沈睡眠俱行欲等，於奢摩他品令住雜染，亦復能令諸奢摩他皆悉滅沒。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, here, through the aspiration and the rest conjoined with laziness, calm-abiding's own class is caused to dwell in defilement, yet this is not able to make every calm-abiding entirely vanish. Through the aspiration and the rest conjoined with torpor and sleepiness, calm-abiding's own class is caused to dwell in defilement, and this is, further, able to make every calm-abiding entirely vanish.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens distinguishing the differences (卯二料簡2), the second of the first two marks' two matters, beginning with toward calm-abiding (辰一於奢摩他) — laziness merely defiles calm-abiding, leaving the practitioner unable to progress diligently but not destroying the attainment outright; torpor and sleepiness, by contrast, blur and eventually erase the object of meditation entirely, extinguishing calm-abiding altogether rather than merely defiling it.

62. Section: Toward Insight, Closing "Distinguishing the Differences" and "The First Two Marks" Entirely
