119. Section: Correlating Their Establishment Begins — Able to Give Rise to Skillful Means

One should know that, here, relying on the first kind of one's own benefit, the two powers of awakened wisdom and diligence are established. Through these two powers, one is able, through skillful means, to give rise to right effort.

Chinese Source

當知此中，依第一自義，建立覺慧、精進二力。由是二力，能有方便發起正勤。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, relying on the first kind of one's own benefit, the two powers of awakened wisdom and diligence are established. Through these two powers, one is able, through skillful means, to give rise to right effort.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens correlating their establishment (卯二配建立2), the second of toward one's own benefit's two matters, beginning with able to give rise to skillful means (辰一能起方便) — awakened wisdom (discerning dependent origination and wholesome from unwholesome dharmas) and diligence together establish, through skillful means, the right efforts that carry a practitioner toward present-life nirvāṇa, each reinforcing the other in a virtuous cycle of awakening and renewed effort.

120. Section: Able to Reach Finality, Closing "Correlating Their Establishment" and "Toward One's Own Benefit" Entirely

Relying on the second kind of one's own benefit, the power of blamelessness is established. Through these three powers, every kind of one's own benefit reaches its finality.

Chinese Source

依第二自義，立無罪力。由此三力，一切自義皆得究竟。

Ru-Yi Translation

Relying on the second kind of one's own benefit, the power of blamelessness is established. Through these three powers, every kind of one's own benefit reaches its finality.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, able to reach finality (辰二能得究竟) closes entirely, closing correlating their establishment (卯二配建立2: 能起方便/能得究竟) entirely, closing **toward one's own benefit (寅一於自義2: 標差別/配建立), the first of correlating and explaining's two matters, entirely** — the power of blamelessness (freedom from the ten unwholesome deeds) secures, at minimum, a fortunate rebirth even where present-life nirvāṇa is not reached; together, all three powers bring every kind of one's own benefit to complete finality.

121. Section: Toward the Benefit of Others Begins — Manifesting Its Establishment

For one who delights in benefiting others, the benefit of others still remains. Through this increase, the power of embracing others is established.

Chinese Source

樂利他者，他義有餘。由此增上，立攝受力。

Ru-Yi Translation

For one who delights in benefiting others, the benefit of others still remains. Through this increase, the power of embracing others is established.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens toward the benefit of others (寅二於他義2), the second of correlating and explaining's two matters, beginning with manifesting its establishment (卯一明建立) — a bodhisattva who delights in benefiting others, since the first three powers alone address only one's own benefit, requires this fourth, remaining power — the power of embracing others through the four means of embrace — to genuinely accomplish others' welfare.

122. Section: Pointing to the Matters of Embrace, Closing "Toward the Benefit of Others," "Correlating and Explaining," and "Awakened Wisdom and the Rest" Entirely

One should know that the matters of embrace should be known in their marks as already distinguished in the Bodhisattva Ground.

Chinese Source

當知攝事，如菩薩地已辯其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that the matters of embrace should be known in their marks as already distinguished in the Bodhisattva Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a substantial cluster of companion sūtras (13-29) and the 披尋記

With this, pointing to the matters of embrace (卯二指攝事) closes entirely, closing toward the benefit of others (寅二於他義2: 明建立/指攝事) entirely, closing **correlating and explaining (丑二配釋2: 於自義/於他義) entirely**, closing **awakened wisdom and the rest (子二覺慧等2: 標列/配釋), the second of the embrace of the powers' six sections, entirely** — the four means of embrace, fully unpacked in the Bodhisattva Ground, fascicle 43 (and also in fascicles 41-42's own treatment of the precept of benefiting sentient beings), are here simply pointed to rather than re-explained, since the first three powers alone cannot accomplish another's benefit, per the 披尋記. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this section to a substantial cluster of companion sūtras (13 through 29): three overlapping triads of power (faith-diligence-wisdom, faith-mindfulness-wisdom, faith-concentration-wisdom, faith and wisdom being the two indispensable anchors, the other three serving as helpers) that together compose the five powers; two further four-power groupings that likewise reduce to the five powers plus this section's own four (awakened wisdom, diligence, blamelessness, embrace); a ranking of each of the four means of embrace's own supreme form (dharma-giving as supreme among giving; timely dharma-teaching as supreme among loving speech; establishing others in wholesome roots as supreme among beneficial conduct; and meeting each person exactly at their own attainment as supreme among cooperation); a verse likening the four means of embrace to a cart's linchpin, without which filial devotion between parent and child, and the very possibility of noble and sagely virtue benefiting the world, would collapse entirely; and a teaching that mastering these four powers frees a practitioner from five fears — of not surviving, of ill repute, of public assemblies, of death, and of an unfortunate rebirth — since blameless conduct and sustained mindfulness leave nothing left to fear.

123. Section: The Nation and the Various Kings Begins — Marking Their Establishment

Further, relying on the nation and the king, whether male or female, whether husband or wife, whether foolish or wise, whether dwelling as a householder or as one gone forth, one should know that ten kinds of power are established.

Chinese Source

復次，依國及王、若男、若女、若夫、若妻、若愚、若智、若處居家、若出家眾，當知建立有十種力。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, relying on the nation and the king, whether male or female, whether husband or wife, whether foolish or wise, whether dwelling as a householder or as one gone forth, one should know that ten kinds of power are established.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the nation and the various kings (子三國及王等2), the third of the embrace of the powers' six sections, named by the verse's second phrase "the nation and the rest, and the various kings" (國等及諸王), beginning with marking their establishment (丑一標建立) — ten powers are established by drawing an analogy across ten kinds of worldly figures and stations, from the ruling king down to the renunciant.

124. Section: Pointing to What the Sūtra Teaches, Closing "The Nation and the Various Kings" Entirely

That is, the various kings possess the power of sovereignty. Such powers as these are extensively explained as in the sūtra.

Chinese Source

謂諸國王有自在力。如是等力，廣說如經。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is, the various kings possess the power of sovereignty. Such powers as these are extensively explained as in the sūtra.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a companion sūtra's folk-wisdom catalog of ten worldly powers

With this, pointing to what the sūtra teaches (丑二指經說) closes entirely, closing **the nation and the various kings (子三國及王等2: 標建立/指經說), the third of the embrace of the powers' six sections, entirely** — a companion sūtra catalogs ten such worldly powers, each figure showing forth their own characteristic strength: the sovereign king's own authority; the deciding minister's own judgment; the skilled artisan's own craft; the bandit's own blade; a resentful woman's own unrelenting grudge; a crying infant's own wail (the only means it has to make its needs known); the foolish person's own habit of criticizing whatever they encounter; the wise person's own careful, penetrating observation; the renunciant's own patience (through which, as the sūtra says, "every dharma is accomplished"); and the well-read person's own capacity to infer the whole from a single part. This tenfold folk-wisdom catalog offers, alongside the doctrinal ten powers, a second sūtra's own worldly analogy for what "power" itself can mean, mapped loosely onto occupation and social role.

125. Section: The Arhat Begins — General Marking

Further, the various arhats accomplish eight powers, truly experiencing that craving, aversion, delusion, and the rest are permanently exhausted without remainder, no longer committing any evil, and cultivating every good.

Chinese Source

復次，諸阿羅漢成就八力，如實領受貪瞋癡等永盡無餘，不造諸惡，修習諸善。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the various arhats accomplish eight powers, truly experiencing that craving, aversion, delusion, and the rest are permanently exhausted without remainder, no longer committing any evil, and cultivating every good.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the arhat (子四阿羅漢2), the fourth of the embrace of the powers' six sections, named by the verse's third phrase's first half "the arhat" (阿羅漢有學), beginning with general marking (丑一總標) — the arhat's eight powers (weariness toward future existence, weariness toward the desires, the foundations of mindfulness, right effort, the bases of supernormal power, the faculties and powers, the factors of enlightenment, and the path-factors) together confirm that every affliction has been permanently exhausted, manifesting in the complete absence of evil conduct and the sustained cultivation of good.

126. Section: Separately Explaining Begins — Not Committing Any Evil Begins — Through Weariness Toward Future Existence

That is, because the mind tends toward seclusion, toward renunciation, and toward parinirvāṇa, one wearies of and turns away from future existence. Through the cause and condition of this weariness and turning away, one does not commit evil karma.

Chinese Source

謂心趣向遠離、出離、般涅槃故，厭背後有。厭背因緣，不造惡業。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is, because the mind tends toward seclusion, toward renunciation, and toward parinirvāṇa, one wearies of and turns away from future existence. Through the cause and condition of this weariness and turning away, one does not commit evil karma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens separately explaining (丑二別釋2), beginning with not committing any evil (寅一不造諸惡2), beginning with through weariness toward future existence (卯一由厭後有) — per the 披尋記, tending toward seclusion means tending away from desire and the various unwholesome dharmas; tending toward renunciation means tending away from the household life's own cramped, oppressive suffering; and tending toward parinirvāṇa means tending toward the sublime realm of nirvāṇa without remainder — this threefold tendency wearies the arhat of future existence entirely, and this very weariness is what forestalls any further evil karma.

127. Section: Through Weariness Toward the Various Desires

Further, seeing the various desires as just like a portion of burning charcoal, one wearies of and turns away from the various desires. Through the cause and condition of this weariness and turning away, one does not commit evil karma.

Chinese Source

又見諸欲猶如一分熱炭火故，厭背諸欲。厭背因緣，不造惡業。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, seeing the various desires as just like a portion of burning charcoal, one wearies of and turns away from the various desires. Through the cause and condition of this weariness and turning away, one does not commit evil karma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens through weariness toward the various desires (卯二由厭諸欲), the second — seeing the desires themselves as a live coal, dangerous to grasp no matter how enticing its glow, the arhat wearies of and turns entirely away from them, and this weariness likewise forestalls further evil karma. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows before further closure is claimed.

128. Section: Cultivating Every Good Begins — Marking

Through these two powers, one does not commit any evil. Because one does not commit evil, one, moreover, cultivates every good through six gateways.

Chinese Source

由此二力，不造諸惡。不造惡故，復由六門修習諸善。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through these two powers, one does not commit any evil. Because one does not commit evil, one, moreover, cultivates every good through six gateways.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens cultivating every good (寅二修習諸善2), the second of separately explaining's two matters, beginning with marking (卯一標) — freed of evil conduct through the two powers of weariness (toward future existence and toward the desires), the arhat's remaining six powers actively cultivate every wholesome dharma through six further gateways.

129. Section: Listing, Closing "Cultivating Every Good," "Separately Explaining," and "The Arhat" Entirely

That is: the foundations of mindfulness, right effort, the bases of supernormal power, the faculties and powers, the factors of enlightenment, and the path-factors.

Chinese Source

謂念住、正斷、神足、根力、覺支、道支。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is: the foundations of mindfulness, right effort, the bases of supernormal power, the faculties and powers, the factors of enlightenment, and the path-factors.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing several companion sūtras, including one comparing the śrāvaka's wisdom to an infant's

With this, listing (卯二列) closes entirely, closing cultivating every good (寅二修習諸善2: 標/列) entirely, closing **separately explaining (丑二別釋2: 不造諸惡/修習諸善) entirely**, closing **the arhat (子四阿羅漢2: 總標/別釋), the fourth of the embrace of the powers' six sections, entirely** — the six gateways of good (the foundations of mindfulness, right effort, the bases of supernormal power, the faculties and powers together, the factors of enlightenment, and the eightfold path) together with the two powers of weariness complete the arhat's own eight powers. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this teaching to a cluster of companion sūtras: the Tathāgata's own six powers of knowledge (knowing what accords or does not accord with reason; knowing every being's karmic ripening across the three times; knowing every meditative attainment; knowing every being's own past lives; possessing the pure heavenly eye to see where beings are reborn; and having severed every affliction, attaining both mental and wisdom liberation) that let him turn the wheel of the pure dharma and roar the lion's roar; a sevenfold power (faith, diligence, mindfulness, concentration, wisdom, shame with regard to self, and shame with regard to others) able to exhaust every outflow; and an eightfold folk-power grouping repeating the earlier tenfold worldly analogy in abbreviated form. A further sūtra distinguishes the Buddha's own wisdom-liberation from the arhat's, comparing the śrāvaka's own wisdom to an infant's own: just as a wet-nurse must, at first, reach into an infant's mouth to remove what should not be swallowed, causing distress but ensuring safety, until the child grows wise enough to know better on its own, so too the Buddha instructs, sometimes sternly, his still-learning disciples, until their wisdom, like the arhat's, no longer needs such correction — the Buddha alone having awakened to dependent origination entirely without a teacher, and alone able to establish the very precepts and paths that his disciples, however accomplished, still rely upon him to have first taught.

130. Section: The Trainee Begins — Setting Out What the Buddha Taught

Further, the various buddha-tathāgatas, by way of self-benefiting conduct and other-benefiting conduct, in order to manifest their own distinction from their various disciples, speak thus: the various trainees accomplish five powers; only the tathāgata accomplishes ten powers.

Chinese Source

復次，諸佛如來依自利行及利他行，為欲顯己與諸弟子有差別故，說如是言：諸有學者成就五力，唯有如來成就十力。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the various buddha-tathāgatas, by way of self-benefiting conduct and other-benefiting conduct, in order to manifest their own distinction from their various disciples, speak thus: the various trainees accomplish five powers; only the tathāgata accomplishes ten powers.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens the trainee (子五有學2), the fifth of the embrace of the powers' six sections, named by the verse's third phrase's second half "the trainee" (阿羅漢有學), beginning with setting out what the Buddha taught (丑一出佛說) — the Tathāgata himself draws this distinction, to manifest his own supremacy: trainees accomplish only five powers (faith, diligence, mindfulness, concentration, wisdom), while only the Tathāgata accomplishes the full ten (knowledge of what is possible and impossible, of karmic ripening, of every meditative attainment, of faculties' relative strength, of every kind of resolve, of every kind of disposition, of every path's destination, of past lives, of death and rebirth, and of the exhaustion of every outflow). Per the 披尋記, these five powers, possessing great, overwhelming force, subdue every army of Māra and are able to realize the permanent exhaustion of every outflow — hence the name "power."

131. Section: Manifesting Their Meaning Begins — Toward the Various Trainees

If there are noble disciples who accomplish the trainee's five powers, practicing self-benefiting conduct, they attain the most supreme fruit of arhatship; from this point on, without interruption, every kind of their own benefit reaches finality.

Chinese Source

若有成就有學五力，行自利行諸聖弟子，獲得最上阿羅漢果，從此無間，一切自義皆得究竟。

Ru-Yi Translation

If there are noble disciples who accomplish the trainee's five powers, practicing self-benefiting conduct, they attain the most supreme fruit of arhatship; from this point on, without interruption, every kind of their own benefit reaches finality.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens manifesting their meaning (丑二顯彼義2), the second of the trainee's two matters, beginning with toward the various trainees (寅一於諸有學) — the trainee's own five powers, cultivated through self-benefiting conduct, lead without interruption to arhatship's own supreme fruit, at which point every kind of self-benefit reaches its complete finality.

132. Section: Toward the Various Tathāgatas Begins — Briefly Distinguishing the Marks Begins — Accomplishing Two Kinds of Conduct

The Tathāgata, having already attained arhatship, accomplishes ten powers, practicing other-benefiting conduct, taking benefiting others as his very own benefit. Supposing that, at such a time, every matter of transforming beings reaches its finality, and he enters the realm of nirvāṇa without remainder — one should know that, at that time, the task to be accomplished is only then brought to completion.

Chinese Source

如來獲得阿羅漢已，成就十力，行利他行，即用利他以為自義。設於是時，一切所化其事究竟，入無餘依般涅槃界，當知爾時，於所作事方得圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

The Tathāgata, having already attained arhatship, accomplishes ten powers, practicing other-benefiting conduct, taking benefiting others as his very own benefit. Supposing that, at such a time, every matter of transforming beings reaches its finality, and he enters the realm of nirvāṇa without remainder — one should know that, at that time, the task to be accomplished is only then brought to completion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens toward the various tathāgatas (寅二於諸如來2), the second of manifesting their meaning's two matters, beginning with briefly distinguishing the marks (卯一略辨相2), beginning with accomplishing two kinds of conduct (辰一成就二行) — the Tathāgata, already having attained arhatship's own self-benefit, takes benefiting others as his very own benefit instead, his task only reaching complete fulfillment once every being he can transform has been transformed and he enters nirvāṇa without remainder.

133. Section: Manifesting Its Excellence, Closing "Briefly Distinguishing the Marks" Entirely

Whether the arhat-conduct that is cultivated, or the conduct of taking benefiting others as one's very own benefit — these two causes and conditions, relative to the various disciples, are both supreme.

Chinese Source

若所修行阿羅漢行，若為利他即自義行，此二因緣，於諸弟子皆為殊勝。

Ru-Yi Translation

Whether the arhat-conduct that is cultivated, or the conduct of taking benefiting others as one's very own benefit — these two causes and conditions, relative to the various disciples, are both supreme.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, manifesting its excellence (辰二顯其殊勝) closes entirely, closing briefly distinguishing the marks (卯一略辨相2: 成就二行/顯其殊勝) entirely — both the Tathāgata's own arhat-conduct and his conduct of taking others' benefit as his own surpass what any disciple, however accomplished, attains — a double excellence unique to the Buddha alone.

134. Section: Pointing to the Fuller Explanation, Closing "Toward the Various Tathāgatas," "Manifesting Their Meaning," and "The Trainee" Entirely

The Tathāgata's ten powers should be known in their marks as already broadly distinguished in the Bodhisattva Ground.

Chinese Source

如來十力，如菩薩地已廣分別。

Ru-Yi Translation

The Tathāgata's ten powers should be known in their marks as already broadly distinguished in the Bodhisattva Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Bodhisattva Ground, fascicles 49-50, and a substantial cluster of companion sūtras

With this, pointing to the fuller explanation (卯二指廣說) closes entirely, closing **toward the various tathāgatas (寅二於諸如來2: 略辨相/指廣說) entirely**, closing **manifesting their meaning (丑二顯彼義2: 於諸有學/於諸如來) entirely**, closing **the trainee (子五有學2: 出佛說/顯彼義), the fifth of the embrace of the powers' six sections, entirely** — the Tathāgata's ten powers are pointed, once more, to the Bodhisattva Ground's own establishment chapter, fascicles 49-50, for their fuller treatment. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this section to a cluster of companion sūtras teaching the five powers (variously named faith-diligence-mindfulness-concentration-wisdom or faith-diligence-shame-conscience-wisdom, each correlated to the four confirmed purities, the four right efforts, the four foundations, the four dhyānas, and the four noble truths in turn), the mutual encouragement or censure a community owes a wavering or steadfast practitioner, and, once more, the wet-nurse simile distinguishing the śrāvaka's five learning-powers (aimed at self-benefit alone) from the Tathāgata's own ten powers, through which he genuinely benefits both himself and others at once.

135. Section: Uprightness Begins — Marking and Distinguishing What It Relies Upon

Further, if there is a person who cherishes themselves, is without flattery and without deceit, and whose nature is upright, then, in order to realize their own benefit, there are four marks. If one relies on a badly-taught dharma and discipline, there is delay and hindrance; only by relying on a well-taught dharma and discipline is there no delay or hindrance.

Chinese Source

復次，若有自愛、無諂無誑、其性質直補特伽羅，為證自義，有四種相。若依惡說法毗奈耶，便有稽留；要依善說法毗奈耶，乃無稽留。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, if there is a person who cherishes themselves, is without flattery and without deceit, and whose nature is upright, then, in order to realize their own benefit, there are four marks. If one relies on a badly-taught dharma and discipline, there is delay and hindrance; only by relying on a well-taught dharma and discipline is there no delay or hindrance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing 攝事分, fascicle 89 (twice)

This opens uprightness (子六質直3), the sixth and final of the embrace of the powers' six sections, named by the verse's fourth phrase's first half "uprightness, coming last" (質直最為後), beginning with marking and distinguishing what it relies upon (丑一標簡所依) — a person who genuinely cherishes themselves, free of flattery and deceit, of upright nature, achieves their own benefit (either a fortunate rebirth or present-life nirvāṇa) through four marks, but only within a well-taught dharma and discipline; an outside path's own rules, however elaborate (even something as arbitrary as vowing to imitate a cow's or a dog's conduct), only ever delay and obstruct. Per 攝事分, fascicle 89, flattery is a crooked, unclear, undisclosed, deceptively-practiced mind — outwardly compliant while inwardly biased, never honestly revealing one's true thought — and deceit is deliberately misleading another, harboring one intention while displaying another, closely bound up with wrong livelihood; a genuinely self-cherishing person, needing neither to flatter a teacher nor practice wrong livelihood, delights in practice for its own sake, showing the same face outwardly as they hold inwardly.

136. Section: Separately Explaining the Four Marks Begins — Questioning

What are the four marks?

Chinese Source

云何四相？

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the four marks?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately explaining the four marks (丑二別釋四相3), beginning with questioning (寅一徵).

137. Section: Listing

First, expounding the teaching of the true dharma; second, instruction and exhortation; third, penetrating it in accordance with principle; fourth, attaining true realization.

Chinese Source

一、說正法教，二、教授教誡，三、如理通達，四、得真實證。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, expounding the teaching of the true dharma; second, instruction and exhortation; third, penetrating it in accordance with principle; fourth, attaining true realization.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens listing (寅二列), the second of separately explaining the four marks' three matters — hearing the true dharma's right teaching (freeing one from doctrines of no cause or false cause), receiving unmistaken instruction and exhortation in concentration and wisdom together with precepts, applying principled attention and thereby penetrating the dharma through hearing- and thinking-wisdom, and finally, truly realizing the ultimate truth through uncontaminated cultivation-wisdom — these four marks together constitute genuine accomplishment of one's own benefit.

138. Section: Explaining Begins — Expounding the Teaching of the True Dharma

The true dharma that is heard is the very support for every kind of confident understanding, since it is able to be free of causelessness and false causation, expounding the meaning of a right cause that accords with principle.

Chinese Source

所聞正法，是諸勝解所依止處。由能遠離無因、惡因，開示稱理正因義故。

Ru-Yi Translation

The true dharma that is heard is the very support for every kind of confident understanding, since it is able to be free of causelessness and false causation, expounding the meaning of a right cause that accords with principle.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining (寅三釋3), the third of separately explaining the four marks' three matters, beginning with expounding the teaching of the true dharma (卯一說正法教) — the true dharma, once heard, becomes the very ground upon which every subsequent confident understanding rests, precisely because it teaches genuine causality, free of both the denial of cause and the misattribution of a false one. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows before further closure is claimed.

139. Section: Instruction and Exhortation

Every unmistaken instruction and exhortation, skillfully according with the preparatory practice of severance, embraces, within its teaching's letter and meaning, the unmistaken dharma, and is able to bring about the realization of the very dharma that serves as the support for confident understanding, as said before.

Chinese Source

諸有無倒教授教誡，善能隨順斷加行教文義所攝無顛倒法，能令證得如前勝解所依處法。

Ru-Yi Translation

Every unmistaken instruction and exhortation, skillfully according with the preparatory practice of severance, embraces, within its teaching's letter and meaning, the unmistaken dharma, and is able to bring about the realization of the very dharma that serves as the support for confident understanding, as said before.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens instruction and exhortation (卯二教授教誡), the second of separately explaining the four marks' three matters — the upright practitioner draws near a good teacher and receives unmistaken instruction in concentration and wisdom together with exhortation in precepts, the teaching's own letter and meaning both free of the self-clinging and the four inverted views, this purifying teaching bringing the practitioner to the very confident understanding named in the first mark.

140. Section: Penetrating in Accordance with Principle and the Rest Begins — General Marking

If there is a self-cherishing good man whose continuum has already been tamed, possessing capability, who comes to enter the inner dharma's discipline, attains right proclamation and right awakening, then he is able to swiftly advance toward what should be advanced toward, penetrating, in accordance with principle, what should be penetrated, and also truly realizing what should truly be realized.

Chinese Source

若有自愛諸善男子，已調相續，有所堪能，來入內法毗奈耶中，得正宣說、得正開悟，便能速疾趣向勝進，如理通達所應通達，亦能實證真所應證。

Ru-Yi Translation

If there is a self-cherishing good man whose continuum has already been tamed, possessing capability, who comes to enter the inner dharma's discipline, attains right proclamation and right awakening, then he is able to swiftly advance toward what should be advanced toward, penetrating, in accordance with principle, what should be penetrated, and also truly realizing what should truly be realized.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens penetrating in accordance with principle and the rest (卯三如理通達等2), the third of separately explaining the four marks' three matters, beginning with general marking (辰一總標) — a self-cherishing practitioner whose continuum is already tamed and capable, entering the inner dharma's own discipline and receiving right proclamation and right awakening, swiftly advances, genuinely penetrating and genuinely realizing what should be — combining the third and fourth marks together.

141. Section: Separately Explaining Begins — Penetrating in Accordance with Principle

That is, taking the four foundations of mindfulness as the support, toward conditioned dharmas, whatever the various wise ones commonly agree exists, or commonly agree does not exist, one rightly knows all of it. Toward the unconditioned dharma, up to the peak of existence, all of it has something higher; one rightly knows this as having something higher. Nirvāṇa has nothing higher; one truly knows this as having nothing higher. This is called penetrating in accordance with principle.

Chinese Source

謂四念住以為依止，於有為法，諸聰慧者共許為有、或許為無，皆正了知。於無為法，乃至有頂皆是有上，能正了知是為有上；涅槃無上，如實了知是為無上。如是名為如理通達。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is, taking the four foundations of mindfulness as the support, toward conditioned dharmas, whatever the various wise ones commonly agree exists, or commonly agree does not exist, one rightly knows all of it. Toward the unconditioned dharma, up to the peak of existence, all of it has something higher; one rightly knows this as having something higher. Nirvāṇa has nothing higher; one truly knows this as having nothing higher. This is called penetrating in accordance with principle.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens separately explaining (辰二別釋2), beginning with penetrating in accordance with principle (巳一如理通達) — resting on the four foundations of mindfulness, the practitioner rightly discerns conditioned dharmas as impermanent (what the wise commonly affirm exists or does not), and, toward the unconditioned, recognizes that everything up through the peak of existence still has something higher, while nirvāṇa alone has nothing higher at all. Per the 披尋記, the unconditioned discussed here is specifically the non-analytical cessation — a mere absence due to missing conditions, not the analytical cessation wisdom actively brings about — and even the form and formless realms' own "unmoved" states are only temporarily free of manifest affliction, not permanently severed of its seed, and are therefore still classed as having something higher; nirvāṇa, conjoined with non-discriminating wisdom, alone is truly unsurpassed.

142. Section: Attaining True Realization, Closing "Separately Explaining," "Penetrating in Accordance with Principle and the Rest," "Explaining," and "Separately Explaining the Four Marks" Entirely

Further, taking the four foundations of mindfulness as the support, through a mind stilled in concentration, having already rightly cultivated the seven factors of enlightenment, one ultimately realizes clear liberation. This is called attaining true realization.

Chinese Source

又四念住以為依止，由靜定心，於七覺支正修習已，於明解脫究竟作證。如是名為得真實證。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, taking the four foundations of mindfulness as the support, through a mind stilled in concentration, having already rightly cultivated the seven factors of enlightenment, one ultimately realizes clear liberation. This is called attaining true realization.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, attaining true realization (巳二得真實證) closes entirely, closing separately explaining (辰二別釋2: 如理通達/得真實證) entirely, closing penetrating in accordance with principle and the rest (卯三如理通達等2: 總標/別釋) entirely, closing **explaining (寅三釋3: 說正法教/教授教誡/如理通達等) entirely**, closing **separately explaining the four marks (丑二別釋四相3: 徵/列/釋) entirely** — resting again on the four foundations, a still, concentrated mind cultivating the seven factors of enlightenment ultimately realizes clear liberation itself — the fourth and final mark, completing the upright practitioner's full accomplishment of their own benefit.

143. Section: Distinguishing the Name "Delay and Hindrance," Closing "Uprightness" and the Entire Embrace of the Powers Entirely

If that self-cherishing good man enters a badly-taught dharma and discipline, he is unable to attain any of these four places; therefore this is called delay and hindrance.

Chinese Source

若彼自愛諸善男子，趣入惡說法毗奈耶，於是四處皆不能得，故名稽留。

Ru-Yi Translation

If that self-cherishing good man enters a badly-taught dharma and discipline, he is unable to attain any of these four places; therefore this is called delay and hindrance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a substantial cluster of companion sūtras on the Tathāgata's ten powers and the four marks

With this, distinguishing the name "delay and hindrance" (丑三簡名稽留) closes entirely, closing **uprightness (子六質直3: 標簡所依/別釋四相/簡名稽留), the sixth and final of the embrace of the powers' six sections, entirely** — the very same self-cherishing good man who, entering a badly-taught outside path instead, attains none of the four marks, remains stalled in the ordinary being's own condition, unable even to reach a fortunate rebirth, let alone nirvāṇa. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this closing section to a cluster of companion sūtras: further teachings on the Tathāgata's own ten powers (both extensively and briefly stated), the four kinds of fearlessness these powers produce, and, correlated one by one to the four marks, an upright disciple's own reception of unmistaken instruction, penetrating understanding of what has something higher and what has nothing higher, and the trainee's five powers set once more against the Tathāgata's ten. **With this section's closure, the embrace of the powers (壬四力攝2: 思擇/覺慧等/國等及諸王/阿羅漢/有學/質直), the fourth of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, closes in its complete entirety.**

144. Section: The Embrace of the Factors of Enlightenment Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing

Further, a summary verse says: establishing them, their distinctions; their food, the gradual sequence; ease, and abiding; cultivation, coming last.

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

立差別　食漸次　安樂住　修居後

Ru-Yi Translation

Establishing them, their distinctions; their food, the gradual sequence; ease, and abiding; cultivation, coming last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens **the embrace of the factors of enlightenment (壬五覺支攝2), the fifth of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics**, named by the general summary verse's third line's first word "the factors of enlightenment" (覺道支息念) — treating the seven factors of enlightenment (念/擇法/精進/喜/安/定/捨) — beginning with the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列) — seven matters unfold in the long-form explanation: establishing them, their distinctions, their nourishing food, the gradual sequence in which they arise, the ease they bring about, the abiding proper to them at every stage, and their own cultivation.

145. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — Establishing Them, Closing "Establishing Them" Entirely

Through the distinct classes of calm-abiding, insight, and both together, the factors of enlightenment are established. This should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

由奢摩他、毗鉢舍那俱品差別，建立覺支。如聲聞地應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Through the distinct classes of calm-abiding, insight, and both together, the factors of enlightenment are established. This should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 29

This opens the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋7), a sevenfold structure matching the verse's seven matters, beginning with establishing them (子一立), named by the verse's first phrase's first half "establishing them" (立差別) — with this section's closure, establishing them (子一立), the first of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, closes entirely, since it stands as a single, undivided matter. The Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 29, distributes the seven factors across three classes: investigation of dharmas, diligence, and joy belong to insight's own class; pliancy, concentration, and equanimity belong to calm-abiding's own class; and mindfulness alone, pervading both classes equally, is called the universally-present factor, since both calm-abiding and insight equally depend upon it.

146. Section: Their Distinctions, Closing "Their Distinctions" Entirely

Further, through the distinction of their own nature, and through the distinction of their object-mark and causal mark, one should know that the seven factors of enlightenment have fourteen distinctions. The broadly distinguished meaning of the object-mark and causal mark should be known in its marks as in the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation and the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

復次，自性差別故，及所緣、因緣相差別故，應知七覺支，十四種差別。所緣、因緣相廣分別義，如三摩呬多地及聲聞地應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, through the distinction of their own nature, and through the distinction of their object-mark and causal mark, one should know that the seven factors of enlightenment have fourteen distinctions. The broadly distinguished meaning of the object-mark and causal mark should be known in its marks as in the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation and the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Compendium of Abhidharma, fascicle 10, and a companion sūtra on the tenfold hindrances

With this, their distinctions (子二差別) closes entirely, since it, too, stands as a single, undivided matter, named by the verse's first phrase's second half "their distinctions" (立差別) — each of the seven factors doubles into two, yielding fourteen: mindfulness splits into inward and outward foundations of mindfulness; investigation of dharmas into discerning the wholesome and discerning the unwholesome; diligence into severing the unwholesome and nurturing the wholesome; joy into joy's own nature and its arising ground; pliancy into bodily and mental pliancy; concentration into its own nature (one-pointedness of mind) and its own mark; and equanimity into equanimity toward the wholesome and equanimity toward the unwholesome. The Compendium of Abhidharma, fascicle 10, identifies the seven factors' shared object as the four noble truths' own true nature (the ultimate, purifying object), and details each factor's own function: mindfulness as the supporting factor (through which every wholesome dharma is never forgotten), investigation of dharmas as the very essence of the path of seeing, diligence as the factor of transcendence, joy as the factor of benefit, and pliancy, concentration, and equanimity together as the undefiled factors (equanimity removing coarseness's fault, concentration effecting the very basis's transformation, and equanimity itself permanently removing craving and sorrow). Master Cháng Bǎi traces the fourteenfold distinction to a companion sūtra recounting how outside paths, challenged that they too teach severing the five hindrances, abiding in the four foundations, and cultivating the seven factors, left a group of monks unable to answer — until the Buddha revealed that the five hindrances themselves divide into ten (inward and outward craving; anger's own nature and its outward expression; heavy sleep and light torpor; restlessness and remorse; and doubt toward wholesome and toward unwholesome dharmas), matching the seven factors' own fourteen, and further taught that a weak, hesitant mind should cultivate investigation, diligence, and joy (fanning a small flame with dry kindling), while a restless, scattered mind should cultivate pliancy, concentration, and equanimity (smothering a roaring fire with spent embers) — mindfulness alone assisting at every time regardless.

147. Section: Their Food Begins — Raising What Serves as Food Begins — Marking

Further, among the dharmas that accord with the factors of enlightenment, there are, briefly, two kinds of unmistaken attention; one should know that these together serve as food for the factors of enlightenment.

Chinese Source

復次，於能隨順覺支法中，略有二種無倒作意，當知總與覺支為食。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, among the dharmas that accord with the factors of enlightenment, there are, briefly, two kinds of unmistaken attention; one should know that these together serve as food for the factors of enlightenment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens their food (子三食2), the third of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, named by the verse's second phrase's first half "their food" (食漸次), beginning with raising what serves as food (丑一舉為食3), beginning with marking (寅一標) — two kinds of unmistaken attention, briefly stated, serve as the very food that nourishes and strengthens the seven factors of enlightenment.

148. Section: Questioning

What are the two?

Chinese Source

何等為二？

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the two?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens questioning (寅二徵), the second of raising what serves as food's three matters. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows before further closure is claimed.

149. Section: Listing, Closing "Raising What Serves as Food" Entirely

First, right attention; second, repeated attention.

Chinese Source

一、正作意，二、數作意。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, right attention; second, repeated attention.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, listing (寅三列) closes entirely, closing **raising what serves as food (丑一舉為食3: 標/徵/列) entirely** — right attention (attention conjoined with every buddha and bodhisattva's own compassionate wish to benefit beings, according with the wisdom of both self- and dharma-emptiness) and repeated attention (sustaining this right attention again and again) together serve as the very food that nourishes the seven factors of enlightenment toward realization.

150. Section: Reversing to What Is Not Food, Closing "Their Food" Entirely

The reverse of this, one should know, is not food.

Chinese Source

與此相違，當知非食。

Ru-Yi Translation

The reverse of this, one should know, is not food.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a substantial cluster of companion sūtras on the food and non-food of the five hindrances and seven factors of enlightenment

With this, reversing to what is not food (丑二翻非食) closes entirely, closing **their food (子三食2: 舉為食/翻非食), the third of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, entirely** — sustained unprincipled attention is what "non-food" names, starving the factors of enlightenment exactly as sustained principled attention nourishes them. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this section to a substantial cluster of companion sūtras detailing, in mirror-image pairs, what feeds and starves each of the five hindrances and seven factors in turn: craving is fed by a pleasing sign and starved by contemplating impurity; aversion is fed by a sense of obstruction and starved by cultivating loving-kindness; torpor is fed by weakness, displeasure, yawning, overeating, and laziness, and starved by contemplating light; restlessness-and-remorse is fed by thoughts of family, of companions, of the heavens, and of past pleasures, and starved by the nine stages of settling the mind; and doubt is fed by uncertainty across the three times, and starved by deep reflection on dependent origination. In parallel, mindfulness is fed by the four foundations and starved by neglecting them; investigation of dharmas is fed by discerning wholesome from unwholesome and starved by laziness in that discernment; diligence is fed by the four right efforts and starved by failing to examine one's own progress; joy is fed by reflecting on its own nature and its arising ground and starved by neglecting that reflection; pliancy is fed by attending to bodily and mental ease and starved by neglecting it; concentration is fed by the four dhyānas and starved by indifference toward them; and equanimity is fed by reflecting on the three realms of severance, freedom from desire, and cessation, and starved by neglecting that reflection — Master Cháng Bǎi further recounts a companion sūtra in which even Prince Abhaya, prompted by an outside teacher's doctrine of causelessness, receives the Buddha's correction that both affliction and purity arise through real causes and conditions, the five hindrances producing affliction and the seven factors producing purity, cultivated in their own proper, gradual sequence.

151. Section: The Gradual Sequence Begins — Marking Their Establishment

Further, at the beginning, in the middle, or at the end, lacking any single factor prevents true awakening from reaching completion. Just as, according to their own kind, the supporting and the supported flow onward and are established, so too, in accordance with their own arising, they are spoken of in a gradual sequence.

Chinese Source

復次，於初中後隨闕一支，令如實覺不得圓滿。如其色類所依、能依流轉安立，隨其生起漸次而說。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, at the beginning, in the middle, or at the end, lacking any single factor prevents true awakening from reaching completion. Just as, according to their own kind, the supporting and the supported flow onward and are established, so too, in accordance with their own arising, they are spoken of in a gradual sequence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the gradual sequence (子四漸次2), the fourth of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, named by the verse's second phrase's second half "the gradual sequence" (食漸次), beginning with marking their establishment (丑一標安立) — missing even one of the seven factors, at any stage of practice, keeps true awakening from ever reaching completion; each factor supports the one that follows, and this supporting relationship is what "gradual sequence" describes.

152. Section: Explaining Their Mutual Support, Closing "The Gradual Sequence" Entirely

One should know that, here, mindfulness is the support, and investigation of dharmas is what is supported by it; one should know that the rest, in accordance with what is fitting, are likewise so.

Chinese Source

當知此中，念為所依，擇法能依；餘隨所應，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, mindfulness is the support, and investigation of dharmas is what is supported by it; one should know that the rest, in accordance with what is fitting, are likewise so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Compendium of Dharma-Aggregates

With this, explaining their mutual support (丑二釋相依) closes entirely, closing **the gradual sequence (子四漸次2: 標安立/釋相依), the fourth of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, entirely** — mindfulness supports investigation of dharmas, which supports diligence, which supports joy, which supports pliancy, which supports concentration, which supports equanimity, each factor arising in turn from the one before. The Compendium of Dharma-Aggregates traces this chain explicitly: dwelling in the contemplation that follows the body, free of delusion, gives rise to the factor of mindfulness; discerning the body's own nature through that mindfulness gives rise to the factor of investigating dharmas; diligent effort free of faint-heartedness gives rise to the factor of diligence; supreme joy, free of craving's own savor, gives rise to the factor of joy; bodily and mental pliancy, free of coarseness, gives rise to the factor of pliancy; the ease born of that pliancy gives rise to the factor of concentration; and the concentrated mind's own extinguishing of craving and sorrow gives rise to the factor of equanimity — the same sequence repeating for feeling, mind, and dharmas as it does for the body.

153. Section: Ease Begins — Distinguishing Abiding Begins — Setting Out the Causes and Conditions Begins — Distinguishing the Difference Begins — Raising Failure to Abide Begins — Marking

Further, if there is a monk who, toward the various factors of enlightenment, cultivates them through skillful means, there are four causes and conditions that prevent him from dwelling securely at ease.

Chinese Source

復次，若有苾芻，於諸覺支方便修習，由四因緣，令其不得安隱而住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, if there is a monk who, toward the various factors of enlightenment, cultivates them through skillful means, there are four causes and conditions that prevent him from dwelling securely at ease.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens ease (子五安樂2), the fifth of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, named by the verse's third phrase's first half "ease" (安樂住), beginning with distinguishing abiding (丑一辨安住2), beginning with setting out the causes and conditions (寅一出因緣2), beginning with distinguishing the difference (卯一簡差別2), beginning with raising failure to abide (辰一舉不安住3), beginning with marking (巳一標) — even a monk diligently cultivating the seven factors of enlightenment through skillful means can still be kept from secure, easeful dwelling by four specific causes and conditions.

154. Section: Questioning

What are called the four causes and conditions?

Chinese Source

何等名為四種因緣？

Ru-Yi Translation

What are called the four causes and conditions?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens questioning (巳二徵), the second of raising failure to abide's three matters.

155. Section: Listing, Closing "Raising Failure to Abide" Entirely

First, because every class of affliction's own coarseness has not yet been abandoned; second, because the various secondary afflictions belonging to calm-abiding's own class are presently manifest; third, because the various secondary afflictions belonging to insight's own class are presently manifest; fourth, because the path has not yet been well tamed, and yet one rides upon it.

Chinese Source

一者、一切煩惱品類麤重皆未離故；二者、奢摩他品諸隨煩惱現在前故；三者、毗鉢舍那品諸隨煩惱現在前故；四者、道未調善而乘駕故。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, because every class of affliction's own coarseness has not yet been abandoned; second, because the various secondary afflictions belonging to calm-abiding's own class are presently manifest; third, because the various secondary afflictions belonging to insight's own class are presently manifest; fourth, because the path has not yet been well tamed, and yet one rides upon it.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, listing (巳三列) closes entirely, closing **raising failure to abide (辰一舉不安住3: 標/徵/列), the first of distinguishing the difference's two matters, entirely** — four causes prevent secure dwelling: every affliction's own underlying coarseness, unabandoned, leaves body and mind incapable; calm-abiding's own secondary afflictions (torpor and sleepiness) manifest when calming is overdone, obscuring the object; insight's own secondary afflictions (restlessness and remorse) manifest when investigation goes too far; and attempting to "ride" the path — practicing without genuine familiarity with how calm-abiding and insight actually operate together — leaves a practitioner unable to diagnose or correct their own difficulties.

156. Section: Reversing to Attained Abiding, Closing "Distinguishing the Difference" Entirely

The reverse of this, four causes and conditions, cause him to attain secure, easeful dwelling. That is: first, one should remedy affliction's own seed; second, one should remedy the torpor and sleepiness that obstruct calm-abiding's own class; third, one should remedy the restlessness, remorse, and doubt that obstruct insight's own class; fourth, one should thoroughly cultivate the noble path, so that the mind becomes tamed and sovereign.

Chinese Source

與此相違，四種因緣，令其獲得安隱而住。即：一、應對治煩惱的種子，二、應對治能障奢摩他品的惛沈、睡眠煩惱，三、應對治能障毗鉢舍那品的掉舉惡作與疑，四、熟修聖道，則心能夠調善自在。

Ru-Yi Translation

The reverse of this, four causes and conditions, cause him to attain secure, easeful dwelling. That is: first, one should remedy affliction's own seed; second, one should remedy the torpor and sleepiness that obstruct calm-abiding's own class; third, one should remedy the restlessness, remorse, and doubt that obstruct insight's own class; fourth, one should thoroughly cultivate the noble path, so that the mind becomes tamed and sovereign.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens reversing to attained abiding (辰二翻得安住), the second of distinguishing the difference's two matters — remedying affliction's own seed (not merely its manifest activity), remedying the torpor and sleepiness that obstruct calm-abiding, remedying the restlessness, remorse, and doubt that obstruct insight, and thoroughly cultivating the noble path until the mind is genuinely tamed rather than merely ridden upon, a monk attains secure, easeful dwelling within the seven factors of enlightenment. With this, distinguishing the difference (卯一簡差別2: 舉不安住/翻得安住) closes entirely.

157. Section: Concluding Right Knowledge, Closing "Setting Out the Causes and Conditions" Entirely

Toward these two kinds of skillful means, the monk, truly penetrating them, dwells with right knowledge.

Chinese Source

於此二種善巧，苾芻如實了達，正知而住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Toward these two kinds of skillful means, the monk, truly penetrating them, dwells with right knowledge.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding right knowledge (卯二結正知) closes entirely, closing **setting out the causes and conditions (寅一出因緣2: 簡差別/結正知), the first of distinguishing abiding's two matters, entirely** — a monk who genuinely penetrates both what prevents and what secures easeful dwelling among the seven factors dwells with right knowledge throughout.

158. Section: Explaining the Difficult Point Begins — Raising the Untamed Path

Because the various attentions involve preparatory exertion, diligence becomes excessive; further, because there is increase and decrease from before to after, the operation is unequal. Through these two conditions, one should know this is called the path not yet well tamed.

Chinese Source

由諸作意有加行故，精進太過；又由前後有增減故，運轉不等。由此二緣，當知名為道不調善。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because the various attentions involve preparatory exertion, diligence becomes excessive; further, because there is increase and decrease from before to after, the operation is unequal. Through these two conditions, one should know this is called the path not yet well tamed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining the difficult point (寅二隨難釋2), the second of distinguishing abiding's two matters, beginning with raising the untamed path (卯一舉不調善) — attention applied too forcefully makes diligence excessive, and a sitting that starts balanced but later tips into restlessness or torpor makes the practice uneven from start to finish — both marks of a path not yet genuinely tamed.

159. Section: Reversing to the Name "Well Tamed," Closing "Explaining the Difficult Point" and "Distinguishing Abiding" Entirely

The reverse of this, through two causes and conditions, is called the path well tamed.

Chinese Source

與此相違，二因緣故，名道調善。

Ru-Yi Translation

The reverse of this, through two causes and conditions, is called the path well tamed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a companion sūtra featuring the elders Aḍḍhamāsa and Upavāna

With this, reversing to the name "well tamed" (卯二翻名調善) closes entirely, closing explaining the difficult point (寅二隨難釋2: 舉不調善/翻名調善) entirely, closing **distinguishing abiding (丑一辨安住2: 出因緣/隨難釋), the first of ease's two matters, entirely** — attention applied neither too forcefully nor too laxly, sustained evenly from beginning to end, is what "well tamed" names. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this section to a companion sūtra in which the elder Upavāna, questioned by the elder Aḍḍhamāsa about how a monk skilled in the seven factors can dwell equally at ease whether in pleasant or painful feeling, explains that mastering the four causes that obstruct abiding, then rightly cultivating each factor in its proper time — remedying torpor in calm-abiding, remedying restlessness in insight, and keeping effort neither excessive nor slack — allows the mind to remain undisturbed regardless of what feeling arises.

160. Section: The Simile of Sovereignty Begins — General Marking

Just as a wheel-turning king, attaining great sovereignty over the four continents, obtains the seven treasures — so too, one should know, the sovereign mind, attaining great sovereignty over the four noble truths, obtains the true, pure treasure of the seven factors of enlightenment, likewise.

Chinese Source

如轉輪王，於四洲渚得大自在，所獲七寶；如是心王，於四聖諦得大自在，所獲真淨七覺支寶，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

Just as a wheel-turning king, attaining great sovereignty over the four continents, obtains the seven treasures — so too, one should know, the sovereign mind, attaining great sovereignty over the four noble truths, obtains the true, pure treasure of the seven factors of enlightenment, likewise.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the simile of sovereignty (丑二喻自在2), the second of ease's two matters, beginning with general marking (寅一總標) — just as a wheel-turning king's sovereignty over the four continents comes with seven treasures that aid his rule, so too the mind that attains sovereignty over the four noble truths comes into possession of the seven factors of enlightenment as its own true, pure treasure.

161. Section: Separately Distinguishing Begins — The Simile for the Factor of Mindfulness

That is, operating within both the class of calm-abiding and the class of insight together, subduing every supreme enemy of affliction — by this meaning, the first, mindfulness, the factor of enlightenment, is just like the wheel treasure.

Chinese Source

謂於奢摩他、毗鉢舍那雙品運轉，降伏一切煩惱勝怨。由此義故，初念覺支猶如輪寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is, operating within both the class of calm-abiding and the class of insight together, subduing every supreme enemy of affliction — by this meaning, the first, mindfulness, the factor of enlightenment, is just like the wheel treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately distinguishing (寅二別辨7), a sevenfold structure matching the seven treasures to the seven factors, beginning with the simile for the factor of mindfulness (卯一喻念覺支) — mindfulness, sustaining both calm-abiding and insight operating together upon the four noble truths, subdues affliction's every supreme enemy, just as the wheel treasure carries the wheel-turning king to all four continents, subduing every rival kingdom in turn.

162. Section: The Simile for the Factor of Investigating Dharmas

The mark of the known object is boundless in its measure, and the very substance of the knowing wisdom is likewise vast in accordance with it — by this meaning, the factor of investigating dharmas is just like the elephant treasure.

Chinese Source

所知境相其量無邊，能知智體亦隨廣大。由此義故，擇法覺支猶如象寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

The mark of the known object is boundless in its measure, and the very substance of the knowing wisdom is likewise vast in accordance with it — by this meaning, the factor of investigating dharmas is just like the elephant treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the simile for the factor of investigating dharmas (卯二喻擇法覺支), the second — investigation of dharmas' own wisdom, vast enough to match its boundless object, penetrating every dharma's own particular and shared characteristics alike, is likened to the six-tusked white elephant treasure, whose vast body carries the king across the entire world in a single day without fatigue, never even wetting its feet as it crosses the water.

163. Section: The Simile for the Factor of Diligence

Relying on this, one is swiftly able to go to the place of conduct, and attain the distinctly supreme place — by this meaning, the factor of diligence is just like the horse treasure.

Chinese Source

依此速能，乃至往彼所行、所得殊異勝處。由此義故，精進覺支猶如馬寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

Relying on this, one is swiftly able to go to the place of conduct, and attain the distinctly supreme place — by this meaning, the factor of diligence is just like the horse treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the simile for the factor of diligence (卯三喻精進覺支), the third — diligence swiftly carries the practitioner to ultimate truth's own supreme attainment, just as the horse treasure swiftly carries the king wherever he needs to go and back again by evening.

164. Section: The Simile for the Factor of Joy

Delighting the mind, without fault, most supreme of all — by this meaning, the factor of joy is just like the woman treasure.

Chinese Source

悅意無罪，最為殊勝。由此義故，其喜覺支猶如女寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

Delighting the mind, without fault, most supreme of all — by this meaning, the factor of joy is just like the woman treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the simile for the factor of joy (卯四喻喜覺支), the fourth — joy delights the mind entirely without fault, just as the woman treasure, virtuous, attentive, and graceful, attends the king with perfect care, sensing and meeting his every need before he must ask.

165. Section: The Simile for the Factor of Pliancy

Body and mind rendered luminous and clear, possessing capability — by this meaning, the factor of pliancy is just like the divine pearl treasure.

Chinese Source

身心映徹，有所堪能。由此義故，輕安覺支如神珠寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

Body and mind rendered luminous and clear, possessing capability — by this meaning, the factor of pliancy is just like the divine pearl treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the simile for the factor of pliancy (卯五喻輕安覺支), the fifth — pliancy renders body and mind luminously clear, capable of penetrating every dharma's own true mark, just as the divine pearl treasure, flawlessly transparent, faithfully reflects and illuminates whatever comes before it, even lighting an entire city through the night.

166. Section: The Simile for the Factor of Concentration

Able to accomplish every matter delighted in and sought — by this meaning, the factor of concentration is just like the treasurer-minister treasure.

Chinese Source

能辦一切所欣求事。由此義故，其定覺支如藏臣寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

Able to accomplish every matter delighted in and sought — by this meaning, the factor of concentration is just like the treasurer-minister treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the simile for the factor of concentration (卯六喻定覺支), the sixth — concentration accomplishes every matter a practitioner might seek, from insight's own cultivation through supernormal power and the exhaustion of every outflow, just as the treasurer-minister treasure, knowing where every hidden store of wealth lies, ensures the king's realm never lacks for resources.

167. Section: The Simile for the Factor of Equanimity, Closing "Separately Distinguishing," "The Simile of Sovereignty," and "Ease" Entirely

Able to crush every army of defiled dharmas, able to lead every army of pure dharmas, able to advance toward the signless, secure abode — by this meaning, the factor of equanimity is just like the general treasure.

Chinese Source

能摧一切染汙法軍，能率一切清淨法軍，能趣無相安隱住處。由此義故，其捨覺支如軍將寶。

Ru-Yi Translation

Able to crush every army of defiled dharmas, able to lead every army of pure dharmas, able to advance toward the signless, secure abode — by this meaning, the factor of equanimity is just like the general treasure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a Madhyama Āgama and Ekottara Āgama companion parallel on the wheel-turning king's own seven treasures

With this, the simile for the factor of equanimity (卯七喻捨覺支) closes entirely, closing **separately distinguishing (寅二別辨7: 念/擇法/精進/喜/輕安/定/捨) entirely**, closing **the simile of sovereignty (丑二喻自在2: 總標/別辨) entirely**, closing **ease (子五安樂2: 辨安住/喻自在), the fifth of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, entirely** — equanimity crushes every defiled army and leads every pure one toward the signless, secure abode of ultimate truth, just as the general treasure, wise and resolute, leads the king's own armies to subdue every bandit and secure peace for his people. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this entire sevenfold simile to a Madhyama Āgama discourse (and its Ekottara Āgama parallel) describing the appearance of a wheel-turning king's seven treasures in full ceremonial detail — the thousand-spoked golden wheel, the white six-tusked elephant, the swift horse, the eight-cornered luminous pearl, the graceful and attentive woman, the treasure-finding minister, and the resolute general — each treasure now mapped in turn onto mindfulness, investigation of dharmas, diligence, pliancy, joy, concentration, and equanimity.

168. Section: Abiding Begins — Marking the Simile

Further, the various practitioners who attain the seven factors of enlightenment are just like a great king who possesses a chest of sublime garments, drawn upon at three times. One should know that abiding in these seven factors of enlightenment, by three divisions, is likewise so.

Chinese Source

復次，諸修行者得七覺支，譬如大王有妙衣篋，三時受用。三分安住彼七覺支，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the various practitioners who attain the seven factors of enlightenment are just like a great king who possesses a chest of sublime garments, drawn upon at three times. One should know that abiding in these seven factors of enlightenment, by three divisions, is likewise so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens abiding (子六住2), the sixth of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, named by the verse's third phrase's second half "abiding" (安樂住), beginning with marking the simile (丑一標喻) — just as a king draws a different garment from his chest at each of the day's three divisions, a practitioner draws upon the seven factors of enlightenment across three corresponding divisions of practice.

169. Section: Explaining the Marks Begins — The Three Times

As for the three times: they are the first portion of the day, the middle portion of the day, and the last portion of the day.

Chinese Source

言三時者，謂初日分時、中日分時、後日分時。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for the three times: they are the first portion of the day, the middle portion of the day, and the last portion of the day.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining the marks (丑二釋相3), a threefold structure, beginning with the three times (寅一三時) — the day divides into three four-hour portions (roughly six to ten, ten to two, and two to six), and the night likewise into three corresponding portions.

170. Section: The Three Divisions

As for the three divisions: they are calm-abiding's own class, insight's own class, and the class shared by both.

Chinese Source

言三分者，謂奢摩他品、毗鉢舍那品及其俱品。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for the three divisions: they are calm-abiding's own class, insight's own class, and the class shared by both.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the three divisions (寅二三分), the second — calm-abiding's own class holds pliancy, concentration, equanimity, and mindfulness; insight's own class holds investigation of dharmas, diligence, joy, and mindfulness; and the class shared by both, practiced together, holds all seven at once.

171. Section: Abiding Begins — Completeness or Deficiency of the Factors

Within the first division, one abides in four factors; within the second division, one abides in four factors; within the third division, one abides fully in all seven factors. The various practitioners have never abided in only a single factor alone.

Chinese Source

於初分中住四覺支，第二分中住四覺支，第三分中具足安住七種覺支。諸修行者，未曾安住唯一覺支。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the first division, one abides in four factors; within the second division, one abides in four factors; within the third division, one abides fully in all seven factors. The various practitioners have never abided in only a single factor alone.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 瑜伽論記

This opens abiding (寅三安住3), the third of explaining the marks' three matters, beginning with completeness or deficiency of the factors (卯一支具闕) — calm-abiding's own division holds four factors (concentration, pliancy, equanimity, mindfulness), insight's own division likewise holds four (investigation of dharmas, diligence, joy, mindfulness), and the shared division, where calm-abiding and insight operate together, holds all seven at once — per the 瑜伽論記, "both concentration and wisdom together, all seven factors complete." No practitioner ever abides in a single, isolated factor alone; the factors always operate in their proper groupings. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows before further closure is claimed.

172. Section: Without Hatred and the Rest

Further, the seven factors of enlightenment, because one is without hatred toward the various outside paths, because one is without contention or rivalry, because one's mind constantly operates holding the aspiration to benefit others, and because one is entirely free from every affliction's bondage, are called without hatred, without enmity, without harm, without any calamity.

Chinese Source

又七覺支，於諸外道無怨憎故，無違競故，恆懷利益意樂轉故，一切煩惱皆離繫故，說名無怨、無敵、無害、無有災患。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the seven factors of enlightenment, because one is without hatred toward the various outside paths, because one is without contention or rivalry, because one's mind constantly operates holding the aspiration to benefit others, and because one is entirely free from every affliction's bondage, are called without hatred, without enmity, without harm, without any calamity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens without hatred and the rest (卯二無怨等), the second of abiding's three matters — a practitioner grounded in the seven factors of enlightenment holds no hatred toward outside paths (recognizing their error as mere temporary delusion), enters no rivalry with them, sustains a constant wish to benefit every being, and remains entirely free of affliction's own bondage — four qualities together named without hatred, without enmity, without harm, and without calamity.

173. Section: Truly Knowing, Closing "Abiding" Entirely

If the practitioner, toward the seven factors of enlightenment, has them manifest at any time, manifest to any measure, this is called abiding. If at some time they withdraw, this is called cessation. Toward all of this, one truly knows it. Because such a one abides with right knowledge in this way, this is called blameless abiding, without any craving-savor, the mind free of the defilement of savoring.

Chinese Source

若修行者，於七覺分隨時現前、隨量現前，說名為住。若時退出，說名為滅。於是一切如實了知。彼由如是正知住故，名無罪住，無有愛味，心離味染。

Ru-Yi Translation

If the practitioner, toward the seven factors of enlightenment, has them manifest at any time, manifest to any measure, this is called abiding. If at some time they withdraw, this is called cessation. Toward all of this, one truly knows it. Because such a one abides with right knowledge in this way, this is called blameless abiding, without any craving-savor, the mind free of the defilement of savoring.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a companion sūtra featuring Śāriputra

With this, truly knowing (卯三如實知) closes entirely, closing **abiding (寅三安住3: 支具闕/無怨等/如實知), the third of explaining the marks' three matters, entirely**, closing **explaining the marks (丑二釋相3: 三時/三分/安住) entirely**, closing **abiding (子六住2: 標喻/釋相), the sixth of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, entirely** — a practitioner who truly knows, moment to moment, whether the seven factors are manifest or have withdrawn, abides blamelessly, free of craving-savor toward the attainment itself, exactly like a king who freely and effortlessly draws whatever garment he needs from his chest at any of the day's three times. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this section to a companion sūtra featuring Śāriputra, who reports having attained just this mastery over the seven factors, freely bringing any factor to bear as needed, in any of the three modes (calm-abiding, insight, or both together) and at any of the day's divisions, always aware the moment a factor arises or withdraws.

174. Section: Cultivation Begins — Marking and Listing the Causes and Conditions

Further, cultivating the various factors of enlightenment conjoined with the twenty-one perceptions should be known, briefly, as resting on two causes and conditions: first, the meaning of conjoined co-occurrence; second, the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence.

Chinese Source

復次，二十一種想俱行修諸覺支者，當知略由二因緣故。一、據相應俱行義，二、據無間俱行義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, cultivating the various factors of enlightenment conjoined with the twenty-one perceptions should be known, briefly, as resting on two causes and conditions: first, the meaning of conjoined co-occurrence; second, the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicles 25 and 28, and the 披尋記

This opens cultivation (子七修3), the seventh and final of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, named by the verse's fourth phrase's first half "cultivation" (修居後), beginning with marking and listing the causes and conditions (丑一標列因緣) — twenty-one perceptions, cultivated together with the seven factors of enlightenment, rest on two distinct relationships: conjoined co-occurrence (a noble one's own subsequently-attained wisdom holding all twenty-one perceptions simultaneously in mind, in general contemplation) and uninterrupted co-occurrence (an ordinary being's own preparatory-stage practice, moving through the perceptions one after another without gaps, in a particular, sequential contemplation). Per the 披尋記, the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 28, names ten perceptions countering the ten dharmas opposed to training (impurity, impermanence, impermanence-as-suffering, suffering-as-selfless, food's repulsiveness, the world's unloveliness, light, freedom from desire, cessation, and death); impurity's own perception is further divided, per fascicle 25, into nine sub-perceptions tracing a corpse's decay (discoloration, festering, rupture, bloating, being devoured, blood-smeared, scattered, reduced to bones, and finally empty); and of the remaining nine perceptions, seven (excluding light and death) are gathered under the three concentrations — the wishless conduct's perception (impermanence, impermanence-as-suffering, food's repulsiveness, the world's unloveliness), the empty conduct's perception (suffering-as-selfless), and the signless conduct's perception (freedom from desire and cessation) — yielding, with the ten and the nine-fold division of impurity and the three concentration-perceptions combined, twenty-one perceptions in total.

175. Section: Correlating and Explaining the Distinctions Begins — Resting on the Meaning of Conjoined Co-occurrence

Cultivating conjoined with the perception of impermanence and the rest, up through cultivating conjoined with the perception of death, rests on the meaning of conjoined co-occurrence.

Chinese Source

無常等想俱行修，乃至死想俱行修者，據相應義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Cultivating conjoined with the perception of impermanence and the rest, up through cultivating conjoined with the perception of death, rests on the meaning of conjoined co-occurrence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens correlating and explaining the distinctions (丑二配釋差別2), the second of cultivation's three matters, beginning with resting on the meaning of conjoined co-occurrence (寅一據相應義) — the eleven perceptions (impermanence, impermanence-as-suffering, suffering-as-selfless, observing food, the world's unloveliness, exhaustion, severance, freedom from desire, cessation, fault, and death) held together, generally and simultaneously, with the seven factors of enlightenment belong to this first relationship.

176. Section: Resting on the Meaning of Uninterrupted Co-occurrence Begins — Raising Impurity and the Rest

Cultivating conjoined with the perception of impurity and the rest, up through cultivating conjoined with the perception of observing emptiness, rests on the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence.

Chinese Source

不淨等想俱行修，乃至觀空想俱行修者，據無間義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Cultivating conjoined with the perception of impurity and the rest, up through cultivating conjoined with the perception of observing emptiness, rests on the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens resting on the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence (寅二據無間義2), the second of correlating and explaining the distinctions' two matters, beginning with raising impurity and the rest (卯一舉不淨等) — the ten perceptions tracing a corpse's own sequential decay (impurity, discoloration, festering, bloating, rupture, being devoured, blood-smeared, scattered, reduced to bones, and finally empty), each following the last without interruption, belong to this second relationship, its own particular, sequential contemplation.

177. Section: The Parallel Case of Cultivating Loving-Kindness and the Rest, Closing "Resting on the Meaning of Uninterrupted Co-occurrence" and "Correlating and Explaining the Distinctions" Entirely

Cultivating conjoined with loving-kindness and the rest should be known as likewise so.

Chinese Source

慈等俱行修，應知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

Cultivating conjoined with loving-kindness and the rest should be known as likewise so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

With this, the parallel case of cultivating loving-kindness and the rest (卯二例修慈等) closes entirely, closing resting on the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence (寅二據無間義2: 舉不淨等/例修慈等) entirely, closing **correlating and explaining the distinctions (丑二配釋差別2: 據相應義/據無間義) entirely** — the four immeasurables (loving-kindness bestowing joy, compassion removing suffering, sympathetic joy rejoicing in others' merit, and equanimity guiding beings past craving, aversion, and delusion), cultivated together with the seven factors of enlightenment in their own proper sequence, likewise rest on the meaning of uninterrupted co-occurrence, per the 披尋記, since noble ones cultivate the four immeasurable concentrations conjoined with the factors of enlightenment in just this sequential way.

178. Section: Broadly Clarifying the Remedies Begins — Cultivating Impermanence and the Rest, and Impurity and the Rest Begins — Marking and Listing What Is Remedied
