Further, toward every formation belonging to the past, future, and present, there is craving-defilement toward these formations; there is laziness and indolence; there is, though the view of a real body has already been severed, the manifest activity of conceit still bound by its habitual residue; there is craving for flavor; there is craving desire toward the various excellent matters of the world; there are the remaining latent afflictions; there is the wish to seek gain; there is the wish to seek continued life; there is craving for the desires; there is craving for existence; and there is, following false discrimination, the fourfold arising of desirous craving: first, craving for beautiful color; second, craving for physical form; third, craving for fine touch; fourth, craving to be attended upon. All of these are able to give rise to every unprincipled fault, and to make the mind stray from its proper path. In order to remedy these, in accordance with what is fitting, there are the distinct cultivations of the factors of enlightenment conjoined with the twenty-one perceptions.

Chinese Source

又於過去、未來、現在一切行中，諸行愛染，若懶惰懈怠，若薩迦耶見雖已斷滅習氣隨縛我慢現行，若貪味愛，若於世間種種妙事欲樂貪愛，若有所餘煩惱隨眠，若希求利養，若希求活命，若諸欲愛，若諸有愛，若隨虛妄分別所起四種欲貪。一、美色貪，二、形貌貪，三、細觸貪，四、承事貪。如是能令生起所有非理過患，及令其心越路而轉。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, toward every formation belonging to the past, future, and present, there is craving-defilement toward these formations; there is laziness and indolence; there is, though the view of a real body has already been severed, the manifest activity of conceit still bound by its habitual residue; there is craving for flavor; there is craving desire toward the various excellent matters of the world; there are the remaining latent afflictions; there is the wish to seek gain; there is the wish to seek continued life; there is craving for the desires; there is craving for existence; and there is, following false discrimination, the fourfold arising of desirous craving: first, craving for beautiful color; second, craving for physical form; third, craving for fine touch; fourth, craving to be attended upon. All of these are able to give rise to every unprincipled fault, and to make the mind stray from its proper path.

Chinese Source

對治彼故，隨其所應，有二十一想俱行修覺支差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to remedy these, in accordance with what is fitting, there are the distinct cultivations of the factors of enlightenment conjoined with the twenty-one perceptions.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Ground of Cultivation-Accomplished Wisdom, fascicle 20, the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 28, and the 披尋記

This opens broadly clarifying the remedies (丑三廣明對治2), the third of cultivation's three matters, beginning with cultivating impermanence and the rest, and impurity and the rest (寅一修無常等及不淨等2), beginning with marking and listing what is remedied (卯一標列所治) — eleven distinct afflictions call for remedy: craving-defilement toward the three times' own formations, laziness, self-view's residual conceit (even once the view itself is severed at stream-entry, its habitual trace persists as innate conceit), craving for flavor, craving toward the world's excellent things, the remaining latent afflictions, craving for gain, craving for continued life, craving for the desires, craving for existence, and, rooted in false discrimination, four kinds of desirous craving toward beautiful color, physical form, fine touch, and being attended upon. The twenty-one perceptions, cultivated conjoined with the seven factors of enlightenment, remedy these eleven afflictions in turn, detailed further, per Master Cháng Bǎi, in the Ground of Cultivation-Accomplished Wisdom, fascicle 20, and the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 28's own treatment of the ten dharmas opposed to training and their ten remedying perceptions.

179. Section: Correlating and Explaining the Cultivated Perceptions Begins — Cultivating the Wishless Conduct's Perception

That is, in order to remedy four obstructions, one cultivates the perception of the conduct of wishlessness, from the perception of impermanence up through the perception that nothing in the entire world is delightful.

Chinese Source

謂為對治四種障故，修無願行想，從無常想乃至一切世間不可樂想。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is, in order to remedy four obstructions, one cultivates the perception of the conduct of wishlessness, from the perception of impermanence up through the perception that nothing in the entire world is delightful.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens correlating and explaining the cultivated perceptions (卯二配釋修想6), a sixfold structure, beginning with cultivating the wishless conduct's perception (辰一修無願行想) — four perceptions, gathered under the wishless conduct's own perception, remedy four obstructions in turn: impermanence remedies craving-defilement toward the three times' formations; impermanence-as-suffering remedies laziness; food's repulsiveness remedies craving for flavor; and the world's unloveliness remedies craving toward the world's excellent things — each named individually but together classed, per the 披尋記, as the wishless conduct's perception.

180. Section: Cultivating the Empty Conduct's Perception

In order to remedy one obstruction, one cultivates the perception of the conduct of emptiness, the perception of suffering as selfless.

Chinese Source

為欲對治一種障故，修空行想，苦無我想。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to remedy one obstruction, one cultivates the perception of the conduct of emptiness, the perception of suffering as selfless.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens cultivating the empty conduct's perception (辰二修空行想), the second — the single obstruction of self-view's residual conceit is remedied by the perception of suffering as selfless, reflecting that the five aggregates are utterly without mastery or self-rule, purely conditioned dharmas — this perception classed as the empty conduct's own.

181. Section: Cultivating the Signless Conduct's Perception

In order to sever and extinguish the obstruction of the remaining latent afflictions, one cultivates the perception of the conduct of the signless, toward the three realms.

Chinese Source

為欲斷滅所餘煩惱隨眠障故，修於三界無相行想。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to sever and extinguish the obstruction of the remaining latent afflictions, one cultivates the perception of the conduct of the signless, toward the three realms.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記

This opens cultivating the signless conduct's perception (辰三修無相行想), the third — the remaining latent afflictions, beyond those already addressed, are remedied by observing the sublime, still merit of three realms (severance, the realm where the path of seeing's own afflictions are severed, corresponding to stream-entry; freedom from desire, the realm where the path of cultivation's own afflictions are severed, corresponding to the non-returner; and cessation, the realm of nirvāṇa with remainder, corresponding to the arhat) — cultivated through the perceptions of severance, freedom from desire, and cessation, classed together as the signless conduct's own perception, since only non-discriminating wisdom conjoined with the signless can sever affliction all the way through these three stages.

182. Section: Cultivating the Perception of Fault Among the Various Desires

In order to remedy the wish to seek gain and craving for the desires, one cultivates, toward the various desires, the perception of their fault.

Chinese Source

為欲對治希求利養及欲愛故，於諸欲中修過患想。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to remedy the wish to seek gain and craving for the desires, one cultivates, toward the various desires, the perception of their fault.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens cultivating the perception of fault among the various desires (辰四於諸欲中修過患想), the fourth — the wish to seek gain and craving toward the sense-desires, both belonging to craving for the objects one clings to, are remedied by contemplating the manifold faults inherent in gain, offerings, and the desires themselves.

183. Section: Cultivating the Perception of Death

In order to remedy the wish to seek continued life and craving for existence, one cultivates the perception of death.

Chinese Source

為欲對治希求活命及有愛故，修習死想。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to remedy the wish to seek continued life and craving for existence, one cultivates the perception of death.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens cultivating the perception of death (辰五修習死想), the fifth — the wish to prolong this life or a future one, and craving for existence across the three realms, both belonging to craving for oneself, are remedied by sustained contemplation of death.

184. Section: Cultivating Impurity and the Rest Begins — Setting Out the Distinction

In order to remedy the fourfold desirous craving that follows false discrimination, one cultivates the perception of impurity as the first, up through the perception of observing emptiness as the last.

Chinese Source

為欲對治隨逐虛妄分別所起四欲貪故，修不淨想為初，乃至觀空想為後。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to remedy the fourfold desirous craving that follows false discrimination, one cultivates the perception of impurity as the first, up through the perception of observing emptiness as the last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens cultivating impurity and the rest (辰六修不淨等想2), the sixth and final of correlating and explaining the cultivated perceptions' matters, beginning with setting out the distinction (巳一出差別) — the fourfold craving born of false discrimination (for beautiful color, physical form, fine touch, and being attended upon) is remedied by the nine-part sequence of impurity's own perception, from a corpse's first discoloration through to its final reduction to emptiness. Reading continues past this point to confirm what follows before further closure is claimed.

185. Section: Manifesting What Is Embraced Begins — Briefly Marking

Further, all of this, from the perception of discoloration up through the perception of observing emptiness, one should know is embraced by the perception of impurity.

Chinese Source

又此一切從青瘀想乃至觀空想，當知皆是不淨想攝。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, all of this, from the perception of discoloration up through the perception of observing emptiness, one should know is embraced by the perception of impurity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens manifesting what is embraced (巳二明所攝2), the second of cultivating impurity and the rest's two matters, beginning with briefly marking (午一略標) — the nine sequential perceptions tracing a corpse's decay (discoloration, festering, rupture, bloating, being devoured, blood-smeared, scattered, reduced to bones, and finally empty) are all, together, embraced by the single perception of impurity.

186. Section: Separately Distinguishing, Closing "Manifesting What Is Embraced," "Cultivating Impurity and the Rest," and "Correlating and Explaining the Cultivated Perceptions" Entirely

Further, here, the perception of discoloration as the first, up through the perception of bloating as the last, remedies craving for beautiful color; the perceptions of being devoured, of being reddened, and of being scattered remedy craving for physical form; the perceptions of bare bone and of the skeleton remedy craving for fine touch; and observing the corpse as without consciousness, empty, remedies craving to be attended upon.

Chinese Source

又於此中，青瘀想為初，膨脹想為後，對治美色貪；食噉想、分赤想、分散想，對治形貌貪；骸骨想、骨鎖想，對治細觸貪；觀無心識空有尸想，對治承事貪。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, here, the perception of discoloration as the first, up through the perception of bloating as the last, remedies craving for beautiful color; the perceptions of being devoured, of being reddened, and of being scattered remedy craving for physical form; the perceptions of bare bone and of the skeleton remedy craving for fine touch; and observing the corpse as without consciousness, empty, remedies craving to be attended upon.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, separately distinguishing (午二別辨) closes entirely, closing **manifesting what is embraced (巳二明所攝2: 略標/別辨) entirely**, closing **cultivating impurity and the rest (辰六修不淨等想2: 出差別/明所攝), the sixth and final of correlating and explaining the cultivated perceptions' matters, entirely**, closing **correlating and explaining the cultivated perceptions (卯二配釋修想6: 無願行想/空行想/無相行想/於諸欲中修過患想/修習死想/修不淨等想) entirely** — the nine-part corpse-decay sequence maps precisely onto the four kinds of desirous craving: the first four stages (discoloration through bloating) remedy craving for beautiful color; the next three (devoured, reddened, scattered) remedy craving for physical form; bare bone and skeleton remedy craving for fine touch; and the final perception of the corpse as utterly without consciousness remedies craving to be attended upon.

187. Section: Cultivating Loving-Kindness to Its Utmost, up to the Pervasively Pure Heaven and the Rest, Closing "Cultivating Impermanence and the Rest, and Impurity and the Rest," "Broadly Clarifying the Remedies," "Cultivation," and the Entire Embrace of the Factors of Enlightenment Entirely

Further, here, cultivating loving-kindness reaches, at its utmost, up to the pervasively pure heaven and the rest; this should be known in its marks as in the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation.

Chinese Source

又於此中，修慈最極至遍淨等，如三摩呬多地應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, here, cultivating loving-kindness reaches, at its utmost, up to the pervasively pure heaven and the rest; this should be known in its marks as in the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation, fascicle 12, and a substantial cluster of companion sūtras

With this, cultivating loving-kindness to its utmost, up to the pervasively pure heaven and the rest (寅二修慈最極至遍淨等) closes entirely, closing **cultivating impermanence and the rest, and impurity and the rest (寅一修無常等及不淨等2: 標列所治/配釋修想) entirely**, closing **broadly clarifying the remedies (丑三廣明對治2: 修無常等及不淨等/修慈最極至遍淨等) entirely**, closing **cultivation (子七修3: 標列因緣/配釋差別/廣明對治), the seventh and final of the embrace of the factors of enlightenment's seven sections, entirely** — the four immeasurables reach their own highest expression at four distinct meditative peaks: loving-kindness culminating at the third dhyāna's pervasively pure heaven (the most sublime pleasure within the three realms, per the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation, fascicle 12), compassion at the base of infinite space (emptiness itself removing suffering), sympathetic joy at the base of infinite consciousness (rejoicing being itself a discernment of consciousness), and equanimity at the base of nothingness (relinquishing every affliction, taking nothingness as its very object). Master Cháng Bǎi traces this closing section to a substantial cluster of companion sūtras: young monks urged to draw near elder monks for the deep dharma that perfects the seven factors; the five hindrances as the unwholesome accumulation set against the seven factors as the wholesome; Ānanda corrected that a good spiritual friend is the entirety of the holy life, not merely half; the Buddha's own back pain and his teaching of the seven factors to Ānanda while resting, with a pointed reminder that diligence alone brings unsurpassed enlightenment; the seven factors' own gradual, sequential arising and their fruits (nirvāṇa without remainder, or the fruit of non-return, itself unfolding into seven kinds of parinirvāṇa for the non-returner); and, finally, each of the twenty-one perceptions in turn — impermanence, impermanence-as-suffering, suffering-as-selfless, food's repulsiveness, the world's unloveliness, exhaustion, severance, freedom from desire, cessation, fault, death, impurity, and the nine-part corpse sequence — each shown to bring about great fruit and great benefit on its own. **With this section's closure, the embrace of the factors of enlightenment (壬五覺支攝2: 立/差別/食/漸次/安樂/住/修), the fifth of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, closes in its complete entirety.**

188. Section: The Embrace of the Path-Factors Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing

Further, a summary verse says: first, the inward and outward powers; purity's own distinctions; the alternate approach, the renunciant; the brāhmaṇa, coming last.

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

初內外力　清淨差別　異門沙門　婆羅門後

Ru-Yi Translation

First, the inward and outward powers; purity's own distinctions; the alternate approach, the renunciant; the brāhmaṇa, coming last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens **the embrace of the path-factors (壬六道支攝2), the sixth of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics**, named by the general summary verse's third line's second half "the path-factors" (覺道支息念) — treating the eightfold noble path — beginning with the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列) — four matters unfold in the long-form explanation: the inward and outward powers that give rise to the path, purity's own distinctions among trainee, adept, and mundane or supramundane practice, the alternate approach naming the noble and the deviant path-factors' own distinct terms, and, finally, the true renunciant and true brāhmaṇa the eightfold path alone establishes.

189. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — The Inward and Outward Powers Begins — Marking and Listing the Most Supreme

Among every power, whether inward or outward, in order to give rise to the eight-limbed noble path, there are two powers that are the most supreme relative to every remaining power. What are the two? First, among the outward powers, the power of a good spiritual friend is the most supreme; second, among the inward powers, the power of right reflection is the most supreme.

Chinese Source

若內若外一切力中，為欲生起八支聖道，有二種力，於所餘力最為殊勝。云何為二？一者、於外力中，善知識力最為殊勝；二者、於內力中，正思惟力最為殊勝。

Ru-Yi Translation

Among every power, whether inward or outward, in order to give rise to the eight-limbed noble path, there are two powers that are the most supreme relative to every remaining power. What are the two? First, among the outward powers, the power of a good spiritual friend is the most supreme; second, among the inward powers, the power of right reflection is the most supreme.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋4), a fourfold structure matching the verse's four matters, beginning with the inward and outward powers (子一內外力2), named by the verse's first phrase's second half "the inward and outward powers" (初內外力), beginning with marking and listing the most supreme (丑一標列最勝) — among every power, inward or outward, that could give rise to the eightfold noble path, two stand supreme above all the rest: a good spiritual friend, outwardly, and right reflection, inwardly.

190. Section: Explaining the Distinctions in Sequence Begins — Explaining the Name "the Remaining"

One should know that, here, being free of every obstruction, having previously cultivated meritorious deeds, and being without lack of clothing, food, and the rest — this is called the remaining outward power. Apart from the perception conjoined with right reflection, the remaining limbs of severance are called the remaining inward power.

Chinese Source

當知此中，離諸障礙，先修福業，於衣食等無匱乏等，名餘外力。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, being free of every obstruction, having previously cultivated meritorious deeds, and being without lack of clothing, food, and the rest — this is called the remaining outward power.

Chinese Source

除正思惟相應想外，餘斷支分，名餘內力。

Ru-Yi Translation

Apart from the perception conjoined with right reflection, the remaining limbs of severance are called the remaining inward power.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing the 披尋記 (twice)

This opens explaining the distinctions in sequence (丑二隨釋差別2), the second of the inward and outward powers' two matters, beginning with explaining the name "the remaining" (寅一釋名所餘) — beyond the good spiritual friend, the remaining outward power means freedom from the three obstructions (karmic, afflictive, and karmic-ripening), sustained by merit cultivated in former lives, ensuring adequate clothing, food, medicine, and other requisites, and freedom from illness-producing karma. Beyond right reflection, the remaining inward power means the other limbs of severance — precepts, sense-restraint, moderation in eating, wakeful yoga, right knowledge while abiding, delight in seclusion, purification of the hindrances, and reliance on samādhi — per the 披尋記, all such factors, while genuinely necessary, remain secondary to right reflection's own supreme place among the inward powers.

191. Section: Explaining Its Supremacy, Closing "Explaining the Distinctions in Sequence" and "The Inward and Outward Powers" Entirely

The outward good spiritual friend means that, from them, one hears the unsurpassed true dharma; because of this, it is called hearing the sound from another. Inward right reflection means that, immediately following this, one is able to give rise to right view as the path's own forerunner.

Chinese Source

外善知識者，謂從彼聞無上正法，由此故名從他聞音。內正思惟者，謂此無間，能發正見為上首道。

Ru-Yi Translation

The outward good spiritual friend means that, from them, one hears the unsurpassed true dharma; because of this, it is called hearing the sound from another. Inward right reflection means that, immediately following this, one is able to give rise to right view as the path's own forerunner.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi, citing a substantial cluster of companion sūtras including the chariot-of-the-noble-path verse

With this, explaining its supremacy (寅二釋其最勝) closes entirely, closing **explaining the distinctions in sequence (丑二隨釋差別2: 釋名所餘/釋其最勝) entirely**, closing **the inward and outward powers (子一內外力2: 標列最勝/隨釋差別), the first of the embrace of the path-factors' four sections, entirely** — a good spiritual friend's supremacy lies in being the very source from which the unsurpassed true dharma is first heard at all; right reflection's supremacy lies in being what immediately, without interruption, gives rise to right view, the very forerunner leading every other limb of the eightfold path. Master Cháng Bǎi traces this section to a substantial cluster of companion sūtras: the five hindrances and eight path-factors set as unwholesome against wholesome accumulations; a good spiritual friend as the entirety, not merely half, of the holy life's own success; the noble eightfold path as a vehicle surpassing even the outside brāhmaṇas' own "brahmā vehicle," subduing affliction's every army; and an extended verse likening faith and precepts to a yoke, shame to a long rein, right mindfulness to a skilled charioteer's own guarding hand, equanimity and samādhi to the chariot's shaft, wisdom and diligence to its wheels, and non-attachment and patience to armor protecting the rider from harm — together forming the noble path's own chariot, which alone carries a practitioner past every sorrow to nirvāṇa's own shore, subduing ignorance's whole army of foes; and, finally, a cluster teaching that right or wrong reflection alone determines whether the eight right or eight wrong path-factors arise, persist, or fade, right reflection alone being the deciding factor throughout.

192. Section: Purity's Distinctions Begins — Bringing Forth the Alternative Names Begins — Purified

Further, when right view and the rest are in a trainee, because they are uncontaminated, they are called purified.

Chinese Source

復次，彼正見等，若在有學，由無漏故，說名清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, when right view and the rest are in a trainee, because they are uncontaminated, they are called purified.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens purity's distinctions (子二清淨差別3), the second of the long-form explanation's four matters, beginning with bringing forth the alternative names (丑一出異名4), beginning with purified (寅一清淨) — those at the trainee stage, from the first fruit up to the path toward the fourth, by much cultivation of the eightfold path gain uncontaminated merit and sever the afflictions of craving and view, up to a portion of the innate afflictions. Because uncontaminated wisdom breaks through ignorance, this is called purified. Master Cháng Bǎi cites 攝事分, fascicle 86: relying on that pure path of practice and cultivating it much, the trainee's dharmas break through the shell of ignorance and move toward the stage beyond training.

193. Section: Bright White

When they are in one beyond training, because the continuum is pure, they are called bright white.

Chinese Source

若在無學，相續淨故，說名鮮白。

Ru-Yi Translation

When they are in one beyond training, because the continuum is pure, they are called bright white.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers bright white (寅二鮮白) — for the arhat, the noble one at the stage beyond training, the noble path is already completely cultivated, and the continuum has gained purity through liberation. Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Explanation of the Different Aspects (攝異門分), fascicle 83: bright white means that the continuum is liberated.

194. Section: Without Dust-Spot

When they are in the worldly, because they stay far from the immeasurable evil wrong practices that follow the outsiders' views, they are therefore called without dust-spot.

Chinese Source

若在世間，遠離無量隨外道見諸惡邪行，是故說名無有塵點。

Ru-Yi Translation

When they are in the worldly, because they stay far from the immeasurable evil wrong practices that follow the outsiders' views, they are therefore called without dust-spot.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers without dust-spot (寅三無有塵點) — in the worldly, the practitioner stays far from the many mistaken practices drawn out by the wrong knowledge and wrong views that follow the outsiders and take a self as their root.

195. Section: Free of Secondary Afflictions, Closing "Bringing Forth the Alternative Names" Entirely

Because they stay far from the defilement of the karmas of later existence that arises from those dust-spots, they are therefore called free of secondary afflictions.

Chinese Source

遠離塵點所起後有諸業雜染，是故說名離隨煩惱。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they stay far from the defilement of the karmas of later existence that arises from those dust-spots, they are therefore called free of secondary afflictions.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, free of secondary afflictions (寅四離隨煩惱) closes entirely, closing bringing forth the alternative names (丑一出異名4: 清淨/鮮白/無有塵點/離隨煩惱) entirely — the dust-spots are the evil wrong practices that arise from following the outsiders' views. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that, being without wrong view and wrong practice, one no longer creates the karma that brings a result in the next life, and he takes this as the realm of the arhat.

196. Section: Clarifying What Embraces Them Begins — Marking and Listing

Stated briefly, all the eight noble path-factors are embraced by two places: first, the worldly; second, the transcendent.

Chinese Source

略說一切八聖道支，二處所攝。一者、世間，二、出世間。

Ru-Yi Translation

Stated briefly, all the eight noble path-factors are embraced by two places: first, the worldly; second, the transcendent.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens clarifying what embraces them (丑二明所攝2), the second of purity's distinctions, beginning with marking and listing (寅一標列) — the worldly is the place of beings still bound by the three contaminants and the four graspings; such practitioners, following worldly right view and cultivating precepts, concentration, and wisdom as wholesome dharmas, can go to the fortunate destinies, and they belong to the stages of accumulation and of preparatory practice, with a right view of cause and effect but no fruit yet realized. The transcendent is the practice of the eightfold path that gradually gains pure liberation and ends the suffering of the three realms, from entry into the first fruit up to arhatship.

197. Section: Explaining in Sequence, Closing "Clarifying What Embraces Them" Entirely

The worldly, because it is followed and bound by the three contaminants and the four graspings, is unable to exhaust suffering; because it is wholesome by nature, it is able to go to the fortunate destinies. The transcendent, the opposite of that, is able to exhaust the many sufferings.

Chinese Source

其世間者，三漏、四取所隨縛故，不能盡苦；是善性故，能往善趣。出世間者，與彼相違，能盡眾苦。

Ru-Yi Translation

The worldly, because it is followed and bound by the three contaminants and the four graspings, is unable to exhaust suffering; because it is wholesome by nature, it is able to go to the fortunate destinies. The transcendent, the opposite of that, is able to exhaust the many sufferings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, explaining in sequence (寅二隨釋) closes entirely, closing clarifying what embraces them (丑二明所攝2: 標列/隨釋) entirely. The three contaminants are the contaminants of desire, of existence, and of ignorance; the four graspings are the grasping of desires, of views, of precepts and vows, and of self-assertion. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the seeds of these follow and bind within the ālaya consciousness, so that beings cannot end the suffering result of birth and death; yet if the discriminating mind accords with wholesome dharmas, cultivating the five precepts and the ten wholesome deeds, they will be born in the fortunate destinies of humans and gods.

One who accomplishes the transcendent eightfold path differs from this, severing the seeds of the three contaminants and the four graspings, not returning to the world for birth and death, and having already ended every suffering result of birth and death.

198. Section: Pointing Back to the Earlier Marks, Closing "Purity's Distinctions" Entirely

Further, the meaning of right view and the rest, the eight noble path-factors, distinguished at length, should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground and the Explanation of the Different Aspects. The seven requisites of concentration are as already said in the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation.

Chinese Source

又正見等八聖道支廣分別義，如聲聞地及攝異門分應知其相。七種定具，如三摩呬多地已說。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the meaning of right view and the rest, the eight noble path-factors, distinguished at length, should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground and the Explanation of the Different Aspects. The seven requisites of concentration are as already said in the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, pointing back to the earlier marks (丑三指前相) closes entirely, closing purity's distinctions (子二清淨差別3: 出異名/明所攝/指前相) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi locates the detail in the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 29, and the Explanation of the Different Aspects, fascicle 83. The seven requisites of concentration (七種定具) are the seven path-factors other than right concentration, per the Yogācārabhūmi Commentary (瑜伽論記).

Per the 披尋記, the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation, fascicle 12, speaks of noble right samādhi with its causes and requisites: five path-factors, right view, right reflection, right speech, right action, and right livelihood, are its causes, by the meaning of leading the way in sequence; three, right view, right effort, and right mindfulness, are its requisites, by the meaning of assisting samādhi. The Blessed One speaks of the first seven path-factors together as its causes and requisites, and so here they are called in general the requisites of concentration. Master Cháng Bǎi adds that the five are distant causes and the three are near conditions.

The companion sūtras are nos. 1 to 21 of the Path Section (聖道分相應) in the 雜中. As the sun's first light comes before the sun, right view comes before the rest of the path; with ignorance going before, the eight wrong path-factors arise, and with illumination going before, the eight right ones. No. 14 gives the difference between the trainee's eightfold path and that of one beyond training, matching this section's purified and bright white, and no. 15 says the same in the terms true person (正士), meaning a trainee, and great person (大士), meaning one beyond training.

199. Section: The Alternate Approach Begins — The Embrace of the Noble Path-Factors Begins — Called Dharma

Further, because the eight noble path-factors headed by right view accord with right principle, they are called dharma.

Chinese Source

復次，正見為首八聖道支，會正理故，說名為法。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, because the eight noble path-factors headed by right view accord with right principle, they are called dharma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the alternate approach (子三異門2), the third of the long-form explanation's four matters, named by the verse's third phrase "the alternate approach, the renunciant" (異門沙門), which explains the eightfold noble path from different angles, beginning with the embrace of the noble path-factors (丑一聖道支攝6), beginning with called dharma (寅一名法) — the path-factors enter the principle of the noble ones, the realized principle that there is only dharma, with no person and no self.

200. Section: Called Discipline

Because they are able to extinguish every affliction, they are called discipline.

Chinese Source

能滅一切諸煩惱故，名毗奈耶。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are able to extinguish every affliction, they are called discipline.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called discipline (寅二名毗奈耶) — the eightfold path accords with subduing and extinguishing every affliction of craving and view. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that vinaya (毗奈耶) means subduing, is rendered as precept, and is also called extinguishing.

201. Section: Called Noble

Because they are extremely far removed from all evil dharmas, and because all the noble and the worthy alike take them as their pattern and practice them, they are called noble.

Chinese Source

去諸惡法極懸遠故，一切聖賢共祖習故，說名為聖。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are extremely far removed from all evil dharmas, and because all the noble and the worthy alike take them as their pattern and practice them, they are called noble.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called noble (寅三名聖) — the noble path severs all evil, unwholesome dharmas and stays far from foolishness and lack of wisdom, and it is the right path that all noble ones and worthy ones together follow and train in.

202. Section: Called Wholesome

Because they are able to accord with and go to the fortunate destinies, they are called wholesome.

Chinese Source

能隨順往諸善趣故，說名為善。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are able to accord with and go to the fortunate destinies, they are called wholesome.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called wholesome (寅四名善) — Master Cháng Bǎi takes this of the worldly noble path, which moves toward the fortunate destinies of humans and gods and brings about a desirable result.

203. Section: Called What Should Be Cultivated

Because they move toward nirvāṇa, they are called what should be cultivated.

Chinese Source

趣涅槃故，說名應修。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they move toward nirvāṇa, they are called what should be cultivated.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called what should be cultivated (寅五名應修) — Master Cháng Bǎi takes this of the transcendent noble path, which extinguishes affliction and birth and death and moves toward nirvāṇa.

204. Section: Called Excellent, Closing "The Embrace of the Noble Path-Factors" Entirely

Because they are praised by all who are wise, they are called excellent.

Chinese Source

諸有智者所稱讚故，說名善哉。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are praised by all who are wise, they are called excellent.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, called excellent (寅六名善哉) closes entirely, closing the embrace of the noble path-factors (丑一聖道支攝6: 名法/名毗奈耶/名聖/名善/名應修/名善哉) entirely — the wise together commend and praise the noble path.

205. Section: The Embrace of the Wrong Path-Factors Begins — Marking the Opposite

The opposite of this, one should know, is precisely the distinctions among the eight wrong path-factors headed by wrong view.

Chinese Source

與此相違，應知即是邪見為首八邪道支所有差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

The opposite of this, one should know, is precisely the distinctions among the eight wrong path-factors headed by wrong view.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the embrace of the wrong path-factors (丑二邪道支攝2), the second of the alternate approach, beginning with marking the opposite (寅一標相違) — what runs contrary to the eightfold noble path is the eight wrong path-factors headed by wrong view, with their own distinct names and marks.

206. Section: Explaining the Distinctions Begins — Called Dark

Because they fall within the class of ignorance and darkness, they are called dark.

Chinese Source

墮在無明黑闇品故，說名為黑。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they fall within the class of ignorance and darkness, they are called dark.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining the distinctions (寅二釋差別5), beginning with called dark (卯一名黑) — the wrong path causes one to fall within the class that is without wisdom and does not rightly know the facts. Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Explanation of the Different Aspects, fascicle 84: ignorance is lack of skill in the matters to be known, and darkness is not rightly knowing real matters.

207. Section: Called Without Benefit

Because they go to the unfortunate destinies, they are called without benefit.

Chinese Source

往惡趣故，說名無義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they go to the unfortunate destinies, they are called without benefit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called without benefit (卯二名無義) — the wrong path draws one toward the three unfortunate destinies of the hells, hungry ghosts, and animals, where there is only suffering and no pleasure, and it is called without benefit because it draws out suffering.

208. Section: Called Inferior

Because they are unwholesome by nature, they are called inferior.

Chinese Source

不善性故，說名下劣。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are unwholesome by nature, they are called inferior.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called inferior (卯三名下劣) — by nature the wrong path is not wholesome, and it gives rise to shamelessness, lack of conscience, and the unwholesome roots such as craving.

209. Section: Called Blameworthy

Because they give rise, in this present life, to every fear and enmity, they are called blameworthy.

Chinese Source

生現法中所有怖畏及怨憎故，說名有罪。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they give rise, in this present life, to every fear and enmity, they are called blameworthy.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called blameworthy (卯四名有罪) — by nature the wrong path is defiled, and within this present life it gives rise to all kinds of fear and to hatred.

210. Section: Called What Should Be Kept Far From, Closing "Explaining the Distinctions," "The Embrace of the Wrong Path-Factors," and "The Alternate Approach" Entirely

Because they are reproached by all who are wise, and because the wise keep far from them, they are called what should be kept far from.

Chinese Source

諸有智者所譏毀故，所遠離故，名應遠離。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are reproached by all who are wise, and because the wise keep far from them, they are called what should be kept far from.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, called what should be kept far from (卯五名應遠離) closes entirely, closing explaining the distinctions (寅二釋差別5: 名黑/名無義/名下劣/名有罪/名應遠離) entirely, closing the embrace of the wrong path-factors (丑二邪道支攝2: 標相違/釋差別) entirely, closing the alternate approach (子三異門2: 聖道支攝/邪道支攝) entirely.

Master Cháng Bǎi gives the companion sūtras as nos. 52 to 62 of the Path Section in the 雜中. Those eleven sūtras explain the eleven names set out here: dharma, discipline, noble, wholesome, what should be cultivated, and excellent for the noble path-factors; and dark, without benefit, inferior, blameworthy, and what should be kept far from for the wrong path-factors. The first of them teaches that the eight right path-factors are dharma and the eight wrong ones are not dharma.

211. Section: The Renunciant and the Brāhmaṇa Begins — Citing the Renunciant Begins — Briefly Showing the Distinctions Begins — Bringing Forth the Establishment Begins — The Meaning of a Renunciant

Further, according to all renunciants in the foremost meaning, these eight noble path-factors are established as the meaning of a renunciant.

Chinese Source

復次，依第一義所有沙門，安立如是八支聖道為沙門義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, according to all renunciants in the foremost meaning, these eight noble path-factors are established as the meaning of a renunciant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the renunciant and the brāhmaṇa (子四沙門婆羅門2), the fourth of the long-form explanation's four matters, named by the verse's third and fourth phrases, beginning with citing the renunciant (丑一舉沙門2), beginning with briefly showing the distinctions (寅一略顯差別3), beginning with bringing forth the establishment (卯一出安立2), beginning with the meaning of a renunciant (辰一沙門義). Master Cháng Bǎi takes the key to be the phrase "all renunciants in the foremost meaning," for two reasons. First, it excludes outsider renunciants, since the word renunciant, a general term for those gone forth, can cover both inner and outer paths. Second, it does not contradict the companion sūtra, no. 98 of the Path Section in the 雜中, which distinguishes the renunciant's dharma, the eight noble path-factors, from the meaning of a renunciant, the permanent exhaustion of craving, aversion, delusion, and every affliction.

Meaning (義) here is principle or realm, and can also be the benefit gained by the noble path. Going forth, the renunciant trains in the eightfold path and by it moves toward the foremost meaning; of renunciants in the foremost meaning, it can further be said that they have already realized the uncontaminated eight noble path-factors, which is spoken of from the side of severance and of the fruit. Among such renunciants there are degrees from the first fruit to the fourth, and for this reason the text says "all."

212. Section: The Nature of a Renunciant, Closing "Bringing Forth the Establishment" Entirely

For the sake of this meaning, within the well-taught dharma and discipline, one goes forth in provisional name and takes on the nature of a renunciant.

Chinese Source

為此義故，於善說法毗奈耶中，假名出家受沙門性。

Ru-Yi Translation

For the sake of this meaning, within the well-taught dharma and discipline, one goes forth in provisional name and takes on the nature of a renunciant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the nature of a renunciant (辰二沙門性) closes entirely, closing bringing forth the establishment (卯一出安立2: 沙門義/沙門性) entirely. Per the 披尋記, this meaning is the wish to realize the meaning of the renunciant's fruit. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one comes to the Buddha's dharma and discipline to go forth and practice for that purpose; though not yet having personally realized the foremost meaning, one shaves head and beard, stops evil, tames body and mind, and diligently practices the wholesome, and so is provisionally said to go forth and take on the nature of a renunciant.

213. Section: Distinguishing Superior and Inferior

Further, because this is finally without loss or ruin, it is called the foremost meaning. The one in provisional name is not like this.

Chinese Source

又此畢竟無失壞故，名第一義。其假名者即不如是。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, because this is finally without loss or ruin, it is called the foremost meaning. The one in provisional name is not like this.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers distinguishing superior and inferior (卯二辨勝劣) — one who accomplishes the meaning of a renunciant has no fault and does not ruin the merit of the noble path, and so is called a renunciant in the foremost meaning. One who has gone forth but has not realized the noble path is provisionally established as a renunciant, and is only that in name.

214. Section: Clarifying the Ultimate Meaning Begins — Marking the Obtaining of the Name

Those who have accomplished this nature of a renunciant in the foremost meaning, one should know, are also called renunciants in the ultimate meaning.

Chinese Source

諸有成就此第一義沙門性者，當知亦名勝義沙門。

Ru-Yi Translation

Those who have accomplished this nature of a renunciant in the foremost meaning, one should know, are also called renunciants in the ultimate meaning.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens clarifying the ultimate meaning (卯三明勝義2), the third of briefly showing the distinctions, beginning with marking the obtaining of the name (辰一標得名) — those who have accomplished the noble path bear the name renunciant in the ultimate meaning (勝義沙門).

215. Section: Showing Permanent Severance Begins — The General Marking

Further, they pursue this fruit of a renunciant, the matter of the final severance of craving, aversion, delusion, and the rest; therefore that is called the meaning of a renunciant.

Chinese Source

又彼追求此沙門果貪瞋癡等畢竟斷義，是故說彼名沙門義。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, they pursue this fruit of a renunciant, the matter of the final severance of craving, aversion, delusion, and the rest; therefore that is called the meaning of a renunciant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens showing permanent severance (辰二顯永斷2), beginning with the general marking (巳一總標) — practitioners pursue the four fruits of a renunciant and finally sever craving, aversion, delusion, and the other afflictions, and for this reason the fruit too can be called the meaning of a renunciant. Master Cháng Bǎi concludes that the noble path and its fruit both belong to the realm of the renunciant, and both can be called the meaning of a renunciant.

216. Section: Separately Expanding, Closing "Showing Permanent Severance," "Clarifying the Ultimate Meaning," and "Briefly Showing the Distinctions" Entirely

This meaning of a renunciant is further of two kinds: first, the establishment by general mark, without distinction; second, the establishment by distinction, of those with something to do and those with nothing to do, of those approaching and those abiding in a fruit.

Chinese Source

此沙門義復有二種。一、無差別總相建立；二、若有所作、若無所作，行向、住果差別建立。

Ru-Yi Translation

This meaning of a renunciant is further of two kinds: first, the establishment by general mark, without distinction; second, the establishment by distinction, of those with something to do and those with nothing to do, of those approaching and those abiding in a fruit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, separately expanding (巳二別廣) closes entirely, closing showing permanent severance (辰二顯永斷2: 總標/別廣) entirely, closing clarifying the ultimate meaning (卯三明勝義2: 標得名/顯永斷) entirely, closing briefly showing the distinctions (寅一略顯差別3: 出安立/辨勝劣/明勝義) entirely.

Per the 披尋記, not distinguishing those approaching from those abiding in a fruit is called the establishment by general mark, without distinction. One who moves along the path toward a fruit is called one approaching, and by this four kinds of persons are established; these should be known as having something to do. One who has attained a fruit of a renunciant is called one abiding in a fruit, and by the fruit of the path four kinds of persons are established; these should be known as having nothing to do. This is the second, the establishment by distinction. Master Cháng Bǎi adds that nothing to do is said relative to those approaching.

217. Section: Concluding by Embracing All of It, Closing "Citing the Renunciant" Entirely

All of this comes to four kinds in total: first, the nature of a renunciant; second, being a renunciant; third, the meaning of a renunciant; fourth, the fruit of a renunciant.

Chinese Source

如是一切總有四種。一、沙門性，二、是沙門，三、沙門義，四、沙門果。

Ru-Yi Translation

All of this comes to four kinds in total: first, the nature of a renunciant; second, being a renunciant; third, the meaning of a renunciant; fourth, the fruit of a renunciant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding by embracing all of it (寅二結攝一切) closes entirely, closing citing the renunciant (丑一舉沙門2: 略顯差別/結攝一切) entirely. The nature of a renunciant is that of one who goes forth seeking the noble path, knowing that going forth means severing affliction. Being a renunciant refers to the kinds of those gone forth, which Master Cháng Bǎi takes from the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 29: the renunciant of superior attainment (勝道沙門), the well-gone who sever craving, aversion, and delusion; the renunciant who teaches the path (說道沙門); the renunciant who lives by the path (活道沙門), who diligently practices the wholesome and is able to nourish the life of wisdom; and the renunciant who corrupts the path (壞道沙門), who practices wrongly and defiles the noble path.

The meaning of a renunciant can be taken in two ways: the uncontaminated eight noble path-factors, or the fruit of a renunciant, the latter established either without distinction or by distinction as in the previous section. The fruit of a renunciant is the first through the fourth fruit.

218. Section: Extending the Case to the Brāhmaṇa, Closing "The Renunciant and the Brāhmaṇa," "The Long-Form Explanation," and the Entire Embrace of the Path-Factors Entirely

The principle of distinctions for the brāhmaṇa, one should know, is likewise so.

Chinese Source

其婆羅門差別道理，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

The principle of distinctions for the brāhmaṇa, one should know, is likewise so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, extending the case to the brāhmaṇa (丑二例婆羅門) closes entirely, closing the renunciant and the brāhmaṇa (子四沙門婆羅門2: 舉沙門/例婆羅門) entirely, closing the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋4: 內外力/清淨差別/異門/沙門婆羅門) entirely, closing the embrace of the path-factors (壬六道支攝2: 頌標列/長行釋), the sixth of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the brāhmaṇa, the highest caste in India, is here another name for the renunciant, and one who accomplishes the foremost truth can be called a brāhmaṇa in the ultimate meaning.

By the arrangement of the 雜中, the companion sūtras are nos. 76 to 114 of the Path Section. Those turned toward wrongness go against the dharma, and their thought, wish, vow, and action all follow wrong view; those turned toward rightness delight in the dharma. No. 78 adds the simile of bitter seeds and sweet seeds: however earth, water, fire, and wind nourish them, each yields the taste of its own seed. No. 82 teaches two kinds of right view: the worldly, contaminated, with grasping, turned toward the fortunate destinies; and the noble, transcendent, and uncontaminated, which reflects on the four noble truths and turns back against the stream of birth and death. Nos. 83 to 89 apply the same to the remaining path-factors, right intention (正志) through right concentration.

Nos. 90 and 91 set out the wrong and the wrong path, the right and the right path, and can be matched to the embrace of the noble and wrong path-factors. Nos. 97 to 102 distinguish the renunciant's dharma, the eight noble path-factors; the renunciant, the one accomplishing them; the meaning of a renunciant, the permanent severance of craving, aversion, delusion, and every affliction; and the fruit of a renunciant, from stream-entry to arhatship. Nos. 103 to 108 say the same of the brāhmaṇa, and nos. 109 to 114 of the holy life, matching this section.

219. Section: The Embrace of Mindfulness of Breathing Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the embrace of mindfulness of breathing (壬七息念攝2), the seventh of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, named by the general summary verse's third line "mindfulness of breathing" (覺道支息念), which explains the method of cultivating the in-breath and out-breath, beginning with the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列).

220. Section: The Verse's Eight Matters, Closing "The Verse Marking and Listing" Entirely

Obstructions, secondary afflictions, investigation and the rest; result, desire, the subtle, bodily weariness; the trainee's abiding and attention; wisdom and non-grasping coming last.

Chinese Source

障隨惑尋等　果欲細身勞　學住及作意　智無執為後

Ru-Yi Translation

Obstructions, secondary afflictions, investigation and the rest; result, desire, the subtle, bodily weariness; the trainee's abiding and attention; wisdom and non-grasping coming last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列) closes entirely — eight matters unfold in the long-form explanation. First, obstructions, secondary afflictions, and investigation and the rest: what obstructs the cultivation of the in-breath and out-breath, and the afflictions born of following investigation and reflection. Second, result: what the cultivation gains. Third, desire: the wholesome desire for it. Fourth, the subtle: the subtlety of mindfulness of breathing and the rest. Fifth, bodily weariness: that it keeps the body from weariness, up to abiding in ease. Sixth, the trainee's abiding: the difference between the trainee and the Tathāgata in severing the five hindrances. Seventh, attention: the difference between principled and unprincipled attention. Eighth, wisdom and non-grasping: that the practice realizes the wisdom of no-self and no longer grasps a self.

221. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — Obstructions, Secondary Afflictions, Investigation and the Rest Begins — Pointing to the Marks of Cultivation

The differences in cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath comprise sixteen modes; their meaning, distinguished at length, should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

入出息念修習差別有十六行，廣分別義，如聲聞地應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

The differences in cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath comprise sixteen modes; their meaning, distinguished at length, should be known in its marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋8), an eightfold structure matching the verse's eight matters, beginning with obstructions, secondary afflictions, investigation and the rest (子一障隨惑尋等3), beginning with pointing to the marks of cultivation (丑一指修相). Per the 披尋記, the Śrāvaka Ground says: what are the sixteen excellent practices (十六勝行)? They begin, in mindfulness of the in-breath, with "I now am able to train in mindfulness of the in-breath," and so on at length. Master Cháng Bǎi locates this in fascicle 27: the foundations of body, feeling, mind, and dharmas each have four practices, sixteen in all, and they can be matched to no. 3 of the Mindfulness of Breathing Section (安那般那念相應) in the 雜中.

222. Section: Bringing Forth the Obstructing Dharmas Begins — Marking

Further, diligent practitioners of yoga, when cultivating such mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath, should at that time know five obstructing dharmas.

Chinese Source

又勤修行諸瑜伽師，修習如是入出息念，爾時應知五障礙法。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, diligent practitioners of yoga, when cultivating such mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath, should at that time know five obstructing dharmas.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens bringing forth the obstructing dharmas (丑二出障法3), beginning with marking (寅一標) — five dharmas can appear that obstruct this cultivation.

223. Section: Listing

First, the mind is scattered toward outer conditions. Second, the turning of the in-breath and out-breath meets with difficulty. Third, the active entanglement of restlessness and regret appears. Fourth, the active entanglement of torpor and sleepiness appears. Fifth, one delights in living mixed together with monastics and laypeople.

Chinese Source

一者、於其外緣，其心散亂；二者、入出息轉有所艱難；三者、掉舉惡作纏現在前；四者、惛沈睡眠纏現在前；五者、樂與道俗共相雜住。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, the mind is scattered toward outer conditions. Second, the turning of the in-breath and out-breath meets with difficulty. Third, the active entanglement of restlessness and regret appears. Fourth, the active entanglement of torpor and sleepiness appears. Fifth, one delights in living mixed together with monastics and laypeople.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers listing (寅二列). The mind clings to outer conditions, such as forms, sounds, smells, tastes, touches, men and women. The breath does not flow smoothly. Restlessness, per Master Cháng Bǎi, is recalling the pleasant things of past and future, and regret is remorse over what one has already done, whether wholesome or evil. Torpor and sleepiness come from eating too much or eating what is not suitable, or from fatigue. Living mixed together with monastics and laypeople of different outlook keeps one from seclusion and quiet.

224. Section: Concluding, Closing "Bringing Forth the Obstructing Dharmas" Entirely

One should know that all five of these dharmas are able to act as obstructions, for one who has not attained concentration and seeks to settle the mind, and, after concentration is attained, redoubled and increased.

Chinese Source

如是五法，於未得定欲求心定，及得定已倍復增長，當知一切能為障礙。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that all five of these dharmas are able to act as obstructions, for one who has not attained concentration and seeks to settle the mind, and, after concentration is attained, redoubled and increased.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding (寅三結) closes entirely, closing bringing forth the obstructing dharmas (丑二出障法3: 標/列/結) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that for one seeking concentration these five are obstructions in every way, and for one who has attained it, if concentration is lost because of them, they are an obstruction redoubled.

225. Section: Distinguishing the Remedies Begins — In the Class of Calm-Abiding

When one is defiled by the secondary afflictions of the class of calm-abiding, torpor arises in the body and feebleness arises in the mind. By rightly cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath, body and mind become pliant, and one is able to keep far from all the gross heaviness of body and mind conjoined with torpor and feebleness.

Chinese Source

奢摩他品諸隨煩惱所染汙時，發身惛沈，生心下劣。由正修習入出息念，身心輕安，能令惛沈下劣俱行身心麤重皆悉遠離。

Ru-Yi Translation

When one is defiled by the secondary afflictions of the class of calm-abiding, torpor arises in the body and feebleness arises in the mind. By rightly cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath, body and mind become pliant, and one is able to keep far from all the gross heaviness of body and mind conjoined with torpor and feebleness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens distinguishing the remedies (丑三辨對治2), beginning with the class of calm-abiding (寅一於奢摩他品) — calm-abiding is the method of recollecting one object again and again. When secondary afflictions defile the mind during this practice, body and mind grow dim, the object is not clear, and the mind is feeble and without capacity. Only by rightly cultivating this mindfulness until body and mind reach pliancy does the gross heaviness conjoined with torpor and feebleness depart.

226. Section: In the Class of Insight Begins — Bringing Forth What Is Remedied

When one is defiled by the secondary afflictions of the class of insight, various false thoughts of investigation and reflection arise: namely, improper investigation and reflection, such as that of desire, and the various false thoughts, such as perceptions of desire, that arise from investigation and reflection on the side of ignorance.

Chinese Source

毗鉢舍那品諸隨煩惱所染汙時，發生種種尋伺妄想，謂欲尋伺等不正尋伺，及無明分尋伺所起諸欲想等種種妄想。

Ru-Yi Translation

When one is defiled by the secondary afflictions of the class of insight, various false thoughts of investigation and reflection arise: namely, improper investigation and reflection, such as that of desire, and the various false thoughts, such as perceptions of desire, that arise from investigation and reflection on the side of ignorance.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the class of insight (寅二於毗鉢舍那品2), beginning with bringing forth what is remedied (卯一出所治). Per the 披尋記, the words "such as" in "improper investigation and reflection, such as that of desire" take in investigation and reflection of aversion and of harm. Master Cháng Bǎi reckons eight kinds of improper thought in all, adding those concerned with one's land, one's kin, not dying, family standing, and contempt for others. Investigation and reflection on the side of ignorance, he explains, do not understand impermanence, suffering, emptiness, and no-self, and grasp a self and what is real.

227. Section: Clarifying What Remedies Begins — Causing Stillness

By rightly cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath, investigation, reflection, and the rest are all stilled.

Chinese Source

由正修習入出息念，令尋伺等悉皆靜息。

Ru-Yi Translation

By rightly cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath, investigation, reflection, and the rest are all stilled.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens clarifying what remedies (卯二明能治2), beginning with causing stillness (辰一令靜息) — the eight kinds of false thought become quiet and do not arise in active manifestation.

228. Section: Causing Completion, Closing "Clarifying What Remedies," "In the Class of Insight," "Distinguishing the Remedies," and "Obstructions, Secondary Afflictions, Investigation and the Rest" Entirely

In order to remedy those false thoughts on the side of ignorance, one purely cultivates the perception on the side of illumination, causing it swiftly to reach completion.

Chinese Source

為欲對治彼無明分諸妄想故，純修明分想，令速得圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

In order to remedy those false thoughts on the side of ignorance, one purely cultivates the perception on the side of illumination, causing it swiftly to reach completion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, causing completion (辰二令圓滿) closes entirely, closing clarifying what remedies (卯二明能治2: 令靜息/令圓滿) entirely, closing the class of insight (寅二於毗鉢舍那品2: 出所治/明能治) entirely, closing distinguishing the remedies (丑三辨對治2: 於奢摩他品/於毗鉢舍那品) entirely, closing obstructions, secondary afflictions, investigation and the rest (子一障隨惑尋等3: 指修相/出障法/辨對治), the first of the long-form explanation's eight matters, entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the side of illumination (明分) refers especially to the light of the dharma, the wisdom conjoined with the real meaning of the emptiness of self; much cultivation of this contemplation brings the perception to completion, up to severing the seeds of ignorance.

A companion sūtra gives five preparatory practices that benefit mindfulness of the breath: abiding in pure precepts, having few desires and few affairs, moderation in food, not clinging to sleep in the first and last watches of the night, and staying in quiet places. Another teaches that this mindfulness brings stillness of body and stillness of mind, and that the practitioner comes to the completion of the perception conjoined with principled attention, up to the fourth fruit.

A third, which sets out the practice in full, has the monk beg with senses guarded, then sit upright in the forest, under a tree, or in the open with mindfulness held on the object, sever craving for worldly objects, leave behind the five hindrances, which weaken wisdom, and take up the sixteen excellent practices. Master Cháng Bǎi explains each practice against the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 27: knowing the breath as long and short; the breath pervading the body; removing the bodily formation; experiencing joy; experiencing bliss; experiencing the mental formation; removing the mental formation; experiencing the mind; gladdening the mind; restraining the mind; liberating the mind; and contemplating impermanence, severance, freedom from desire, and cessation.

229. Section: Result Begins — Keeping Equipoise Free of Gaps and Lacks

Further, for practitioners of yoga rightly and diligently cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath: the investigation and reflection taking past formations as object, which are able to cause gaps and lacks in the equipoise arising without interval, are swiftly diminished; the investigation and reflection taking future formations as object, which are able to cause gaps and lacks in the equipoise arising without interval, are swiftly stopped; the investigation and reflection taking present formations as object, which are able to cause gaps and lacks in the equipoise arising without interval, are swiftly quieted.

Chinese Source

復次，正勤修習入出息念諸瑜伽師，於緣過去諸行尋伺，能令無間所生等持有間闕者，速得損減；於緣未來諸行尋伺，能令無間所生等持有間闕者，速得止息；於緣現在諸行尋伺，能令無間所生等持有間闕者，速得寂靜。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, for practitioners of yoga rightly and diligently cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath: the investigation and reflection taking past formations as object, which are able to cause gaps and lacks in the equipoise arising without interval, are swiftly diminished; the investigation and reflection taking future formations as object, which are able to cause gaps and lacks in the equipoise arising without interval, are swiftly stopped; the investigation and reflection taking present formations as object, which are able to cause gaps and lacks in the equipoise arising without interval, are swiftly quieted.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens result (子二果2), the second of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's second phrase "result, desire, the subtle, bodily weariness" (果欲細身勞), beginning with keeping equipoise free of gaps and lacks (丑一能令等持無有間闕). Master Cháng Bǎi recalls fascicle 27: this object is the remedy for those whose practice is full of discursive thought. Equipoise is the even holding of the mind, each thought level with the last and continuing unbroken, which is called without interval; investigation and reflection make gaps and lacks in it.

Thoughts taking the past, the future, and the present five aggregates as object are thus diminished, stopped, and quieted by this practice, and the practitioner quickly gains worldly concentration, the preliminary stage, up to the third dhyāna.

230. Section: Severing the Six Fetters and Gaining the Fruit-Benefits Begins — Marking the Sūtras' Words

Further, in brief, one should know that, by permanently severing six kinds of fetters, two kinds, four kinds, and seven kinds of fruit-benefits are established, as the sūtras explain at length.

Chinese Source

又若略說，由能永斷六種結故，當知建立二種、四種及以七種諸果勝利。如經廣說。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, in brief, one should know that, by permanently severing six kinds of fetters, two kinds, four kinds, and seven kinds of fruit-benefits are established, as the sūtras explain at length.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens severing the six fetters and gaining the fruit-benefits (丑二能斷六結得諸果利3), beginning with marking the sūtras' words (寅一標經說) — relying on this mindfulness to attain concentration, and within concentration cultivating insight into impermanence, suffering, emptiness, no-self, and the four noble truths, one permanently severs six kinds of affliction. Master Cháng Bǎi sums up the two results of this practice: equipoise without gaps or lacks, and the benefits of the noble fruits from the first fruit up to arhatship.

231. Section: Explaining the Six Fetters

What are the six fetters? They are the two fetters according with the lower portion and the upper portion, the two fetters severed by the path of seeing and by the path of cultivation, and the two fetters of the states of arising and of being born.

Chinese Source

云何六結？謂順下分、上分二結，見道、修道所斷二結，若起、若生二分位結。

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the six fetters? They are the two fetters according with the lower portion and the upper portion, the two fetters severed by the path of seeing and by the path of cultivation, and the two fetters of the states of arising and of being born.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers explaining the six fetters (寅二釋六結). The fetters according with the lower portion belong to the desire realm: the view of a real body, the grasping of precepts and vows, doubt, craving, and aversion. Those according with the upper portion belong to the form and formless realms: craving for form, craving for the formless, restlessness, conceit, and ignorance. Severed by the path of seeing are the 112 afflictions of view, the discriminated view of self; severed by the path of cultivation are the sixteen innate afflictions, whose complete severance realizes arhatship.

The two states, per the Yogācārabhūmi Commentary (瑜伽論記) as Master Cháng Bǎi follows it here, concern the non-returner before nirvāṇa: all affliction severed in the intermediate existence is called arising, and all affliction severed after taking birth is called being born.

232. Section: Correlating the Establishment, Closing "Severing the Six Fetters and Gaining the Fruit-Benefits" and "Result" Entirely

Thus, one should know, taken each apart, six kinds of fetters are stated in all; in their respective order, two kinds, four kinds, and seven kinds of fruit-benefits are established.

Chinese Source

如是別別當知總說有六種結；如其次第，建立二種、四種、七種諸果勝利。

Ru-Yi Translation

Thus, one should know, taken each apart, six kinds of fetters are stated in all; in their respective order, two kinds, four kinds, and seven kinds of fruit-benefits are established.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, correlating the establishment (寅三配建立) closes entirely, closing severing the six fetters and gaining the fruit-benefits (丑二能斷六結得諸果利3: 標經說/釋六結/配建立) entirely, closing result (子二果2: 能令等持無有間闕/能斷六結得諸果利) entirely. Per the 披尋記, permanently severing the lower and upper fetters establishes two fruits, the non-returner and the arhat. Permanently severing the two fetters of seeing and cultivation establishes four fruits, from stream-entry to arhat. Permanently severing the two fetters of the states of arising and being born establishes seven fruits: stream-entry, once-return, and the five kinds of non-returner, those attaining final nirvāṇa in the intermediate existence (中般涅槃), on being born (生般涅槃), without effort (無行般涅槃), with effort (有行般涅槃), and those going upstream (上流般涅槃), as distinguished in the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 26.

Master Cháng Bǎi notes that the 披尋記 differs here from the Yogācārabhūmi Commentary (瑜伽論記), which counts the seven as the three kinds of attainment in the intermediate existence, swift, not swift, and long-delayed, together with the remaining four kinds of non-returner. The Śrāvaka Ground compares the three to a spark that goes out as soon as it appears, one that goes out while flying up from the struck iron, and one that goes out as it falls, before touching the ground.

The companion sūtras are nos. 4 to 11 of the Mindfulness of Breathing Section in the 雜中. No. 4 teaches that this mindfulness severs thoughts and false discriminations, leaving equipoise without gaps. Nos. 5 to 11 replace this with not being shaken, great fruit, great benefit, the ultimate nectar, which is nirvāṇa, two fruits, four fruits, and seven fruits, respectively.

233. Section: Desire Begins — Marking and Listing the Two Kinds

Further, the differences in cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath are, briefly, of two kinds: first, surpassable; second, unsurpassed.

Chinese Source

復次，入出息念修習差別，略有二種。一者、有上，二者、無上。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the differences in cultivating mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath are, briefly, of two kinds: first, surpassable; second, unsurpassed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens desire (子三欲2), the third of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's second phrase, the wholesome desire for this practice, beginning with marking and listing the two kinds (丑一標列二種) — cultivating worldly concentration on the basis of the in-breath and out-breath is called surpassable; cultivating the transcendent noble path on that basis is called unsurpassed.

234. Section: Explaining the Distinctions in Sequence Begins — The Surpassable Begins — Bringing Forth the Contemplation

The surpassable means this: there is someone who, staying alone in a quiet, empty place, with a still and concentrated mind, contemplates in accordance with principle: "The life-faculty is tied to the in-breath and out-breath. If after the in-breath I had no out-breath, or after the out-breath no in-breath, the life-faculty would at once be cut off. Yet within the aspect of impermanence there is this marvelous matter: when the in-breath has ceased, my life-faculty remains, and I go on to reach the time when the out-breath arises; when the out-breath has ceased, my life-faculty remains, and I go on to reach the time when the in-breath arises."

Chinese Source

其有上者，謂如有一，獨處空閑，以靜定心如理觀察：命根繫屬入息出息。若我於入息後無有出息，或出息後無入息者，如是命根即應斷滅。而於無常行中，有希奇事。入息滅已，我命根住，乃復得至出息生時。出息滅已，我命根住，乃復得至入息生時。

Ru-Yi Translation

The surpassable means this: there is someone who, staying alone in a quiet, empty place, with a still and concentrated mind, contemplates in accordance with principle: "The life-faculty is tied to the in-breath and out-breath. If after the in-breath I had no out-breath, or after the out-breath no in-breath, the life-faculty would at once be cut off. Yet within the aspect of impermanence there is this marvelous matter: when the in-breath has ceased, my life-faculty remains, and I go on to reach the time when the out-breath arises; when the out-breath has ceased, my life-faculty remains, and I go on to reach the time when the in-breath arises."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining the distinctions in sequence (丑二隨釋差別2), beginning with the surpassable (寅一有上2), beginning with bringing forth the contemplation (卯一出觀察) — this practitioner, having right mindfulness of impermanence, cultivates diligently. Life is impermanent and so is the breath, yet the life-faculty remains from one breath to the next. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one who reflects on this often will be diligent, because when a breath does not come, this life is over.

235. Section: Clarifying the Cultivation, Closing "The Surpassable" Entirely

Because they take such a matter as their object, they are deeply disenchanted, and they purify their mind of the craving and aversion aroused toward the objects of the three times. This is called surpassable.

Chinese Source

彼由攀緣如是事故，深心厭離，於三世境所發愛恚淨修其心，是名有上。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they take such a matter as their object, they are deeply disenchanted, and they purify their mind of the craving and aversion aroused toward the objects of the three times. This is called surpassable.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, clarifying the cultivation (卯二明修習) closes entirely, closing the surpassable (寅一有上2: 出觀察/明修習) entirely — from the depths of the mind the practitioner turns away from the objects of past, present, and future, and by this mindfulness restrains the craving and aversion that arise toward forms, sounds, smells, tastes, and touches, so that they do not become active. Relying on this, one can gain worldly concentration, from the first dhyāna to the third.

236. Section: The Unsurpassed, Closing "Explaining the Distinctions in Sequence" and "Desire" Entirely

Cultivation by the sixteen modes, one should know, is unsurpassed.

Chinese Source

十六行修，當知無上。

Ru-Yi Translation

Cultivation by the sixteen modes, one should know, is unsurpassed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the unsurpassed (寅二無上) closes entirely, closing explaining the distinctions in sequence (丑二隨釋差別2: 有上/無上) entirely, closing desire (子三欲2: 標列二種/隨釋差別) entirely — the sixteen excellent practices can realize the first fruit and finally bring the fourth fruit to complete accomplishment.

In one companion sūtra, the monk Ariṣṭa (阿梨瑟吒) tells the Buddha that he has no longing for past aggregates, no delight in future formations, and no attachment to present ones, and that he has subdued perceptions of craving and aversion. The Buddha goes on to teach that true mindfulness of the breath observes the arising and ceasing of the breath in walking, standing, sitting, and lying down, which is the sixteen-fold practice. In another, the Buddha points out to the monks the monk Kapphiṇa (罽賓那, also 摩訶劫賓那), sitting upright in the Andha Forest (安陀林), mind and body straight, intent on the sixteen practices, and urges them to learn from his wholesome desire: one so trained until it is thoroughly familiar attains it at will, without added effort.

237. Section: The Subtle Begins — Called Subtle Abiding

Further, because such mindfulness of the in-breath and out-breath, abiding, takes subtle wind-form as its object, it is called subtle abiding.

Chinese Source

復次，如是入息出息念住，緣細風色為境界故，名微細住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, because such mindfulness of the in-breath and out-breath, abiding, takes subtle wind-form as its object, it is called subtle abiding.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the subtle (子四細3), the fourth of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's second phrase, beginning with called subtle abiding (丑一名微細住) — wind is a kind of form, called wind-form, and in this mindfulness the mind, without affliction, takes that subtle form as its object.

Master Cháng Bǎi cites Great Master Zhìyǐ (智者大師) in the 釋禪波羅蜜, who names four marks of breath: wind, panting, air, and breath proper. The first has sound in the nose; the second is silent but blocked; the third is neither, but not fine; the fourth is soundless, unblocked, unhurried, and continuous, and it alone is the well-regulated breath, most suited to seated meditation.

238. Section: Called Not Flowing Out and Scattering

Because it is cut off from all scattered investigation and reflection, it is called not flowing out and scattering.

Chinese Source

隔絕一切亂尋伺故，名不流散。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because it is cut off from all scattered investigation and reflection, it is called not flowing out and scattering.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called not flowing out and scattering (丑二名不流散) — while rightly cultivating, the mind is cut off from all confused thoughts of desire, aversion, and harm, and does not reach out to the objects of the five desires.

239. Section: Called Unsubduable, Closing "The Subtle" Entirely

Because it gives rise to the vast, sublime pliancy of body and mind, it is called unsubduable.

Chinese Source

發生廣大身心所有妙輕安故，名不可伏。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because it gives rise to the vast, sublime pliancy of body and mind, it is called unsubduable.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, called unsubduable (丑三名不可伏) closes entirely, closing the subtle (子四細3: 名微細住/名不流散/名不可伏) entirely — when the mind is quiet and pure, one feels the subtle ease and pleasure of pliancy throughout body and mind, and Master Cháng Bǎi says this comes about naturally.

In the companion sūtra, the Buddha spends two months in seated meditation, seen only by the monk bringing food and at the half-monthly recitation. On emerging he tells the monks that if outsiders ask how the renunciant Gautama sat during those two months, they should say he sat in mindfulness of the breath, the sixteen excellent practices. Having known these, he reflected that this was still a coarse abiding in thought, and he stilled it and abode in a subtler one. Three devas came after midnight: the first said the renunciant Gautama had entered concentration; the second, that he had gone further into the brahmā abiding or the deva abiding; the third, that he had entered the noble abiding.

Master Cháng Bǎi glosses the terms. The noble abiding (聖住) is the three samādhis of emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness, or the concentration of cessation; the deva abiding (天住) is the four dhyānas and four formless attainments; the brahmā abiding (梵住) is the four immeasurable minds. The trainee's abiding, the abiding of one beyond training, and the Tathāgata's abiding are also named. Breath grows finer as the mind grows quieter, and by this practice one can abide in all of these.

240. Section: Bodily Weariness Begins — Keeping the Body from Weariness
