Further, cultivating such mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath keeps the body from weariness, because it is well able to dispel the secondary afflictions of the class of calm-abiding.

Chinese Source

復次，修習如是入出息念，令身無勞，善能除遣奢摩他品隨煩惱故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, cultivating such mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath keeps the body from weariness, because it is well able to dispel the secondary afflictions of the class of calm-abiding.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens bodily weariness (子五身勞5), the fifth of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's second phrase, which gives five merits of this practice, beginning with keeping the body from weariness (丑一令身無勞) — it dispels the laziness, torpor, sleepiness, and other secondary afflictions of the class of calm-abiding.

241. Section: Keeping the Eye from Weariness

It keeps the eye from weariness, because it is well able to dispel the secondary afflictions of the class of insight.

Chinese Source

令眼無勞，善能除遣毗鉢舍那品隨煩惱故。

Ru-Yi Translation

It keeps the eye from weariness, because it is well able to dispel the secondary afflictions of the class of insight.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers keeping the eye from weariness (丑二令眼無勞) — Master Cháng Bǎi explains that this eye is not the eye of flesh but the wisdom eye, the mind, which does not tire once the restlessness, regret, and other secondary afflictions of the class of insight are dispelled. When calm-abiding is accomplished the body does not tire, and when insight is accomplished the mind does not tire.

242. Section: Called Continuous Observation

Because one continuously observes the bliss of nirvāṇa, it is called the bliss of continuous observation.

Chinese Source

由隨觀察涅槃樂故，名隨觀樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because one continuously observes the bliss of nirvāṇa, it is called the bliss of continuous observation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called continuous observation (丑三名隨觀察) — one follows and observes that nirvāṇa alone permanently severs all suffering and attains the bliss of the ultimate meaning. Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Grounds with Investigation and Reflection and the Rest, fascicle 4: in the uncontaminated realm all gross heaviness and suffering are permanently severed, and so this alone is the bliss of the ultimate meaning, the rest being suffering. Ordinary people, not understanding nirvāṇa, fear it; the noble ones observe this bliss and do not.

243. Section: Called the Bliss of Experiencing

Because one follows and experiences the bliss of the ground of the third dhyāna, it is called the bliss of experiencing.

Chinese Source

由隨領受第三靜慮地中樂故，名領受樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because one follows and experiences the bliss of the ground of the third dhyāna, it is called the bliss of experiencing.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers called the bliss of experiencing (丑四名領受樂) — by worldly practice, one experiences in the third dhyāna the sublime bliss apart from joy, the most excellent pleasure within the three realms.

244. Section: Called Abiding in Ease, Closing "Bodily Weariness" Entirely

Because one abides undefiled, and because one is without fear, it is called abiding in ease.

Chinese Source

無染住故，無恐畏故，名安樂住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because one abides undefiled, and because one is without fear, it is called abiding in ease.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, called abiding in ease (丑五名安樂住) closes entirely, closing bodily weariness (子五身勞5: 令身無勞/令眼無勞/名隨觀察/名領受樂/名安樂住) entirely — realizing the transcendent noble path, one has severed the seeds of craving, aversion, and delusion and so abides undefiled; having severed the grasping of a self, the mind is without fear.

One companion sūtra teaches that much cultivation of this mindfulness brings the body no weariness and the eye no trouble, that one abides observing nirvāṇa, experiences the bliss of the third dhyāna, and abides undefiled and fearless, gaining great fruit and great benefit; whoever seeks the first dhyāna and those above it, the first three fruits, or the supernormal powers should rely on it. Another teaches that the Buddhas of the three times, like the monks at present, cultivate this mindfulness to sever thoughts. There the elder monks gather the young monks under their guidance, and the Buddha is pleased; those with attainments who seek further should cultivate these sixteen practices.

245. Section: The Trainee's Abiding Begins — Abiding Without Difference

Further, it is possible that someone may think: "The Tathāgata and that most inferior one who has attained the arhat fruit, liberated by wisdom, are without difference." That is, thinking this in terms of liberation: "The Tathāgata's liberation and the liberation of an arhat liberated by wisdom are without difference."

Chinese Source

復次，容有是處，或有一人作如是念：如來與彼最極下劣得慧解脫阿羅漢果無有差別。謂依解脫作是思惟：如來解脫與慧解脫阿羅漢果所有解脫無有差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, it is possible that someone may think: "The Tathāgata and that most inferior one who has attained the arhat fruit, liberated by wisdom, are without difference." That is, thinking this in terms of liberation: "The Tathāgata's liberation and the liberation of an arhat liberated by wisdom are without difference."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the trainee's abiding (子六學住2), the sixth of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's third phrase "the trainee's abiding and attention" (學住及作意), beginning with abiding without difference (丑一住無差別) — the most inferior here is one without the concentration of cessation, who may have relied on the preliminary stage of concentration to reach arhatship liberated by wisdom. Such a person's thought is based on liberation from birth and death, since neither will revolve in it again.

246. Section: Abiding With Difference Begins — Raising the Thought

Could there further be someone who thinks: "The Tathāgata has the abiding free of the hindrances. Those within the inner dharma who are most inferior, whether trainees or ordinary beings, by the power of diligence abide having suppressed and severed the five hindrances, and this is called abiding free of the hindrances. Are this abiding free of the hindrances and that abiding free of the hindrances without difference, as with liberation, or do they differ?"

Chinese Source

頗復有人作如是念：如來所有離諸蓋住；居內法中最極下劣若諸有學、若諸異生，由精進力，於其五蓋伏斷而住，名離蓋住。此離蓋住、彼離蓋住，為如解脫無有差別，為有差別？

Ru-Yi Translation

Could there further be someone who thinks: "The Tathāgata has the abiding free of the hindrances. Those within the inner dharma who are most inferior, whether trainees or ordinary beings, by the power of diligence abide having suppressed and severed the five hindrances, and this is called abiding free of the hindrances. Are this abiding free of the hindrances and that abiding free of the hindrances without difference, as with liberation, or do they differ?"

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens abiding with difference (丑二住有差別2), beginning with raising the thought (寅一舉作念) — the Buddha, with his inconceivable concentrations, abides apart from all the hindrances; those who train in the worldly and transcendent paths abide with the hindrances suppressed and severed. The question is whether the two are alike or not.

247. Section: Showing the Right Meaning Begins — Marking the Difference

One should know that these two abidings free of the hindrances differ very greatly.

Chinese Source

應知如是二離蓋住，極大差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that these two abidings free of the hindrances differ very greatly.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens showing the right meaning (寅二顯正義2), beginning with marking the difference (卯一標差別).

248. Section: Explaining Their Marks Begins — The Trainee

That is, though with respect to active manifestation the trainees' mind abiding free of the hindrances is equal to the Tathāgata's, yet because their latent tendencies are not yet permanently severed, the hindrances again and again interrupt the continuum of the mind, and again and again they apply attention and strive to dispel them.

Chinese Source

謂諸有學，雖現行故，離蓋住心與如來等。然彼隨眠未永斷故，諸蓋數數間心相續，數數作意勵力除遣。

Ru-Yi Translation

That is, though with respect to active manifestation the trainees' mind abiding free of the hindrances is equal to the Tathāgata's, yet because their latent tendencies are not yet permanently severed, the hindrances again and again interrupt the continuum of the mind, and again and again they apply attention and strive to dispel them.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining their marks (卯二釋彼相2), beginning with the trainee (辰一有學). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that trainees can suppress the five hindrances and abide apart from them, their pure mind then being the same as the Tathāgata's; but the seeds of the innate afflictions are not yet severed, so with conditions the hindrances return, breaking the continuity of pure mind, and they must apply principled attention and exert themselves each time.

249. Section: The Tathāgata, Closing "Explaining Their Marks," "Showing the Right Meaning," "Abiding With Difference," and "The Trainee's Abiding" Entirely

Because the Tathāgata's hindrances are finally severed, he abides free of the hindrances. This and their abiding free of the hindrances differ very greatly; it is not as with liberation, where there is no difference.

Chinese Source

如來諸蓋畢竟斷故，離諸蓋住。與彼所有離諸蓋住，極大差別，非如解脫無有差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because the Tathāgata's hindrances are finally severed, he abides free of the hindrances. This and their abiding free of the hindrances differ very greatly; it is not as with liberation, where there is no difference.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the Tathāgata (辰二如來) closes entirely, closing explaining their marks (卯二釋彼相2: 有學/如來) entirely, closing showing the right meaning (寅二顯正義2: 標差別/釋彼相) entirely, closing abiding with difference (丑二住有差別2: 舉作念/顯正義) entirely, closing the trainee's abiding (子六學住2: 住無差別/住有差別) entirely — the Buddha's seeds of the five hindrances are finally ended and their habitual residue as well, and he abides apart from them spontaneously; the trainees' seeds remain, and they must make effort. Master Cháng Bǎi adds that even an arhat, though liberated from the afflictions of craving and view, retains habitual residue, while the Buddha has neither.

In the companion sūtra, Mahānāma (摩訶男) asks the Venerable Jiāmó (迦磨) whether the trainee's abiding and the Tathāgata's abiding are the same in severing the five hindrances. He answers that the trainee severs their active manifestation and abides much of the time free of them, while the Buddha has extinguished their seeds, like a palmyra palm with its crown cut off, which does not grow now and will not grow again; the two are not the same.

250. Section: Attention Begins — Bringing Forth the Basis Begins — Being Able to Know

Further, a practitioner of yoga, taking mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath as the support, and taking as the basis the principled attention with which the four foundations of mindfulness are cultivated, truly knows, of whatever unprincipled attention is within the mind and not yet severed, that it is unprincipled; and truly knows, of whatever principled attention is within, that it is principled.

Chinese Source

復次，修瑜伽師，入出息念為所依止，修四念住如理作意以為依止，於諸未斷內心所有非理作意，如實了知是為非理；於內所有如理作意，如實了知是為如理。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a practitioner of yoga, taking mindfulness of in-breath and out-breath as the support, and taking as the basis the principled attention with which the four foundations of mindfulness are cultivated, truly knows, of whatever unprincipled attention is within the mind and not yet severed, that it is unprincipled; and truly knows, of whatever principled attention is within, that it is principled.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens attention (子七作意2), the seventh of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's third phrase, beginning with bringing forth the basis (丑一出依止2), beginning with being able to know (寅一能了知). Master Cháng Bǎi reads this as follows: in cultivating the in-breath and out-breath the practitioner takes right mindfulness as the basis, and in cultivating the foundations of body, feeling, mind, and dharmas takes principled attention as the basis. Whichever kind of attention arises, it is truly known for what it is.

251. Section: In Order to Sever Permanently, Closing "Bringing Forth the Basis" Entirely

Having known this, they keep entirely far from whatever unprincipled attention is within, and entirely cultivate whatever principled attention is within, in order to cause the former to be permanently severed and extinguished.

Chinese Source

既了知已，於內所有非理作意，一向遠離；於內所有如理作意，一向修習。為欲令彼永斷滅故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Having known this, they keep entirely far from whatever unprincipled attention is within, and entirely cultivate whatever principled attention is within, in order to cause the former to be permanently severed and extinguished.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, in order to sever permanently (寅二為永斷) closes entirely, closing bringing forth the basis (丑一出依止2: 能了知/為永斷) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that self and others are only the gathering of form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, with no self or being in them, and a self and beings are merely designated upon the aggregates. Thoughts of harm and the like toward oneself and toward beings dear or not dear, resting on what is only designated, are unprincipled attention; reflecting on the true principles the Buddha taught, that the five aggregates are impermanent, suffering, empty, without self, and dependently arisen, is principled.

252. Section: Raising the Simile Begins — Unprincipled Attention

Further, here, the four dharmas, body and the rest, are like four great roads. Unprincipled attention is like a mound of dust and earth, because it is not firm, because it is not real, and because it confuses the mind.

Chinese Source

又於此中，身等四法如四大路。非理作意如塵土丘，不堅牢故，不真實故，迷亂心故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, here, the four dharmas, body and the rest, are like four great roads. Unprincipled attention is like a mound of dust and earth, because it is not firm, because it is not real, and because it confuses the mind.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens raising the simile (丑二舉譬喻2), beginning with unprincipled attention (寅一非理作意) — in cultivating the four foundations, the body, feeling, mind, and dharmas are like the four great roads of east, south, west, and north. Unprincipled attention is like a mound of dust, neither stable nor true, and it confuses the practitioner's mind on the path.

253. Section: Principled Attention, Closing "Raising the Simile" and "Attention" Entirely

Principled attention is like carriages, carts, and wagons coming from the four directions: operating through the gates of the four objects, body and the rest, it is able to destroy that unprincipled attention which is like a mound of dust and earth, and it also makes the entire continuum pure.

Chinese Source

如理作意如四方來輿乘車輦，緣身等四境界門轉，能損害彼如塵土丘非理作意，亦令一切相續清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Principled attention is like carriages, carts, and wagons coming from the four directions: operating through the gates of the four objects, body and the rest, it is able to destroy that unprincipled attention which is like a mound of dust and earth, and it also makes the entire continuum pure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, principled attention (寅二如理作意) closes entirely, closing raising the simile (丑二舉譬喻2: 非理作意/如理作意) entirely, closing attention (子七作意2: 出依止/舉譬喻) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi applies the simile: observing the body as impure, feeling as suffering, mind as impermanent, and dharmas as without self, principled attention scatters the mound as carriages would, and body, feeling, mind, and dharmas become wholly pure in their continuum.

In the companion sūtra, the Buddha tells the monks that, as carriages coming from the four directions trample and break up a mound of earth at a crossroads, so one who rightly observes the four foundations, knowing truly and reflecting well within, extinguishes and casts off the craving and distress of unprincipled attention. This is called a monk's diligent practice of the four foundations of mindfulness.

254. Section: Wisdom and Non-Grasping Begins — Bringing Forth No-Self Begins — The General Marking

Further, those who diligently cultivate mindfulness of breathing, by rightly cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness, are, because of the equality of no-self, equal and equal.

Chinese Source

復次，精勤修習諸息念者，由正修習四種念住，無我等故，平等平等。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, those who diligently cultivate mindfulness of breathing, by rightly cultivating the four foundations of mindfulness, are, because of the equality of no-self, equal and equal.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens wisdom and non-grasping (子八智無執2), the eighth and last of the long-form explanation's eight matters, named by the verse's fourth phrase "wisdom and non-grasping coming last" (智無執為後), beginning with bringing forth no-self (丑一出無我2), beginning with the general marking (寅一總標).

Per the 披尋記, it was said before that a practitioner of yoga, attending in accordance with principle to the six inner faculties and diligently practicing the four foundations of mindfulness, at that time experiences by these foundations the six objects as fields of activity, and that this, being pure, is called emergence. From this one should know that there is no separate inner self experiencing the fields of the six faculties. Each faculty-gate experiences its own field and there is no self, and in this meaning they are equal; this is called the equality of no-self. Penetrating the principle of truth and not giving rise to the discrimination of what takes an object and what is taken as object is called equal and equal. Master Cháng Bǎi adds that the observing mind and the observed object are both without self.

255. Section: Explaining Separately Begins — Citing the Cultivation of the Foundation of Mindfulness of the Body

The principled attention that takes the body as its object is of the body's own kind. Just as the body is without self, so too is attention. Therefore it is said to be one portion of the body. One who is able to cultivate such a foundation of mindfulness of the body cannot be found at all.

Chinese Source

是身種類，能取於身如理作意。如身無我，作意亦爾。是故說彼為身一分。能修如是身念住者，都不可得。

Ru-Yi Translation

The principled attention that takes the body as its object is of the body's own kind. Just as the body is without self, so too is attention. Therefore it is said to be one portion of the body. One who is able to cultivate such a foundation of mindfulness of the body cannot be found at all.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining separately (寅二別釋2), beginning with citing the cultivation of the foundation of mindfulness of the body (卯一舉修身念住). Per the 披尋記, principled attention is able to take the body as object, and for this reason it is said to be of the body's kind, one portion of the body. As the body by its own nature is not a self, and is said to have no self, so attention by its own nature is not a self. Apart from these dharmas alone there is no separate real self, and therefore it is said that one who is able to cultivate this foundation cannot be found at all.

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Yogācārabhūmi Commentary (瑜伽論記): this is observing dharmas by dharmas. What observes is a mental dharma; what is observed is also dharma. Principled attention itself arises only from the gathering of conditions, such as attending on good people and hearing the true dharma, and so it too is not a self.

256. Section: Extending the Case to Cultivating the Remaining Foundations of Mindfulness, Closing "Explaining Separately" and "Bringing Forth No-Self" Entirely

As with the foundation of mindfulness of the body, and so on at length up to cultivating the foundation of mindfulness of dharmas, one should know that it is likewise so.

Chinese Source

如身念住，廣說乃至修法念住，當知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

As with the foundation of mindfulness of the body, and so on at length up to cultivating the foundation of mindfulness of dharmas, one should know that it is likewise so.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, extending the case to cultivating the remaining foundations of mindfulness (卯二例修餘念住) closes entirely, closing explaining separately (寅二別釋2: 舉修身念住/例修餘念住) entirely, closing bringing forth no-self (丑一出無我2: 總標/別釋) entirely — feeling, mind, and dharmas as objects are without self, and the principled attention taking them as object is without self, so that there is no grasping of a self who cultivates the four foundations.

257. Section: Concluding Non-Grasping, Closing "Wisdom and Non-Grasping," "The Long-Form Explanation," and the Entire Embrace of Mindfulness of Breathing Entirely

Such teaching of the Buddhas on cultivating the foundations of mindfulness is entirely absent from the outsiders' dharmas; therefore this teaching on cultivating the foundations of mindfulness is called "not held by any outsider."

Chinese Source

如是諸佛修念住教，外道法中皆無所有，是故說此修念住教，名非一切外道所執。

Ru-Yi Translation

Such teaching of the Buddhas on cultivating the foundations of mindfulness is entirely absent from the outsiders' dharmas; therefore this teaching on cultivating the foundations of mindfulness is called "not held by any outsider."

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding non-grasping (丑二結無執) closes entirely, closing wisdom and non-grasping (子八智無執2: 出無我/結無執) entirely, closing the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋8: 障隨惑尋等/果/欲/細/身勞/學住/作意/智無執) entirely, closing the embrace of mindfulness of breathing (壬七息念攝2: 頌標列/長行釋), the seventh of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that outsiders do not teach no-self: they hold that a self practices and a self attains, and on this basis give rise to views of permanence, of annihilation, and the sixty-two views. What all Buddhas teach accords with no-self, and this differs from the outsiders' realm.

One companion sūtra teaches that the five aggregates, though a mass of suffering, are not a self, and that by this mindfulness, abiding in the true dharma and going on to penetrate no-self, one reaches final liberation and the stilling of all suffering. No. 17 of the Mindfulness of Breathing Section in the 雜中, Ānanda's question, teaches that much cultivation of this one dharma completes the four foundations of mindfulness, the seven factors of enlightenment, and the three knowledges and two liberations: the foundations by the sixteen practices, the factors of enlightenment from the foundations, beginning with mindfulness, and the knowledges and liberations from the factors, each relying on seclusion, on freedom from desire, on cessation, and turning toward equanimity.

Dharma brings forth like dharma, the sūtra says, and when one is accomplished the others follow; these thirteen dharmas all have this mindfulness as their dominant dharma and their entry. No. 18 gives the same with a certain monk as questioner, and no. 19 with the Buddha questioning the monks.

258. Section: The Embrace of Training Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the embrace of training (壬八學攝2), the eighth of the factors of enlightenment's nine great topics, named by the general summary verse's fourth line "training" (學證淨為後), which explains the three trainings of precepts, concentration, and wisdom, beginning with the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列). Master Cháng Bǎi calls the three trainings the general framework of the Buddha's dharma: whichever of the three vehicles one would accomplish, one must go by way of them.

259. Section: The Verse's Five Matters, Closing "The Verse Marking and Listing" Entirely

First, revering śīla; the completeness of pure precepts; manifest conduct; the benefits of training; the distinctions of training coming last.

Chinese Source

初尊重尸羅　清淨戒圓滿　現行學勝利　學差別為後

Ru-Yi Translation

First, revering śīla; the completeness of pure precepts; manifest conduct; the benefits of training; the distinctions of training coming last.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列) closes entirely — five matters unfold in the long-form explanation. First, revering śīla (初尊重尸羅): the practitioner should first revere the right precepts the Buddha established, and Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Avataṃsaka Sūtra, which calls precepts the root of unsurpassed awakening. Second, the completeness of pure precepts: the definitions of the completeness of pure precepts, of wholesome dharmas, and of individual liberation. Third, manifest conduct: the conduct of body and speech, both what is to be done and what is to be refrained from, gathered under dominant conduct, dominant discipline, and dominant individual liberation. Fourth, the benefits of the three trainings. Fifth, the distinctions among the trainings of precepts, mind, and wisdom.

260. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — Revering Śīla Begins — Pointing to the Marks of Training

Training is of three kinds: the heightened training in precepts, the heightened training in mind, and the heightened training in wisdom. The distinctions by which these three trainings are established should be known in their marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

學有三種，謂增上戒學、增上心學、增上慧學。建立如是三學差別，如聲聞地應知其相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Training is of three kinds: the heightened training in precepts, the heightened training in mind, and the heightened training in wisdom. The distinctions by which these three trainings are established should be known in their marks as in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋5), a fivefold structure matching the verse's five matters, beginning with revering śīla (子一尊重尸羅3), beginning with pointing to the marks of training (丑一指學相). Per the 披尋記, abiding endowed with precepts and the rest is called the heightened training in precepts; abiding in the four dhyānas and the rest, the heightened training in mind; true knowledge and vision of the four noble truths and the rest, the heightened training in wisdom. The meaning should be known as said in the Śrāvaka Ground, which Master Cháng Bǎi locates in fascicle 28.

Master Cháng Bǎi explains dominant (增上) as having two senses: what one moves toward, and the most excellent. Precepts move one toward concentration and are not shared with those of ordinary people and outsiders; concentration, with torpor, sleepiness, restlessness, and regret absent, moves one toward wisdom and true knowledge and vision; and wisdom, free of the outsiders' view of self, helps liberate one from affliction and purifies body and mind.

He points to the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 22, on abiding endowed with precepts; the Ground of Concentrated Cultivation, fascicles 11 and 12, on the four dhyānas; and the Śrāvaka Ground, fascicle 34, on the four noble truths.

261. Section: Distinguishing What Should Be Known Begins — Generally Marking the Two Kinds of Practice

Further, in brief, among these things to be trained in, all wrong practice should be rightly known, and all right practice should be rightly known.

Chinese Source

又略於此諸所學中，所有邪行應正了知，所有正行應正了知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, in brief, among these things to be trained in, all wrong practice should be rightly known, and all right practice should be rightly known.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens distinguishing what should be known (丑二辨應知3), the second of revering śīla's three matters, beginning with generally marking the two kinds of practice (寅一總標二行) — within the three trainings of precepts, concentration, and wisdom, some practice is mistaken and some is right; distinguishing them clearly, so as not to take the wrong road, is itself something to be trained in.

262. Section: Separately Explaining the Marks Begins — Wrong Practice Begins — Bringing Forth the Lack of Reverence Begins — Raising Pure Precepts

As for wrong practice: there is someone who, having no reverence for the precepts, goes forth in a casual way. Though having gone forth, this person does not take pure precepts as dominant.

Chinese Source

言邪行者，謂如有一，不尊重戒，泛爾出家。雖復出家，不以淨戒為其增上。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for wrong practice: there is someone who, having no reverence for the precepts, goes forth in a casual way. Though having gone forth, this person does not take pure precepts as dominant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately explaining the marks (寅二別釋其相2), beginning with wrong practice (卯一邪行2), bringing forth the lack of reverence (辰一出不尊重2), and raising pure precepts (巳一舉於淨戒). Master Cháng Bǎi explains "casual" (泛爾) as drifting and unconsidered. This person goes forth without the right vow of renunciation or of enlightenment, having had no training in the precepts and no prior study of the Dharma, because another urged it and without wise deliberation.

He adds that this is only a point of reference, since the mind is impermanent: one who goes forth with a poor motive, and then diligently cultivates precepts, concentration, and wisdom, can still make it good.

263. Section: By Extension to Concentration and Wisdom, Closing "Bringing Forth the Lack of Reverence" Entirely

As with pure precepts, so one should know it is with concentration and with wisdom.

Chinese Source

如於淨戒，於定、於慧，應知亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

As with pure precepts, so one should know it is with concentration and with wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, by extension to concentration and wisdom (巳二例於定慧) closes entirely, closing bringing forth the lack of reverence (辰一出不尊重2: 舉於淨戒/例於定慧) entirely. One who cannot revere pure precepts or rely on them to advance will find it harder still to revere and understand the deeper concentration and wisdom. Pure precepts are the most basic of the three.

264. Section: Clarifying the Transgression, Closing "Wrong Practice" Entirely

Such a person may well commit an offense without remainder, and of such a person the World-Honored One says that they are incapable of the realizations of the renunciant's fruits. Therefore one should know that such a person wholly transgresses all three trainings.

Chinese Source

彼可容有犯無餘罪，於彼世尊說其於諸沙門果證為無能者。是故當知，彼於三學一向毀犯。

Ru-Yi Translation

Such a person may well commit an offense without remainder, and of such a person the World-Honored One says that they are incapable of the realizations of the renunciant's fruits. Therefore one should know that such a person wholly transgresses all three trainings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, clarifying the transgression (辰二明其毀犯) closes entirely, closing wrong practice (卯一邪行2: 出不尊重/明其毀犯) entirely. Per the 披尋記, the offense without remainder is explained below: the five classes of offense that embrace every offense are the class of defeat (他勝), the class of the remainder (眾餘), the class of downfall (隕墜), the class of individual confession (別悔), and the class of wrongdoing (惡作). The four other classes are called offenses with remainder; the class of defeat is called the offense without remainder.

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one who has never trained in the precepts easily commits this gravest offense, for which no place remains in the assembly. In the śrāvaka teaching it cannot be repented, and such a person has no capacity in this life for any of the four fruits. Committing a root offense therefore amounts to transgressing precepts, concentration, and wisdom together. Fascicles 99 and 100 take this up at length.

265. Section: Right Practice Begins — Marking and Listing the Three Kinds

As for right practice, there are three right practices: lower, middle, and upper.

Chinese Source

言正行者，有三正行，謂下中上。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for right practice, there are three right practices: lower, middle, and upper.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens right practice (卯二正行2), beginning with marking and listing the three kinds (辰一標列三種).

266. Section: Separately Explaining the Marks Begins — Lower Right Practice Begins — Distinguishing Reverence Begins — Reverence for Pure Precepts

As for lower right practice: there is someone who reveres pure precepts and also takes pure precepts as dominant, the opposite of what was said before.

Chinese Source

下正行者，謂如有一，尊重淨戒，亦以淨戒為其增上，與前相違。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for lower right practice: there is someone who reveres pure precepts and also takes pure precepts as dominant, the opposite of what was said before.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately explaining the marks (辰二別釋其相3), beginning with lower right practice (巳一下正行2), distinguishing reverence (午一辨尊重2), and reverence for pure precepts (未一尊重淨戒). This practitioner reveres the pure precepts the Buddha laid down and strives to keep them, in contrast with the wrong practitioner above.

267. Section: Not Revering Concentration and Wisdom, Closing "Distinguishing Reverence" Entirely

Toward concentration and toward wisdom, this person gives rise to no reverence and does not take them as dominant.

Chinese Source

於定、於慧不生尊重，不為增上。

Ru-Yi Translation

Toward concentration and toward wisdom, this person gives rise to no reverence and does not take them as dominant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, not revering concentration and wisdom (未二非重定慧) closes entirely, closing distinguishing reverence (午一辨尊重2: 尊重淨戒/非重定慧) entirely.

268. Section: Clarifying the Transgression, Closing "Lower Right Practice" Entirely

Such a person cannot commit an offense without remainder, yet may commit lesser and minor offenses. Of such a person the Tathāgata does not say that they are incapable of the realizations of the renunciant's fruits.

Chinese Source

此不容有犯無餘罪，而容有犯小隨小罪。於此如來不說其於沙門果證為無能者。

Ru-Yi Translation

Such a person cannot commit an offense without remainder, yet may commit lesser and minor offenses. Of such a person the Tathāgata does not say that they are incapable of the realizations of the renunciant's fruits.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, clarifying the transgression (午二明毀犯) closes entirely, closing lower right practice (巳一下正行2: 辨尊重/明毀犯) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the lesser and minor offenses include such things as wrongdoing (惡作). The Buddha does not say that this person cannot reach the four fruits, for pure keeping of the precepts leads on, stage by stage, to nirvāṇa.

269. Section: Middle Right Practice Begins — Distinguishing Reverence

As for middle right practice: one reveres both precepts and concentration, and also takes them as dominant.

Chinese Source

中正行者，謂於戒定皆悉尊重，亦為增上。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for middle right practice: one reveres both precepts and concentration, and also takes them as dominant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens middle right practice (巳二中正行3), beginning with distinguishing reverence (午一辨尊重).

270. Section: Clarifying the Transgression

As with reverence for the precepts, the order of transgression is likewise here.

Chinese Source

如尊重戒，毀犯次第，此中亦爾。

Ru-Yi Translation

As with reverence for the precepts, the order of transgression is likewise here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers clarifying the transgression (午二明毀犯). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the pattern is the same as for lower right practice: root offenses are not committed, and lesser and minor ones may be. Because these can be restored to purity through repentance, the Tathāgata does not say that such a person cannot realize the noble fruits.

271. Section: Concluding the Boundary, Closing "Middle Right Practice" Entirely

Therefore one should know that this reaches as far as every stage of the ordinary being.

Chinese Source

是故當知，乃至所有諸異生位。

Ru-Yi Translation

Therefore one should know that this reaches as far as every stage of the ordinary being.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding the boundary (午三結邊際) closes entirely, closing middle right practice (巳二中正行3: 辨尊重/明毀犯/結邊際) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that lower and middle right practice both belong to practitioners at the stage of the ordinary being.

272. Section: Upper Right Practice Begins — Distinguishing Reverence

As for upper right practice: one has already seen the truths, and reveres all three trainings.

Chinese Source

上正行者，謂已見諦，於三種學皆悉尊重。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for upper right practice: one has already seen the truths, and reveres all three trainings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens upper right practice (巳三上正行2), beginning with distinguishing reverence (午一辨尊重). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that this practitioner is a noble one of the first fruit or above, who has realized non-discriminating wisdom. Those who have not yet seen the truths should train in the same way, since the three trainings are equally important.

273. Section: Bringing Forth the Absence of Transgression, Closing "Upper Right Practice," "Right Practice," and "Separately Explaining the Marks" Entirely

Such a person has already attained the realization of a renunciant's fruit, and there is no need to weigh whether they are capable or incapable.

Chinese Source

此已獲得沙門果證，不待思擇有能、無能。

Ru-Yi Translation

Such a person has already attained the realization of a renunciant's fruit, and there is no need to weigh whether they are capable or incapable.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, bringing forth the absence of transgression (午二出無犯) closes entirely, closing upper right practice (巳三上正行2: 辨尊重/出無犯) entirely, closing separately explaining the marks (辰二別釋其相3: 下正行/中正行/上正行) entirely, closing right practice (卯二正行2: 標列三種/別釋其相) entirely, closing separately explaining the marks (寅二別釋其相2: 邪行/正行) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that what this person has accomplished is the precepts that accompany the path (道共戒), and so no transgression occurs.

274. Section: Concluding and Clarifying the Opening and Combining, Closing "Distinguishing What Should Be Known" Entirely

In this way, the two practices open into four kinds, and these four kinds combine into the two practices. These two and these four are exactly equal.

Chinese Source

如是二行開為四種，即此四種合為二行。此二與四平等平等。

Ru-Yi Translation

In this way, the two practices open into four kinds, and these four kinds combine into the two practices. These two and these four are exactly equal.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding and clarifying the opening and combining (寅三結明開合) closes entirely, closing distinguishing what should be known (丑二辨應知3: 總標二行/別釋其相/結明開合) entirely. Per the 披尋記, wrong practice and right practice are the two practices. Wrong practice is only one, and right practice has three, lower, middle, and upper, and these are the four kinds. Master Cháng Bǎi adds that the content is the same; only the opening and combining differ.

275. Section: Sifting What Is to Be Done, Closing "Reverence for Discipline" Entirely

One should know that, here, where there is the training in concentration, there must be the training in precepts; where there is the training in wisdom, there must be the training in concentration; one who has the training in precepts does not necessarily have the training in concentration or the training in wisdom. If a yoga practitioner reveres all the trainings, one should know that this is called the completion of what is to be done; the others are called only a portion of what is to be done.

Chinese Source

當知此中，若有定學，必有戒學；若有慧學，必有定學；有戒學者，不必定有定學、慧學。若瑜伽師尊重諸學，當知是名所作圓滿；其餘但名所作一分。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, where there is the training in concentration, there must be the training in precepts; where there is the training in wisdom, there must be the training in concentration; one who has the training in precepts does not necessarily have the training in concentration or the training in wisdom. If a yoga practitioner reveres all the trainings, one should know that this is called the completion of what is to be done; the others are called only a portion of what is to be done.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, sifting what is to be done (丑三簡所作) closes entirely, closing reverence for discipline (子一尊重尸羅3: 指學相/辨應知/簡所作) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one with right concentration keeps the precepts purely, having the precepts that accompany concentration (定共戒), which arise together with the dhyānas and are accomplished only in a concentrated mind. The precepts of individual liberation, by contrast, are received through formal ordination and can be accomplished in an ordinary mind.

One with the wisdom of non-discrimination must have concentration, though its depth varies, since affliction is cut off by cultivating calm and insight within concentration. Keeping the precepts of individual liberation concerns only body and speech, and afflictions may still arise within, so precepts alone do not guarantee the other two. A practitioner of any of the three vehicles reveres all three trainings; one who attends to only one or two has done only a portion.

276. Section: Purified Precepts Perfected Begins — The First Meaning Begins — The Perfection of Pure Precepts

Further, because one is able to stay far from what is an offense by nature, one should know that this is called the perfection of pure precepts.

Chinese Source

復次，於性罪處能遠離故，當知是名淨戒圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, because one is able to stay far from what is an offense by nature, one should know that this is called the perfection of pure precepts.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens purified precepts perfected (子二清淨戒圓滿2), the second phrase of the verse on the trainings, beginning with the first meaning (丑一第一義3) and the perfection of pure precepts (寅一淨戒圓滿). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that an offense by nature (性罪) is evil in its very nature, such as killing, stealing, sexual misconduct, and lying, and whoever commits it receives the result, whether or not the Buddha laid down a precept.

277. Section: The Perfection of Wholesome Dharmas

Toward all the wholesome dharmas that embrace pure precepts, such as closely guarding the doors of the faculties, one upholds them without interruption and they proceed in continuity; because of this, one should know that this is called the perfection of wholesome dharmas.

Chinese Source

於能密護諸根門等攝受淨戒所有善法，無間受持相續轉故，當知是名善法圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Toward all the wholesome dharmas that embrace pure precepts, such as closely guarding the doors of the faculties, one upholds them without interruption and they proceed in continuity; because of this, one should know that this is called the perfection of wholesome dharmas.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers the perfection of wholesome dharmas (寅二善法圓滿). Per the 披尋記, "such as" takes in moderation in food and all the other wholesome dharmas. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that closely guarding the doors of the faculties means, when the six faculties meet the six objects, protecting the mind from chasing after them. The chief point is guarding the mental faculty, for the false discrimination of the sixth consciousness, and not the five outer faculties, is the root of offense. The rest includes wakeful yoga, abiding with right knowledge, drawing near good friends, and hearing the Dharma.

278. Section: The Perfection of Individual Liberation, Closing "The First Meaning" Entirely

Because one is able to stay far from what is a prohibited offense, one should know that this is called the perfection of individual liberation.

Chinese Source

於遮罪處能遠離故，當知是名別解脫圓滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because one is able to stay far from what is a prohibited offense, one should know that this is called the perfection of individual liberation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the perfection of individual liberation (寅三別解脫圓滿) closes entirely, closing the first meaning (丑一第一義3: 淨戒圓滿/善法圓滿/別解脫圓滿) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that a prohibited offense (遮罪) is not evil by nature. Liquor, for example, is not evil in itself, but the Buddha forbade it, because drinking leads on to breaking the precepts he laid down. To stay far from one such offense is to be freed in that one respect.

279. Section: The Second Meaning, Closing "Purified Precepts Perfected" Entirely

Further, relying on the precepts the noble ones cherish, relying on the five kinds of skill in the aggregates and the rest, and relying on the restraint of individual liberation, upholding all the conventional prohibitions: respectively, one should know these as the distinctions of the second gate of the perfection of pure precepts and the rest.

Chinese Source

又依聖所愛戒，若依蘊等五種善巧，及依別解脫律儀，受持世俗所有禁戒，隨其次第，應知淨戒圓滿等第二門差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, relying on the precepts the noble ones cherish, relying on the five kinds of skill in the aggregates and the rest, and relying on the restraint of individual liberation, upholding all the conventional prohibitions: respectively, one should know these as the distinctions of the second gate of the perfection of pure precepts and the rest.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the second meaning (丑二第二義) closes entirely, closing purified precepts perfected (子二清淨戒圓滿2: 第一義/第二義) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that, respectively, the precepts the noble ones cherish are the perfection of pure precepts: having attained the path, the noble ones at once accomplish right speech, right action, and right livelihood, and with right knowledge they never commit bodily or verbal misconduct. The five skills, in the aggregates, sense-bases, elements, dependent origination, and what is and is not the case, are the perfection of wholesome dharmas. The precepts of individual liberation are the perfection of individual liberation. He matches these to the precepts that accompany the path, those that accompany concentration, and the precepts of individual liberation.

Companion sūtras follow. The precepts are likened to a firm city wall guarding the six faculties day and night, against which no enemy can enter. The heightened training in precepts, mind, and wisdom is then set out: abiding in the precepts, guarding individual liberation, perfect deportment and range of activity, seeing fear in the smallest offense, and taking up the training rules; leaving behind evil dharmas and attaining the four dhyānas; and truly knowing the four noble truths. Some have the heightened precepts without concentration or wisdom, and some have precepts and concentration without heightened wisdom.

Others link the trainings to the fruits. A monastic who stresses the precepts confesses each small fault at once and, cutting off the three fetters, reaches stream-entry, with at most seven births. One who stresses precepts and concentration cuts off the five lower fetters and reaches non-return. One who reveres all three is freed from the outflows of desire, existence, and ignorance, and knows that birth is ended. Master Cháng Bǎi explains the further grades these sūtras add, including the one-seed noble one, the follower of the Dharma and the follower of faith, and the five kinds of non-returner.

280. Section: Manifest Activity Begins — Bringing Forth the Two Distinctions Begins — Briefly Marking and Listing: The First Kind

Further, based on pure discipline, there are in brief two distinctions in what is trained in. First, upholding the received discipline not embraced by stopping: the training rules embraced by all lawful manifest activity of body and speech.

Chinese Source

復次，依淨尸羅略有二種所學差別。一者、受持非止所攝所受尸羅，所有如法身語現行所攝學處；

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, based on pure discipline, there are in brief two distinctions in what is trained in. First, upholding the received discipline not embraced by stopping: the training rules embraced by all lawful manifest activity of body and speech.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens manifest activity (子三現行2), named by the third phrase of the verse on the trainings, beginning with bringing forth the two distinctions (丑一出二差別2) and briefly marking and listing (寅一略標列). Per the 披尋記, to rightly receive the full precepts in order to stop evil is called the received discipline embraced by stopping. The lawful activity of body and speech here is what one must diligently train in and rightly do, and not merely a barring of what must not be done; hence it is called the received discipline not embraced by stopping.

Master Cháng Bǎi identifies this first kind as the precepts of action (作持), such as the purifying declaration, the recitation of the precepts, and the formal acts of the assembly.

281. Section: Briefly Marking and Listing: The Second Kind, Closing "Briefly Marking and Listing" Entirely

Second, upholding the training rules embraced by the received discipline that is embraced by stopping.

Chinese Source

二者、受持是止所攝所受尸羅所攝學處。

Ru-Yi Translation

Second, upholding the training rules embraced by the received discipline that is embraced by stopping.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, briefly marking and listing (寅一略標列) closes entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi identifies this second kind as the precepts of stopping (止持), from the five precepts through the full precepts of monks and nuns, which keep body and speech from killing, stealing, sexual misconduct, and other evil deeds.

282. Section: Expanding the Latter, Closing "Bringing Forth the Two Distinctions" Entirely

This is further of two kinds: there is what is taught in the vinaya but is not taught in the individual liberation; and there is what is taught in the vinaya and is also taught in the individual liberation.

Chinese Source

此復二種。謂或有是毗奈耶所說，非別解脫所說；或有是毗奈耶所說，亦是別解脫所說。

Ru-Yi Translation

This is further of two kinds: there is what is taught in the vinaya but is not taught in the individual liberation; and there is what is taught in the vinaya and is also taught in the individual liberation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, expanding the latter (寅二廣後一) closes entirely, closing bringing forth the two distinctions (丑一出二差別2: 略標列/廣後一) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains the difference by scope: the vinaya is broad, including both action and stopping, while the individual liberation is narrow, containing only stopping. He takes the first kind as the precepts of action, set out in the twenty chapters (犍度) of the vinaya texts on ordination, the recitation, the rains retreat, dwellings, and so on. He takes the second kind as the precepts of stopping, which guard body and speech, such as the five and eight precepts.

283. Section: Concluding the Three Dominants, Closing "Manifest Activity" Entirely

Therefore, speaking of all of it together in brief, there are three training rules: first, dominant manifest activity; second, the dominant vinaya; third, the dominant individual liberation.

Chinese Source

是故一切總略而言，有三學處。一、增上現行，二、增上毗奈耶，三、增上別解脫。

Ru-Yi Translation

Therefore, speaking of all of it together in brief, there are three training rules: first, dominant manifest activity; second, the dominant vinaya; third, the dominant individual liberation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding the three dominants (丑二結三增上) closes entirely, closing manifest activity (子三現行2: 出二差別/結三增上) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that dominant manifest activity is the discipline of action, which makes all lawful activity of body and speech positively manifest. The dominant vinaya is the discipline of stopping, by which one stops evil, cultivates good, and lawfully confesses what one has committed. The dominant individual liberation is illustrated by the precept against killing, which, being taken toward each sentient being, is as limitless as they are.

In the companion sūtra the Buddha names three trainings, which Master Cháng Bǎi matches to these three: the higher precept, the higher deportment, and the higher prātimokṣa. Its verse says that one who trains on the straight path first gains the knowledge of extinction and then the end of ignorance, and, unshakably liberated, bears a last body, having overcome Māra.

284. Section: The Benefits of Training

Further, abiding with training as its benefit, wisdom as foremost, liberation as the firm core, mindfulness as dominant, cultivating the three trainings to swift completion, and so on: one should know these as broadly discussed in the Explanation of the Expositions (攝釋分).

Chinese Source

復次，學勝利住、慧為上首、解脫堅固、念為增上，修習三學速圓滿等，如攝釋分廣辯應知。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, abiding with training as its benefit, wisdom as foremost, liberation as the firm core, mindfulness as dominant, cultivating the three trainings to swift completion, and so on: one should know these as broadly discussed in the Explanation of the Expositions (攝釋分).

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers the benefits of training (子四學勝利), the second half of the third phrase of the verse on the trainings. Master Cháng Bǎi takes the first four phrases in turn, each by its stage, mark, and attention. Abiding with training as its benefit is keeping the precepts, on the stage of accumulation; it is the basis of the seven attentions and belongs to none of them. Wisdom as foremost is the stage of preparation, where one distinguishes the marks of all dharmas by the attentions of discerning the mark and of resolve. Liberation as the firm core runs through the stages of seeing, cultivation, and the ultimate, where affliction is cut off from coarse to subtle. Mindfulness as dominant runs from the start to completion, since each step depends on it, and it belongs to the attention of investigation.

Companion sūtras follow. One sets out this same fourfold abiding and has a verse in which training brings much benefit, concentration is single-minded, and wisdom overcomes the armies of Māra. Another expands it. The Buddha laid down precepts for reasons that are also their benefits, including gathering in the saṅgha, inspiring faith in those who lack it, deepening it in those who have it, subduing the shameless, giving ease to those with a sense of shame, and letting the holy life long endure. Wisdom examines the dharmas, liberation firmly stands, and right mindfulness keeps watch over it all.

285. Section: The Distinctions of the Trainings Begins — The Distinctions of the Training in Precepts Begins — Distinguishing Begins — Pointing Back to the Prior Marks

Further, abiding endowed with the precepts and the rest should be known as already discussed in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Chinese Source

復次，住具戒等，如聲聞地應知已辯。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, abiding endowed with the precepts and the rest should be known as already discussed in the Śrāvaka Ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the distinctions of the trainings (子五學差別2), the last phrase of the verse on the trainings, beginning with the distinctions of the training in precepts (丑一戒學差別2), distinguishing (寅一辨2), and pointing back to the prior marks (卯一指前相). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the treatise gathers the essentials of the vinaya into six marks, set out in fascicle 22: abiding endowed with the precepts, guarding the restraint of individual liberation, perfect deportment, perfect range of activity, seeing great fear in the smallest offense, and taking up the training rules.

286. Section: Explaining the Alternate Names Begins — Named Restraint

Further, because pure precepts themselves counter every evil of breaking the precepts, and because they are the basis for closely guarding the doors of the faculties, they are called restraint.

Chinese Source

又即淨戒，對治一切犯戒惡故，密護根門所依處故，說名律儀。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, because pure precepts themselves counter every evil of breaking the precepts, and because they are the basis for closely guarding the doors of the faculties, they are called restraint.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining the alternate names (卯二釋異名7), beginning with named restraint (辰一名律儀). Master Cháng Bǎi notes that two reasons are given. With pure precepts, when the six faculties meet the six objects, the mind attends properly and the mental faculty is guarded, so the faculties are not stained by outer objects.

287. Section: Named Perfect and Pure

Because they are well received in the beginning, they are called perfect. Because they are well guarded afterward, they are called pure.

Chinese Source

初善受故，說名圓滿。後善守故，說名清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are well received in the beginning, they are called perfect. Because they are well guarded afterward, they are called pure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers named perfect and pure (辰二名圓滿清淨). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one who goes forth and receives the precepts rightly, seeking only the noble path and nirvāṇa and not a livelihood, receives them perfectly, which counters a wrong reception at the start. One who afterward keeps them well, through the six marks above, relies on the precepts to accomplish concentration in this present life, and being free of desire is pure within.

288. Section: Named Wholesome and Blameless

Because they bring about desirable results, they are called wholesome. Because they are without defilement, they are called blameless.

Chinese Source

感愛果故，說名為善。無染汙故，說名無罪。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they bring about desirable results, they are called wholesome. Because they are without defilement, they are called blameless.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers named wholesome and blameless (辰三名善無罪). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that pure precepts bring the desirable results of humans and gods, and that even without the wisdom of the ultimate truth, one who keeps them purely does not in principle go to the three evil destinies. One who keeps them purely is free of the stain of affliction and does nothing culpable.

289. Section: Named Harmless and Accordant

Because they accord well with the concentration of a loving-kind mind toward all sentient beings, they are called harmless. Because they accord well with the renunciant's nature, they are called accordant.

Chinese Source

於諸有情能善隨順慈心定故，說名無害。於沙門性善隨順故，說名隨順。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they accord well with the concentration of a loving-kind mind toward all sentient beings, they are called harmless. Because they accord well with the renunciant's nature, they are called accordant.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers named harmless and accordant (辰四名無害隨順). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the spirit of the precepts is compassion: according with loving-kindness, one wishes all beings the highest benefit and never harms them. The renunciant's nature (沙門性) is the renunciant's fruit, the very nature of the noble path, which fully accords with the ultimate truth.

290. Section: Named In Accord with Clarity and Not Following

Because they head toward the clarity that the noble ones cherish, they are called in accord with clarity. Because they never follow grasping at rites and vows, they are called not following.

Chinese Source

趣聖所愛澄清性故，名順澄清。終不隨順戒禁取故，名不隨順。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they head toward the clarity that the noble ones cherish, they are called in accord with clarity. Because they never follow grasping at rites and vows, they are called not following.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers named in accord with clarity and not following (辰五名順澄清及不隨順). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the noble ones delight in keeping the precepts purely, free of affliction, and those who attain the four confirmed purities have no stain of mind. The precepts the Buddha laid down always accord with wholesome dharmas, and so never follow the mistaken grasping at rites and vows of outsiders, such as the cow and dog practices.

291. Section: Named Same in Kind and Proceeding in Accord

Because they are shared in common with those of the same dharma, they are called same in kind. Because they serve as the basis for rightly cultivating heightened mind and wisdom, and proceed in accord with them, they are called proceeding in accord.

Chinese Source

與同法者為同分故，名同色類。於正修習增上心、慧為所依處，隨順轉故，名為順轉。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are shared in common with those of the same dharma, they are called same in kind. Because they serve as the basis for rightly cultivating heightened mind and wisdom, and proceed in accord with them, they are called proceeding in accord.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers named same in kind and proceeding in accord (辰六名同色類及與順轉). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that practitioners who together train in pure precepts are alike in refraining from killing, stealing, sexual misconduct, and lying. Pure precepts make the mind clear and glad, free of remorse and affliction, and so are the basis of heightened concentration and wisdom, the three trainings beginning from the precepts and each supporting the next.

292. Section: Named Free of Feverish, Burning, and Remorseful Torment, Closing "Explaining the Alternate Names" and "Distinguishing" Entirely

Because one does not torment others but proceeds in benefiting them, and further because one rightly stays far from the practice of self-mortification, they are called free of feverish torment. Because, toward what one has received and upholds, there is no change of heart, they are called free of burning torment. Because transgressions do not manifest, and because one lawfully confesses and removes what one has committed, they are called free of remorseful torment.

Chinese Source

不惱於他饒益轉故，又正遠離自苦行故，名無熱惱。於所受持無變悔故，名無燒惱。於諸毀犯不現行故，如法悔除已所犯故，名無悔惱。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because one does not torment others but proceeds in benefiting them, and further because one rightly stays far from the practice of self-mortification, they are called free of feverish torment. Because, toward what one has received and upholds, there is no change of heart, they are called free of burning torment. Because transgressions do not manifest, and because one lawfully confesses and removes what one has committed, they are called free of remorseful torment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, named free of feverish, burning, and remorseful torment (辰七名無熱惱無燒惱無悔惱) closes entirely, closing explaining the alternate names (卯二釋異名7) entirely, closing distinguishing (寅一辨2: 指前相/釋異名) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one with pure precepts does not disturb others, patiently bears being disturbed, benefits beings, and rightly leaves behind useless austerities. He describes freedom from burning as using requisites without taint or firm attachment, so that greed, aversion, and delusion do not burn in the mind. If mindfulness lapses and a small fault occurs, one sees it at once and confesses it lawfully, and so is not vexed by regret.

293. Section: The Conclusion, Closing "The Distinctions of the Training in Precepts" Entirely

These are called all the distinctions of the heightened training in precepts.

Chinese Source

如是名為增上戒學所有差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

These are called all the distinctions of the heightened training in precepts.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the conclusion (寅二結) closes entirely, closing the distinctions of the training in precepts (丑一戒學差別2: 辨/結) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi counts thirteen distinct marks across the seven sets of names, which show the merit of pure precepts from different angles.

294. Section: The Distinctions of the Trainings in Mind and Wisdom Begins — General Marking

Taking the three abidings as the basis, one should know all the distinctions of the heightened training in mind and in wisdom.

Chinese Source

三住為依，當知增上心學、慧學所有差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Taking the three abidings as the basis, one should know all the distinctions of the heightened training in mind and in wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the distinctions of the trainings in mind and wisdom (丑二心學慧學差別3), beginning with general marking (寅一總標). The three abidings are the heavenly abiding, the Brahmā abiding, and the noble abiding.

295. Section: Separately Correlating

Namely, by the distinctions of the heavenly abiding and the Brahmā abiding, one should know the distinctions of the heightened training in mind. By the distinctions of the noble abiding in all the dharmas such as the factors of enlightenment, one should know the distinctions of the heightened training in wisdom.

Chinese Source

謂由天住、梵住差別，應知增上心學差別。由諸所有覺分等法聖住差別，應知增上慧學差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Namely, by the distinctions of the heavenly abiding and the Brahmā abiding, one should know the distinctions of the heightened training in mind. By the distinctions of the noble abiding in all the dharmas such as the factors of enlightenment, one should know the distinctions of the heightened training in wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers separately correlating (寅二別配). The dharmas meant here include the seven factors of enlightenment and the eightfold noble path.

296. Section: Explaining in Sequence Begins — The Heavenly Abiding

Namely, the four dhyānas, the four formless attainments, and the rest are called the heavenly abiding.

Chinese Source

謂四靜慮、四無色等，名為天住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Namely, the four dhyānas, the four formless attainments, and the rest are called the heavenly abiding.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens explaining in sequence (寅三隨釋3), beginning with the heavenly abiding (卯一天住). Per the 披尋記, "and the rest" takes in the remaining concentrations (等持) and attainments (等至). Master Cháng Bǎi distinguishes these two: concentration occurs only with mind and spans the concentrated and the distracted states, while attainment spans states with and without mind and occurs only in concentration. Details are in the Stage of Concentration, fascicle 11.

297. Section: The Brahmā Abiding

The concentrations of the four immeasurables are called the Brahmā abiding.

Chinese Source

四無量定，名為梵住。

Ru-Yi Translation

The concentrations of the four immeasurables are called the Brahmā abiding.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers the Brahmā abiding (卯二梵住): the immeasurable mind of loving-kindness, giving happiness; compassion, removing suffering; joy, rejoicing that beings are freed from suffering; and equanimity, without hate or love toward any being.

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the Bodhisattva Ground, fascicle 44, for three levels. Ordinary beings cultivate loving-kindness with sentient beings as object, toward friend, enemy, and stranger. Those of the two vehicles cultivate it with dharmas as object, seeing beings as only the five aggregates. Practitioners of the great vehicle cultivate it without object, for whom neither one who conditions nor being conditioned can be found.

298. Section: The Noble Abiding Begins — Marking and Listing All

The wisdom of the four noble truths, the four foundations of mindfulness, on through the path-factors, the four practice-traces, excellent calm-abiding and insight, the four dharma-traces, and the rest: one should know that all these are called the noble abiding.

Chinese Source

四聖諦智，四種念住，乃至道支，四種行跡，勝奢摩他、毗鉢舍那，四法跡等，當知一切皆名聖住。

Ru-Yi Translation

The wisdom of the four noble truths, the four foundations of mindfulness, on through the path-factors, the four practice-traces, excellent calm-abiding and insight, the four dharma-traces, and the rest: one should know that all these are called the noble abiding.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the noble abiding (卯三聖住2), beginning with marking and listing all (辰一標列一切). Per the 披尋記, "and the rest" takes in the four dwellings and the four reliances. The four dwellings are the dwelling of wisdom, of truth, of relinquishment, and of stillness. The four reliances are to rely on the dharma and not the person, on the meaning and not the words, on sūtras of definitive meaning and not those of provisional meaning, and on wisdom and not consciousness; the 顯揚聖教論 (fascicle 2) explains them.

Master Cháng Bǎi explains the four practice-traces: "painful" means one has not attained a root dhyāna and "pleasant" that one has; "slow" means dull faculties and "quick" sharp ones. Thus the painful practitioner sees the truths relying on the preparatory concentration, and the pleasant one relying on the four dhyānas. "Excellent" calm-abiding and insight are those conjoined with the non-discriminating wisdom of the ultimate truth.

299. Section: Broadly Explaining the Undefiled Dharmas Begins — Marking and Listing the Four Kinds: What They Do

Further, there are four undefiled dharmas, whether in going about or in abiding, which cause the practitioner of contemplation either to withdraw and go about among objects, or to settle the mind in stillness on the meditative object, free of every defilement and abiding in ease.

Chinese Source

又有四種若行、若住無雜染法，令修觀者，或於境界退出遊行，或於所緣安心靜定，離諸雜染安隱而住。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, there are four undefiled dharmas, whether in going about or in abiding, which cause the practitioner of contemplation either to withdraw and go about among objects, or to settle the mind in stillness on the meditative object, free of every defilement and abiding in ease.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens broadly explaining the undefiled dharmas (辰二廣無染法2), an expansion of the four dharma-traces, beginning with marking and listing the four kinds (巳一標列四種). Per the 披尋記, to withdraw and go about among objects means that toward the agreeable and disagreeable marks of the objects one moves among, one gives rise to neither greed nor aversion, and the mind is undisturbed. Master Cháng Bǎi takes "going about" as daily life and "abiding" as the time of formal calm and insight.

300. Section: Marking and Listing the Four Kinds: What They Are, Closing "Marking and Listing the Four Kinds" Entirely

What are the four? First, toward the defiled joys in objects conducive to the feeling of joy, to abandon them from the depth of the heart. Second, toward the defiled sorrows in objects conducive to the feeling of sorrow, to abandon them from the depth of the heart. Third, to purify the mind of the secondary afflictions of the insight class. Fourth, to purify the mind of the secondary afflictions of the calm-abiding class.

Chinese Source

云何為四？一、於隨順喜受境界諸雜染喜，深心棄捨；二、於隨順憂受境界諸染汙憂，深心棄捨；三、於毗鉢舍那品諸隨煩惱淨修其心；四、於奢摩他品諸隨煩惱淨修其心。

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the four? First, toward the defiled joys in objects conducive to the feeling of joy, to abandon them from the depth of the heart. Second, toward the defiled sorrows in objects conducive to the feeling of sorrow, to abandon them from the depth of the heart. Third, to purify the mind of the secondary afflictions of the insight class. Fourth, to purify the mind of the secondary afflictions of the calm-abiding class.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, marking and listing the four kinds (巳一標列四種) closes entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the first two belong to daily life: the first is the dharma-trace of non-greed, abandoning the defilement of greed in pleasing objects, and the second the dharma-trace of non-aversion, abandoning that of aversion in displeasing ones. The latter two belong to formal practice: when secondary afflictions arise, one returns to the meditative object and does not let them continue, the dharma-trace of right mindfulness; and one removes torpor and sleep through such means as the six recollections or the perception of light, the dharma-trace of right concentration.

301. Section: Concluding Their Basis, Closing "Broadly Explaining the Undefiled Dharmas," "The Noble Abiding," "The Distinctions of the Trainings," and the Entire Embrace of the Trainings Entirely

As for these four dharmas of abiding in ease, free of every defilement, whether in going about or in abiding: one should know them as the dharma-traces relied on as the four footholds. Respectively, one should know that these are non-greed, non-aversion, right mindfulness, and right concentration.

Chinese Source

於是四種若行、若住離諸雜染安隱住法，應知四種安足處所所依法跡。如其所應，當知即是無貪、無瞋、正念、正定。

Ru-Yi Translation

As for these four dharmas of abiding in ease, free of every defilement, whether in going about or in abiding: one should know them as the dharma-traces relied on as the four footholds. Respectively, one should know that these are non-greed, non-aversion, right mindfulness, and right concentration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding their basis (巳二結其所依) closes entirely, closing broadly explaining the undefiled dharmas (辰二廣無染法2) entirely, closing the noble abiding (卯三聖住2) entirely, closing explaining in sequence (寅三隨釋3: 天住/梵住/聖住) entirely, closing the distinctions of the trainings in mind and wisdom (丑二心學慧學差別3) entirely, closing the distinctions of the trainings (子五學差別2) entirely, closing the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋5) entirely, closing the embrace of the trainings (壬八學攝2: 頌標列/長行釋) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that a foothold (安足) is where the foot is placed, and that a trace (跡) is a way or a course.

Companion sūtras follow. A farmer plows, waters, and sows in due season, and the crop ripens in time, and a hen tends her eggs without breaking them open, and the chicks hatch of themselves; so too one who trains day after day need not wish for liberation at once. A monastic who does not train is like a donkey following a herd of cattle, unlike them in form and voice. Vṛjiputra (跋耆子) says that he cannot keep the two hundred fifty precepts; told to train in the three trainings instead, he practices alone and becomes an arhat.

A monastic of the Kāśyapa clan, displeased when the Buddha praises the precepts, later regrets it and confesses. The Buddha accepts this, adding that he does not praise an elder monastic who does not value the training, since others would then draw near such a person; Master Cháng Bǎi explains that seniority here is counted by rains retreats. Further sūtras define the three trainings and vary the same pattern with the immeasurables, the formless attainments, the foundations of mindfulness, and on through the path, the four ways, the four dharma statements, and calm and insight. By this treatise the four dharma statements (四法句) are the four dharma-traces, and Master Cháng Bǎi notes that elsewhere they are four ways of speaking well: what is noble, what is pleasing, what is true, and what is the Dharma.

302. Section: The Embrace of the Confirmed Purities Begins — The Verse Marking and Listing: Introducing the Verse

Further, a summary verse says:

Chinese Source

復次，嗢柁南曰：

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, a summary verse says:

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the embrace of the confirmed purities (壬九證淨攝2), the ninth and last of the embraces, beginning with the verse marking and listing (癸一頌標列). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that purity comes from realization: having realized the noble path, one accomplishes in the mind the pure merits of the Buddha, the Dharma, the Saṅgha, and the precepts, and this is called confirmed purity.

303. Section: The Verse of the Five Matters

The establishing of the confirmed purities first; / having change comes next; / the path to the heavens, the simile of the bright mirror; / and declaration stays last of all.

Chinese Source

證淨初安立　有變異為先　天路喻明鏡　記別最居後

Ru-Yi Translation

The establishing of the confirmed purities first; / having change comes next; / the path to the heavens, the simile of the bright mirror; / and declaration stays last of all.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi draws five matters from the verse. First is establishing the content of the four confirmed purities in words, with faith and precepts as their nature. Second is what changes and what does not for the noble disciples who have accomplished them. Third is the path to the heavens, the heavens here being those of the ultimate meaning, the five pure abodes of the noble ones, and not the heavens of ordinary beings. Fourth is the simile of the bright mirror, in which one checks one's faith in the Three Jewels and one's precepts. Fifth is declaring the different marks of attaining the fruits, which also unfolds the six recollections.

304. Section: The Long-Form Explanation Begins — Establishing Begins — Marking and Listing the Two Dharmas: The Establishing

Because a disciple of the Tathāgata endowed with right view, in brief by two dharmas, is able rightly to take up the quality of clarity, one should know that four kinds of confirmed purity are established.

Chinese Source

具足正見如來弟子，略由二法，能正攝受澄清性故，應知建立四種證淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because a disciple of the Tathāgata endowed with right view, in brief by two dharmas, is able rightly to take up the quality of clarity, one should know that four kinds of confirmed purity are established.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋5), beginning with establishing (子一安立4) and marking and listing the two dharmas (丑一標列二法). Per the 披尋記, the four are the confirmed purity in the Buddha, in the Dharma, in the Saṅgha, and in the precepts, as the 顯揚聖教論 (fascicle 3) explains. Master Cháng Bǎi adds that the first three belong to faith and the fourth to the precepts, and that clarity (澄清性) describes faith and pure precepts free of turbidity and deceit.

He also cites the Great Vibhāṣā (fascicle 103) for six reasons for their order, among them these: the Buddha is like a good physician, the Dharma like freedom from illness, the Saṅgha like one who cares for the sick, and the precepts like wonderful medicine; or the Buddha is a caravan leader showing the way, the Dharma an island of treasure, the Saṅgha fellow travelers, and the precepts provisions.

305. Section: Marking and Listing the Two Dharmas: What the Two Are, Closing "Marking and Listing the Two Dharmas" Entirely

Namely, faith and precepts embraced within the renunciant's meaning.

Chinese Source

謂沙門義所攝信、戒。

Ru-Yi Translation

Namely, faith and precepts embraced within the renunciant's meaning.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, marking and listing the two dharmas (丑一標列二法) closes entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi points back to the statement earlier in this fascicle that, for renunciants in the ultimate sense, the eight-limbed noble path is established as the renunciant's meaning. Faith and precepts are embraced within that path.

306. Section: Separately Explaining the Marks Begins — The Clarity of Faith

Pure faith toward the one who teaches, toward the renunciant's meaning, toward those of the same dharma, and toward the companions able to realize the renunciant's state is deeply rooted and firm, and even in other lives it cannot be drawn away. Because it is without deceit, it is called the quality of clarity.

Chinese Source

於能說者、於沙門義、於同法者、於能證得沙門助伴所有淨信，深固根本，於餘生中亦不可引。無虛誑故，名澄清性。

Ru-Yi Translation

Pure faith toward the one who teaches, toward the renunciant's meaning, toward those of the same dharma, and toward the companions able to realize the renunciant's state is deeply rooted and firm, and even in other lives it cannot be drawn away. Because it is without deceit, it is called the quality of clarity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately explaining the marks (丑二別釋其相2), beginning with the clarity of faith (寅一信澄清性). Per the 披尋記, the sense here is the unshakable pure mind, born of true awakening, toward the Buddha, Dharma, and Saṅgha. The one who teaches is the Buddha. The renunciant's meaning is the Dharma, since the eight-limbed noble path was established as the renunciant's meaning. Those of the same dharma and the companions able to realize the renunciant's state are the Saṅgha. Pure faith toward these spans the worldly and the transcendent.
