Master Cháng Bǎi adds that such faith is rooted like a great tree: even one still in saṃsāra who is born into an outsider family will return to the Dharma.

307. Section: The Clarity of the Precepts, Closing "Separately Explaining the Marks" Entirely

And pure discipline, toward all evil, unwholesome dharmas able to lead to the evil destinies, obtains the restraint of ultimately not doing them; therefore it too gains the name quality of clarity.

Chinese Source

及淨尸羅，於其一切能往惡趣惡不善法，獲得畢竟不作律儀，是故亦得名澄清性。

Ru-Yi Translation

And pure discipline, toward all evil, unwholesome dharmas able to lead to the evil destinies, obtains the restraint of ultimately not doing them; therefore it too gains the name quality of clarity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the clarity of the precepts (寅二戒澄清性) closes entirely, closing separately explaining the marks (丑二別釋其相2: 信澄清性/戒澄清性) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi notes that "pure discipline" does not mean that the precepts are realized, for precepts are upheld and not realized. Faith purifies the mind and the precepts purify body and speech, so that the two together purify all three doors of action.

308. Section: Clarifying the Meaning of the Establishing Begins — The First Meaning Begins — Showing the Most Supreme Begins — The Clarity of Faith

One should know that, here, relying on pure faith, one gives rise to deep faith and understanding within the well-taught dharma and discipline. Because of the clarity of this pure faith, even in another life one ultimately does not turn away from the Buddha's well-taught dharma and discipline.

Chinese Source

應知此中，依止淨信，於善說法毗奈耶中深生信解。由此淨信澄清性故，設在餘生，於佛善說法毗奈耶畢竟無轉。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, relying on pure faith, one gives rise to deep faith and understanding within the well-taught dharma and discipline. Because of the clarity of this pure faith, even in another life one ultimately does not turn away from the Buddha's well-taught dharma and discipline.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens clarifying the meaning of the establishing (丑三明建立義2), beginning with the first meaning (寅一第一義2), showing the most supreme (卯一顯最勝2), and the clarity of faith (辰一信澄清性). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the understanding does not waver in later lives, and this is why the clarity of faith is established.

309. Section: The Clarity of the Precepts, Closing "Showing the Most Supreme" Entirely

Further, out of fear of the suffering of the evil destinies, one receives and upholds pure precepts, countering misconduct. Because of taking up this clarity of the precepts, even in another life one does not commit evil and fall into the evil destinies, ultimately without regress, up to nirvāṇa.

Chinese Source

又由怖畏諸惡道苦，受持淨戒，對治惡行。由此攝受戒澄清性，設在餘生，亦不造惡墮諸惡趣，畢竟無退，乃至涅槃。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, out of fear of the suffering of the evil destinies, one receives and upholds pure precepts, countering misconduct. Because of taking up this clarity of the precepts, even in another life one does not commit evil and fall into the evil destinies, ultimately without regress, up to nirvāṇa.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the clarity of the precepts (辰二戒澄清性) closes entirely, closing showing the most supreme (卯一顯最勝2: 信澄清性/戒澄清性) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi concludes that one who at the start establishes pure faith in the true dharma and discipline and trains in pure precepts will not turn to outsiders, will not create the karma of the three evil destinies, and can go on to nirvāṇa; hence these two are supreme.

310. Section: Sifting What Is Said, Closing "The First Meaning" Entirely

Because they are the basis for ultimately not turning away from the well-taught dharma and discipline, and because they are the basis for ultimately not going to any evil destiny, their function is most supreme, and so only faith and precepts are said to be the quality of clarity. It is not that the remaining dharmas such as diligence, mindfulness, and concentration are not the quality of clarity.

Chinese Source

由於善說法毗奈耶畢竟無轉所依處故，畢竟不往一切惡趣所依處故，其用最勝，唯說信、戒為澄清性，非餘精進、念、定等法非澄清性。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are the basis for ultimately not turning away from the well-taught dharma and discipline, and because they are the basis for ultimately not going to any evil destiny, their function is most supreme, and so only faith and precepts are said to be the quality of clarity. It is not that the remaining dharmas such as diligence, mindfulness, and concentration are not the quality of clarity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, sifting what is said (卯二簡所說) closes entirely, closing the first meaning (寅一第一義2: 顯最勝/簡所說) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the others are not excluded; faith and precepts are singled out because their effect on the mind and on conduct is strongest. Faith counters lack of faith, and precepts counter the fault of breaking precepts.

311. Section: The Second Meaning, Closing "Clarifying the Meaning of the Establishing" Entirely

Further, this faith and these precepts are the basis of the heightened training in precepts, concentration, and wisdom. By saying that faith and precepts are pure, the meaning shows that all three trainings attain purity. For this reason, only these two are said to be confirmed purity. This is called the distinction of the second gate of meaning.

Chinese Source

又此信、戒，是其增上戒定慧學所依止處，由說信、戒是清淨故，義顯三學皆得清淨。由是因緣，唯說此二以為證淨。是名第二義門差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, this faith and these precepts are the basis of the heightened training in precepts, concentration, and wisdom. By saying that faith and precepts are pure, the meaning shows that all three trainings attain purity. For this reason, only these two are said to be confirmed purity. This is called the distinction of the second gate of meaning.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, the second meaning (寅二第二義) closes entirely, closing clarifying the meaning of the establishing (丑三明建立義2: 第一義/第二義) entirely. Faith and precepts are the ground on which the three trainings stand, and through them all three reach purity.

312. Section: Broadly Explaining the Alternate Names Begins — Named Nourishing Merit

Because these confirmed purities are well able to nourish all the white, pure dharmas that fall within the realms, they are called nourishing merit.

Chinese Source

如是證淨，善能滋潤一切墮界白淨法故，名滋潤福。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because these confirmed purities are well able to nourish all the white, pure dharmas that fall within the realms, they are called nourishing merit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens broadly explaining the alternate names (丑四廣釋異名3), beginning with named nourishing merit (寅一名滋潤福). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the white dharmas within the three realms include the five precepts, the ten good deeds, and the cultivation of concentration, and that from the four confirmed purities they yield measureless merit.

313. Section: Named Nourishing the Good

Because they are able to bring forth the excellent noble paths, they are called nourishing the good.

Chinese Source

能引殊勝諸聖道故，名滋潤善。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are able to bring forth the excellent noble paths, they are called nourishing the good.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers named nourishing the good (寅二名滋潤善). The four confirmed purities lead toward and bring forth the noble path of the ultimate truth.

314. Section: Named Able to Bring Forth Happiness, Closing "Broadly Explaining the Alternate Names" and "Establishing" Entirely

Because they are able to bring forth the severance of the remaining afflictions, they are called able to bring forth happiness.

Chinese Source

能引所餘煩惱斷故，名能引樂。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are able to bring forth the severance of the remaining afflictions, they are called able to bring forth happiness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, named able to bring forth happiness (寅三名能引樂) closes entirely, closing broadly explaining the alternate names (丑四廣釋異名3: 名滋潤福/名滋潤善/名能引樂) entirely, closing establishing (子一安立4) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the more the bonds of defilement are cut, the calmer and more joyful the mind becomes.

Companion sūtras follow. One warns of five faults in faith placed in a person and not in the Three Jewels and the precepts: if that person is cast out or censured by the assembly, goes elsewhere, returns to lay life, or dies, the one who revered them stops coming to the monastery, no longer hears the Dharma, and loses wholesome dharmas. Another calls the four confirmed purities a food of happiness, nourishing merit as the four foods nourish beings. Another says that they free a householder from the stain of stinginess and lead to delight in giving. Another says that their merit cannot be measured, as the water of the great rivers cannot be measured by the pot. The sūtra closes with the three names given here.

315. Section: Having Change Comes Next Begins — Bringing Forth What Changes Begins — Marking

Further, noble disciples who have accomplished the confirmed purities and are wholly certain to go to the good destinies, while abiding in the good destinies, still have three fears of heavy suffering arising from the mutual conflict and change of the great elements; yet they have no fear of the evil destinies.

Chinese Source

復次，一向決定能往善趣，成就證淨諸聖弟子，猶有住於善趣三種諸大互違變異所起重苦怖畏，然無惡趣所有怖畏。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, noble disciples who have accomplished the confirmed purities and are wholly certain to go to the good destinies, while abiding in the good destinies, still have three fears of heavy suffering arising from the mutual conflict and change of the great elements; yet they have no fear of the evil destinies.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens having change comes next (子二有變異為先2), beginning with bringing forth what changes (丑一出有變異3) and marking (寅一標). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that these noble disciples have attained the first fruit, and will take birth only as humans and gods.

316. Section: Questioning

What are the three fears of heavy suffering?

Chinese Source

云何三種重苦怖畏？

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the three fears of heavy suffering?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers questioning (寅二徵).

317. Section: Listing, Closing "Bringing Forth What Changes" Entirely

First, the suffering of illness; second, the suffering of old age; third, the suffering of death, in which the vital points (末摩) are severed.

Chinese Source

一者、病苦，二者、老苦，三者、斷截末摩死苦。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, the suffering of illness; second, the suffering of old age; third, the suffering of death, in which the vital points (末摩) are severed.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, listing (寅三列) closes entirely, closing bringing forth what changes (丑一出有變異3: 標/徵/列) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that these three apply to those in the human destiny; in the heavens there is only the suffering of death. Citing fascicle 61, he explains that illness brings bodily change, sorrow, and lack of delight in what is pleasing; old age brings decline in vigor, strength, the faculties, enjoyment, and lifespan; and death brings parting from what is loved, namely wealth, friends, family, and one's own body.

318. Section: Sifting What Does Not Change, Closing "Having Change Comes Next" Entirely

Therefore it is said: the four great elements can be made to change; noble disciples who have accomplished the four confirmed purities cannot be subject to change.

Chinese Source

是故說言：其四大種可令變異，非已成就四種證淨諸聖弟子可有變異。

Ru-Yi Translation

Therefore it is said: the four great elements can be made to change; noble disciples who have accomplished the four confirmed purities cannot be subject to change.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, sifting what does not change (丑二簡非變異) closes entirely, closing having change comes next (子二有變異為先2: 出有變異/簡非變異) entirely. Per the 披尋記, the four great elements can cause noble disciples to change and have suffering; it is not that noble disciples can cause the four great elements to change. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the body of a noble disciple still changes with the elements, and that even an arhat has bodily suffering from past karma, though not suffering of mind. What does not change is that such a disciple will not fall into the three evil destinies.

Companion sūtras follow. Noble disciples with the four confirmed purities, wherever born, receive ten things among gods or humans, including long life, beauty, renown, and happiness, and their true wisdom is not moved by others. They are not poor, for their minds are rich. A wheel-turning king who has not cut off the suffering of the evil destinies falls far short of a monastic in discarded robes who has. One who has compassion should teach these four to others, and the Buddha adds that the four great elements change, but these four purities never do.

319. Section: The Path to the Heavens Begins — Bringing Forth Freedom from Harm

Further, those gods who are pure in the ultimate sense are called supreme and free of harm, because with body, speech, and mind they ultimately have no harmful act. It is by this pure divine nature that the four confirmed purities are said to be the path to the heavens.

Chinese Source

復次，若第一義清淨諸天，說名最勝無有惱害，由身語意畢竟無有惱害事故。即依如是清淨天性，說四證淨名為天路。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, those gods who are pure in the ultimate sense are called supreme and free of harm, because with body, speech, and mind they ultimately have no harmful act. It is by this pure divine nature that the four confirmed purities are said to be the path to the heavens.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens the path to the heavens (子三天路2), beginning with bringing forth freedom from harm (丑一出無惱害). Per the 披尋記, there are two kinds of pure gods, as said in fascicle 87: the not-well-pure, who enter only worldly concentration and have neither penetrated the truths nor well liberated the mind, and the well-pure, who enter the concentration of the inner dharma, have penetrated the truths, and have well liberated the mind. Only the well-pure gods are taken here, and so they are called pure in the ultimate sense.

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that the first kind are those who, by subduing the afflictions of the desire realm with worldly dhyāna, take birth in the form realm without having cut off affliction; the second are the noble ones of the five pure abodes.

320. Section: Clarifying the Cultivation of Recollection Begins — General Marking

Further, taking the four confirmed purities as support, noble disciples, by three gates, cultivate the six recollections.

Chinese Source

又四證淨為所依止，諸聖弟子，依三種門修六隨念。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, taking the four confirmed purities as support, noble disciples, by three gates, cultivate the six recollections.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens clarifying the cultivation of recollection (丑二明修隨念2), beginning with general marking (寅一總標). The six recollections, as Master Cháng Bǎi lists them, are of the Buddha, Dharma, Saṅgha, generosity, precepts, and gods.

321. Section: Separately Distinguishing Begins — Distinguishing the Purpose

First, to sever the defiled torment arising from the secondary afflictions of the calm-abiding class. Second, to sever the defiled torment arising from the secondary afflictions of the insight class. Third, to sever the two classes of secondary affliction which, although there is no defiled torment now, may yet arise in the future.

Chinese Source

一者、為斷奢摩他品諸隨煩惱所起染惱；二者、為斷毗鉢舍那品諸隨煩惱所起染惱；三者、為斷雖無染惱，而於未來當可生起二隨煩惱。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, to sever the defiled torment arising from the secondary afflictions of the calm-abiding class. Second, to sever the defiled torment arising from the secondary afflictions of the insight class. Third, to sever the two classes of secondary affliction which, although there is no defiled torment now, may yet arise in the future.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens separately distinguishing (寅二別辨3), beginning with distinguishing the purpose (卯一辨所為). Master Cháng Bǎi explains the third gate: while the seeds of defilement have not been cut off, the secondary afflictions of both classes may arise when conditions meet, even though none is active now.

322. Section: Distinguishing the Secondary Afflictions

One should know that, here, torpor and sleep are called the secondary afflictions of the calm-abiding class. Restlessness conjoined with delight in desires, and the unwholesome discursive thoughts of desire and so on born from the faults of greed and the rest, the defiled dharmas that make the mind flow and scatter, are called the secondary afflictions of the insight class.

Chinese Source

當知此中，惛沈睡眠，名奢摩他品諸隨煩惱。欣樂諸欲俱行掉舉、貪等過失所生不善欲尋伺等，令心流散諸雜染法，名毗鉢舍那品諸隨煩惱。

Ru-Yi Translation

One should know that, here, torpor and sleep are called the secondary afflictions of the calm-abiding class. Restlessness conjoined with delight in desires, and the unwholesome discursive thoughts of desire and so on born from the faults of greed and the rest, the defiled dharmas that make the mind flow and scatter, are called the secondary afflictions of the insight class.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers distinguishing the secondary afflictions (卯二辨隨惑). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that torpor arises when, dwelling long on one object, the mind loses interest or lacks strength and the object is no longer clear; sleep is a heavier torpor. Neither has the capacity for calm. One then rouses the mind by recollecting the merits of the Buddha, Dharma, and Saṅgha and one's own precepts and giving, and gladness dispels them.

Restlessness is the agitation of a mind recalling past pleasures, from which arise greed and aversion and thoughts of desire, ill will, and harm, and of homeland, kin, and family. When it is present, he says, one should return to the object of calm and bind the mind to it by the nine stages of settling, until it reaches single-pointedness.

323. Section: Distinguishing the Differences, Closing "Clarifying the Cultivation of Recollection" and "The Path to the Heavens" Entirely

Further, the recollection attained by the principle of the ultimate truth is called the valor of meaning. The recollection attained by the principle of conventional truth is called the valor of dharma.

Chinese Source

又由勝義諦理所得隨念，名義威勇。由世俗諦理所得隨念，名法威勇。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, the recollection attained by the principle of the ultimate truth is called the valor of meaning. The recollection attained by the principle of conventional truth is called the valor of dharma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, distinguishing the differences (卯三辨差別) closes entirely, closing separately distinguishing (寅二別辨3: 辨所為/辨隨惑/辨差別) entirely, closing clarifying the cultivation of recollection (丑二明修隨念2) entirely, closing the path to the heavens (子三天路2: 出無惱害/明修隨念) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains by example: recollection in accord with the principle of the emptiness of self and dharmas is the valor of meaning; recollection still dependent on the words for that emptiness is the valor of dharma.

Companion sūtras follow. The Buddha contrasts the false path of outsiders, who rub the body with powders, lie on ground spread with cow dung, or run naked eastward heedless of wild animals and thorns in hope of Brahmā's heaven, with the eightfold path that wisely leads to nirvāṇa. Śāriputra answers on the stream, which is the eight noble path-factors, and the factors of stream-entry: drawing near good friends, hearing the true Dharma, right reflection, and practice in accord with the Dharma. Ānanda tells Śāriputra that one who has given up lack of faith and bad precepts, and accomplished the four confirmed purities, is declared a stream-enterer.

Others teach that one who stills the five fears and animosities of killing and the rest, is free of doubt about the Three Jewels, and truly knows the noble path may declare of oneself the end of the evil destinies; the noble path may be named as the four truths, the eightfold path, or the twelve links. Four dharmas of ascent to the gods are recollecting the Buddha, the Dharma, the Saṅgha, and one's precepts. Each brings gladness, ease, and happiness, on through concentration, and with it the resolve to have no aversion toward anything in the world.

324. Section: The Simile of the Bright Mirror

Further, just as a person holds up a bright mirror in order to observe the pure and impure marks of their own face, so the noble disciples of the Tathāgata hold up the subtle mirror of the confirmed purities in order truly to observe the marks of defilement and purity in their own person.

Chinese Source

復次，譬如有人執持明鏡，為觀自面淨不淨相；如是如來諸聖弟子，執持微妙證淨明鏡，為如實觀自身所有染淨諸相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, just as a person holds up a bright mirror in order to observe the pure and impure marks of their own face, so the noble disciples of the Tathāgata hold up the subtle mirror of the confirmed purities in order truly to observe the marks of defilement and purity in their own person.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This covers the simile of the bright mirror (子四喻明鏡). Master Cháng Bǎi says that the Dharma is like a mirror in which the many marks of beings are clear at a glance.

In the companion sūtras, when monastics ask where those who have died were reborn, the Buddha answers each time and then teaches the Dharma Mirror Sūtra (法鏡經), in which the noble disciple accomplishes the four confirmed purities. Asked about many who died at the village of Nādikā, he says that those who cut off the five lower fetters attained nirvāṇa in that heaven, adds that he does not delight in such questions, since whatever is born must die, and then teaches dependent origination and the same mirror.

325. Section: Declaration Begins — Distinctions of Realizing the Fruits Begins — Distinguishing the First Three Fruits Begins — Stream-Entry

Further, if one who has accomplished the four confirmed purities makes a declaration to others based only on those four confirmed purities of their own, and not on the recollections cultivated at a higher stage that are conducive to joy, then by this cause and condition one should know this to be a declaration of the realization of the stream-entry fruit, because such a person has not yet advanced to the path cultivated at a higher stage.

Chinese Source

復次，若有成就四種證淨，唯即依自四種證淨為他記別，不依上位能順歡喜所修隨念。由此因緣，當知記別預流果證，未趣上位所修道故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, if one who has accomplished the four confirmed purities makes a declaration to others based only on those four confirmed purities of their own, and not on the recollections cultivated at a higher stage that are conducive to joy, then by this cause and condition one should know this to be a declaration of the realization of the stream-entry fruit, because such a person has not yet advanced to the path cultivated at a higher stage.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens declaration (子五記別2), beginning with distinctions of realizing the fruits (丑一果證差別2), distinguishing the first three fruits (寅一辨前三果3), and stream-entry (卯一預流). Master Cháng Bǎi explains that one at the first fruit can declare only on the basis of the four confirmed purities and does not know the realm of the higher stages.

In the companion sūtras, the Buddha tells Nandiya (難提) that one who lacks the five faculties of faith and the rest is heedless and an ordinary being. A noble disciple who rests content with the four confirmed purities, and does not go on to strengthen concentration and wisdom, is also heedless.

326. Section: Once-Returning Begins — Marking the Cause and Condition

If one makes a declaration to others based on the five recollections of a higher stage that are conducive to joy, then by this cause and condition one should know this to be a declaration of the realization of the once-returning fruit.

Chinese Source

若於上位能順歡喜五種隨念，為他記別。由是因緣，當知記別一來果證。

Ru-Yi Translation

If one makes a declaration to others based on the five recollections of a higher stage that are conducive to joy, then by this cause and condition one should know this to be a declaration of the realization of the once-returning fruit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This opens once-returning (卯二一來2), beginning with marking the cause and condition (辰一標因緣). Per the 披尋記, the Stage of Cultivation (fascicle 20) says that one who has entered direct realization of the noble truths, wishing to attain swift penetrating wisdom, attends to matters of joy. Having seen the truths and attained confirmed purity, and relying on it, the noble disciple attends to the fields of merit of the Buddha, Dharma, and Saṅgha, and to their own higher rebirth and final good, namely the flourishing of body, wealth, and realization, and joy arises. These are the five recollections: of the Buddha, Dharma, Saṅgha, precepts, and relinquishment.

Master Cháng Bǎi adds that the recollection of the gods is lacking at this stage because such a noble one has not yet attained the first dhyāna.

327. Section: Explaining the Reason, Closing "Once-Returning" Entirely

This is because samādhi has not yet been brought to completion, because the path of freedom from desire has not yet been perfected, and because those gods have not yet been directly seen. In order to seek freedom from desire, one cultivates the dharmas conducive to joy. With this joy as support, tranquility arises; through tranquility, one experiences bodily happiness; through experiencing happiness, the mind attains right concentration, yet still concentration has not yet been brought to completion.

Chinese Source

由三摩地未成滿故，於離欲道未圓滿故，於彼諸天未現見故。為求離欲，修習能順歡喜諸法；由此歡喜為所依故，發生輕安；由輕安故，領受身樂；由受樂故，心得正定，而於靜定未得成滿。

Ru-Yi Translation

This is because samādhi has not yet been brought to completion, because the path of freedom from desire has not yet been perfected, and because those gods have not yet been directly seen. In order to seek freedom from desire, one cultivates the dharmas conducive to joy. With this joy as support, tranquility arises; through tranquility, one experiences bodily happiness; through experiencing happiness, the mind attains right concentration, yet still concentration has not yet been brought to completion.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, explaining the reason (辰二釋所由) closes entirely, closing once-returning (卯二一來2: 標因緣/釋所由) entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi cites the 法蘊足論 (fascicle 5) on right concentration as the mind's settled single-pointedness conjoined with uncontaminated attention to the truths, and cites the 瑜伽論記 for the point that "those gods" are the uncontaminated gods of the ultimate meaning in the form realm.

In the companion sūtra, the Śākyan Nandiya is sorry that the Buddha is going away, and the Buddha teaches him five places of joy to recollect at all times, in walking, standing, sitting, or lying down, and even among his family: the Buddha, the Dharma, the Saṅgha, his own precepts, and his own giving.

328. Section: Non-Returning, Closing "Distinguishing the First Three Fruits" Entirely

If one makes a declaration to others based on the six recollections of a higher stage, then by this cause and condition one should know this to be a declaration of the realization of the non-returning fruit.

Chinese Source

若於上位六種隨念，為他記別。由是因緣，當知記別不還果證。

Ru-Yi Translation

If one makes a declaration to others based on the six recollections of a higher stage, then by this cause and condition one should know this to be a declaration of the realization of the non-returning fruit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, non-returning (卯三不還) closes entirely, closing distinguishing the first three fruits (寅一辨前三果3: 預流/一來/不還) entirely. Per the 披尋記, the six recollections are the former five with the recollection of the gods, added because at this higher stage one can directly see the assemblies of gods. The 顯揚聖教論 (fascicle 20) says that declaration by recollection belongs to the non-returner, who has attained a root concentration, directly sees the assemblies of gods, converses with those of Brahmā's assembly and others, and recollects that the merit of the vehicle being sought is not yet complete.

329. Section: Sifting the Highest Fruit, Closing "Distinctions of Realizing the Fruits" Entirely

The fruit of arhatship can be approached and realized only by the transcendent path, and all recollections are only worldly. Therefore, above the realization of the non-returning fruit, there is no further declaration by such recollection.

Chinese Source

阿羅漢果唯出世道乃能趣證，所有隨念唯是世間，是故不還果證已上，更無如是隨念記別。

Ru-Yi Translation

The fruit of arhatship can be approached and realized only by the transcendent path, and all recollections are only worldly. Therefore, above the realization of the non-returning fruit, there is no further declaration by such recollection.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, sifting the highest fruit (寅二簡最上果) closes entirely, closing distinctions of realizing the fruits (丑一果證差別2: 辨前三果/簡最上果) entirely.

330. Section: Distinctions of the Confirmed Purities, Closing "Declaration," the Entire Embrace of the Confirmed Purities, and the Fourth Great Division, the Embrace of the Factors of Enlightenment, Entirely

Further, in the stream-entry fruit, the four confirmed purities are said only to be pure; in the remaining fruits of training, they are said to be perfect purity; and in the highest fruit, they are said to be the foremost perfect purity.

Chinese Source

又四證淨，預流果中唯說為淨，於餘學果說圓滿淨，於最上果說為第一圓滿清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, in the stream-entry fruit, the four confirmed purities are said only to be pure; in the remaining fruits of training, they are said to be perfect purity; and in the highest fruit, they are said to be the foremost perfect purity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, distinctions of the confirmed purities (丑二證淨差別) closes entirely, closing declaration (子五記別2: 果證差別/證淨差別) entirely, closing the long-form explanation (癸二長行釋5: 安立/有變異為先/天路/喻明鏡/記別) entirely, closing the embrace of the confirmed purities (壬九證淨攝2: 頌標列/長行釋) entirely. It is the last of the nine embraces of the factors of enlightenment (菩提分法擇攝). The purity is the same four throughout; only its name deepens from the first fruit, through the second and third, to arhatship.

331. Section: Concluding and Pointing to the Rest, Closing Sūtra Matters Entirely

Thus, in brief, the essentials of the sūtras conjoined with object and wisdom, in accord with this treatise, have been drawn out as a mātṛkā. All the rest, following this one corner, should be awakened to and understood.

Chinese Source

如是略引隨順此論境智相應諸經宗要摩怛理迦，其餘一切隨此方隅，皆當覺了。

Ru-Yi Translation

Thus, in brief, the essentials of the sūtras conjoined with object and wisdom, in accord with this treatise, have been drawn out as a mātṛkā. All the rest, following this one corner, should be awakened to and understood.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With this, concluding and pointing to the rest (戊四結指餘) closes entirely. Master Cháng Bǎi explains that fascicles 85 through 98 have drawn out the essentials of the sūtras in accord with this treatise's wisdom of the śrāvaka's objects, chiefly from the Saṃyukta Āgama among the four Āgamas. From this small portion, the essentials of all other sūtras and treatises can be worked out by reasoning and understood.

Companion sūtras follow. After the rains retreat, the Śākyan Nandiya is sorry to see the Buddha leave, and the Buddha teaches him the six recollections. The same is told of the brothers Ṛṣidatta (梨師達多) and Pūraṇa (富蘭那). Ṛṣidatta, a minister of King Prasenajit, says that even when riding on the king's business among the palace women he guards three things: the elephant's course, his own mind, and himself from falling. He shares his wealth with monastics and lay followers alike, not holding it as his own, and the Buddha praises him.

With this, fascicle 98 comes to its close, and with it sūtra matters (素怛纜事), the first of the three great topics of the embrace of the bases, which has occupied fascicles 85 through 98. The next fascicle turns to matters of monastic discipline (調伏事).
